Non-perturbative localization on the strip and Avila’s almost reducibility conjecture Thanks: R. Han is partially supported by NSF DMS-2143369. W. Schlag is partially supported by NSF grant DMS-1902691.
Rui Han, Wilhelm Schlag

Abstract
. We prove non-perturbative Anderson localization and almost localization for a family of quasi-periodic operators on the strip. As an application we establish Avila’s almost reducibility conjecture for Schrödinger operators with trigonometric potentials and all Diophantine frequencies, whose proof for analytic potentials was announced in 2015 in [ Av3 ] . As part of our analysis, we derive a non-selfadjoint version of Haro and Puig’s formula connecting Lyapunov exponents of the dual model to those of the original operator.



1.
Introduction

In the first part of this paper, we prove non-perturbative Anderson localization for a family of one-dimensional finite-range quasi-periodic Schrödinger operators, which can also be viewed as an operator on the strip
\mathbb{Z}\times\{1,...,d\}
or block-valued quasi-periodic Jacobi matrices.
Here non-perturbative refers to the positive Lyapunov exponent (half of exponents of the corresponding symplectic cocycles are positive) regime.
We also prove almost localization all
\theta
and any Diophantine
\alpha
(those satisfy
\beta(\alpha)=0
, see (
2.21
)).
This together with the dual (almost) reducibility techniques developed by Avila-Jitomirskaya in
[
AJ2
]
, proves Avila’s almost reducibility conjecture (ARC) in
[
Av3
]
for Schrödinger operators with trigonometric potentials and all Diophantine
\alpha
’s.
Combined with Avila’s proof of ARC for Liouville
\alpha
(those satisfying
\beta(\alpha)>0
), this gives a complete proof of ARC for trigonometric potentials.

Let

(1.1)

\displaystyle(H_{\alpha,\theta,\varepsilon}u)_{n}=u_{n+1}+u_{n-1}+v(\theta+n\alpha+i\varepsilon)u_{n},

v:\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
analytic, be a quasi-periodic Schrödinger operator on
\mathbb{Z}
.
Let

(1.2)

\displaystyle A_{E,\varepsilon}(\theta)=\left(\begin{matrix}E-v(\theta+i\varepsilon)&-1\\
1&0\end{matrix}\right)

be the transfer matrix, and
(\alpha,A_{E,\varepsilon})
be the associated cocycle. Let
L(\alpha,A_{E,\varepsilon})
be the Lyapunov exponent.
In his seminal work
[
Av3
]
, Avila introduced the
acceleration
, defined as:

(1.3)

\displaystyle\kappa(\alpha,A_{E,\varepsilon})=\lim_{\delta\to 0^{+}}\frac{L(\alpha,A_{E,\varepsilon+\delta})-L(\alpha,A_{E,\varepsilon})}{2\pi\delta}.

Furthermore, he proved the acceleration is quantized, meaning
\kappa(\alpha,A_{E,\varepsilon})\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Using the quantized acceleration, Avila divides the spectrum
\sigma(H_{\alpha,\theta,0})=\sigma(H_{\alpha})
(we restrict to the irrational
\alpha
case, for which the spectrum is independent of
\theta
) into three regimes:
the supercritical regime

\Sigma_{\alpha,+}:=\{E\in\sigma(H_{\alpha}):\,L(\alpha,A_{E,0})>0,\text{ and }\kappa(\alpha,A_{E,0})>0\},

the subcritical regime

\Sigma_{\alpha,0}:=\{E\in\sigma(H_{\alpha}):\,L(\alpha,A_{E,0})=0,\text{ and }\kappa(\alpha,A_{E,0})=0\},

and the critical regime

\Sigma_{\alpha,c}:=\{E\in\sigma(H_{\alpha}):\,L(\alpha,A_{E,0})=0,\text{ and }\kappa(\alpha,A_{E,0})>0\}.

In the supercritical regime, non-perturbative proofs of Anderson localization have been developed for the Harper operator with all
\alpha\in\mathrm{DC}
1
1

1

\mathrm{DC}=\bigcup_{c>0}\bigcup_{b>1}\{\alpha\in\mathbb{T}:\,\|n\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geq c\,|n|^{-b},n\neq 0\}\subsetneq\{\alpha\in\mathbb{T}:\,\beta(\alpha)=0\}
in
[
J1
]
(where
v(\theta)=2\lambda\cos(2\pi\theta)
with
|\lambda|>1
), for general analytic potentials with a.e.
\alpha
in
[
BG
]
, and for all
\alpha\in\mathrm{DC}
in the first supercritical stratum (where
\kappa(\alpha,A_{E,0})=1
) in
[
HS
]
.
In the critical regime, the spectrum is conjectured to be singular continuous (see Conjecture 3.1 in
[
AJM
]
). This conjecture has been proved for the critical Harper operator (where
v(\theta)=2\cos(2\pi\theta)
) in
[
Av1
,
AJM
,
J2
]
.

In the subcritical regime, Avila formulated the almost reducibility conjecture (ARC), which we shall elaborate on below.
A cocycle
(\alpha,A)
is called (analytically)
reducible
if there exists
D\in C^{\omega}(\mathbb{T},\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R}))
such that the analytic conjugacy
D^{-1}(\theta+\alpha)A(\theta)D(\theta)
is a constant.
A cocycle is called
almost reducible
if the closure of its analytic conjugacies contains a constant.

Conjecture 1.1

(ARC by Avila,
[
Av3
]
)
.

Subcritical cocycles are almost reducible.

As remarked by Avila in
[
Av3
]
, “
Proving this almost reducibility conjecture would at once provide an almost complete understanding of subcriticality.
”
This conjecture has thus far been proved in two different cases:

•

for Diophantine
\alpha
’s: (i) for general analytic potentials in the small coupling regime (ii) for the entire subcritical regime of the almost Mathieu (or Harper) operator; both in
[
AJ2
]
. See also
[
GYZ
]
.

•

for Liouville
\alpha
’s (those
\beta(\alpha)>0
) in
[
Av2
]
.

In this paper, we prove the following non-perturbative quantitative ARC for Schrödinger cocycles with trigonometric potentials and all Diophantine
\alpha
. In the rest of the paper, we assume that
v(x)=\sum_{1\leq|k|\leq d}\hat{v}_{k}e^{2\pi ikx}
is a real-valued trigonometric polynomial, which implies for
1\leq k\leq d
,
\hat{v}_{k}=\overline{\hat{v}_{-k}}
. Without loss of generality we assume that
\hat{v}_{0}=0
.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
\beta(\alpha)=0
, and
E\in\Sigma_{\alpha,0}
.
Let
0<\varepsilon_{0}<\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})}{30\hat{L}^{d}(\hat{A}_{E})+100C_{3}d}
where
C_{3}
is the absolute constant in Lemma
3.10
, and
\delta=\min(\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})}{5000},\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{25})
. Let
\theta=\theta(E)
be given by Theorem
A.1
. Let
\{n_{j}\}
be the sequence of
\varepsilon_{0}
-resonances of
\theta
and
L_{j}:=\|2\theta-n_{j}\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}
. Then there exists an absolute constant
C>0
such that for
j>j_{0}(\alpha,v,E,\varepsilon_{0})
there exists
W\in C^{\omega}_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}(\mathbb{T},\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R}))
such that
|\mathrm{deg}(W)|\leq C|n_{j}|
,
\|W\|_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}\leq L_{j}^{-C}
and

(1.4)

\displaystyle\|W(x+\alpha)^{-1}A_{E}(x)W(x)-R_{-\theta}\|_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}\leq e^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})}{300}|n_{j+1}|}.

Above,
\hat{L}_{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})>0
is the
d
-th Lyapunov exponent of the dual operator, see Corollary
1.12
.
The core of the proof is to establish the almost localization for the dual model (Theorem
3.8
below). Once established, one can combine it with the quantitative duality arguments in
[
AJ2
]
to prove Theorem
1.2
. We include the duality argument in Appendix
A
in order to keep the paper self-contained.

One example to which Theorem
1.2
applies is the perturbation of the subcritical almost Mathieu operator, which is also subcritical due to the upper semi-continuity of the acceleration.

Example 1.3
.

Let
H^{\mathrm{PAMO}}_{\alpha,\theta,\nu,w}
be the operator with
v(\theta)=2\lambda\cos(2\pi\theta)+\nu\cdot w(\theta)
and
\varepsilon=0
, where
w(\theta)
is a trigonometric polynomial. For
|\lambda|<1
and sufficiently small
|\nu|<\nu_{0}(\alpha,\lambda,w)
,

\sigma(H^{\mathrm{PAMO}}_{\alpha,\theta,\nu,w})=\Sigma_{\alpha,0}
.

Theorem 1.4
.

For
|\lambda|<1
,
\beta(\alpha)=0
, and sufficiently small
|\nu|<\nu_{0}(\alpha,\lambda,w)
, for any
E\in\sigma(H^{\mathrm{PAMO}}_{\alpha,\theta,\nu,w})
, the cocycle
(\alpha,A_{E}^{\mathrm{PAMO}})
associated to
H^{\mathrm{PAMO}}_{\alpha,\theta,\nu,w}
is quantitatively almost reducible.

Remark 1.5
.

It is well-known that for
E\notin\sigma(H^{\mathrm{PAMO}}_{\alpha,\theta,\nu,w})
,
(\alpha,A_{E}^{\mathrm{PAMO}})
is
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
reducible, see e.g. Theorem 2.1 of
[
AJ2
]
.

As a consequence of Theorem
1.4
, we can prove the dry Ten Martini problem for
H^{\mathrm{PAMO}}_{\alpha,\theta,\nu,w}
.

Theorem 1.6
.

For
|\lambda|<1
,
\beta(\alpha)=0
, and for sufficiently small
|\nu|<\nu_{0}(\alpha,\lambda,w)
, all the spectral gaps of
H^{\mathrm{PAMO}}_{\alpha,\theta,\nu w}
as predicted by the gap labelling theorem are open.

This is an analogue of Theorem 1.1 of
[
AJ2
]
, we include its proof in the appendix for completeness. Next, we introduce the dual model of
H_{\alpha,\theta,\varepsilon}
:

(1.5)

\displaystyle(\hat{H}_{\alpha,\theta,\varepsilon}\phi)_{n}=\sum_{0<|k|\leq d}\hat{v}_{k}\phi_{n-k}e^{-2\pi k\varepsilon}+2\cos(2\pi(\theta+n\alpha))\phi_{n}.

This long-range operator can also be written in terms of block-valued Jacobi matrix, acting on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{C}^{d})
, as follows.

(1.6)

\displaystyle(\hat{H}_{\alpha,\theta,\varepsilon}\Phi)_{n}=B_{\varepsilon}\Phi_{n+1}+\tilde{B}_{\varepsilon}\Phi_{n-1}+C_{\varepsilon}(\theta+nd\alpha)\Phi_{n},

where
\Phi_{n}=(\phi_{nd+d-1},...,\phi_{nd+1},\phi_{nd})^{T}\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
, and
B_{\varepsilon},\tilde{B}_{\varepsilon}
and
C_{\varepsilon}
are defined in Section
2.3
.
The transfer matrix formulation of the block-valued Hamiltonian eigenvalue equation
\hat{H}_{\alpha,\theta,\varepsilon}\Phi=E\Phi
is

(1.7)

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}B_{\varepsilon}\Phi_{n+1}\\
\Phi_{n}\end{matrix}\right)=M_{E}^{\varepsilon}(\theta+nd\alpha)\left(\begin{matrix}B_{\varepsilon}\Phi_{n}\\
\Phi_{n-1}\end{matrix}\right),

where

(1.8)

\displaystyle M_{E}^{\varepsilon}(\theta)=\left(\begin{matrix}(E-C_{\varepsilon}(\theta))B_{\varepsilon}^{-1}&-\tilde{B}_{\varepsilon}\\
B_{\varepsilon}^{-1}&0\end{matrix}\right).

We denote its Lyapunov exponents by
\hat{L}_{1}(\alpha,M_{E}^{\varepsilon})\geq...\geq\hat{L}_{2d}(\alpha,M_{E}^{\varepsilon})
.
The transfer matrix
M_{E}^{\varepsilon}
can also be viewed as
d
-step transfer matrix for the scalar-valued Hamiltonian, where

(1.9)

\displaystyle M_{E}^{\varepsilon}(\theta)=\mathrm{diag}(B_{\varepsilon},I_{d})\cdot\hat{A}_{d,E}^{\varepsilon}(\alpha,\theta)\cdot\mathrm{diag}(B_{\varepsilon}^{-1},I_{d}),

in which the one step transfer matrix
\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon}
is as in (
2.46
).
It is easy to see by (
1.9
) that for
1\leq j\leq 2d
,

(1.10)

\displaystyle\hat{L}_{j}(d\alpha,M_{E}^{\varepsilon})=d\cdot\hat{L}_{j}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon}).

The transfer matrix
M_{E}^{0}
at an arbitrary energy
E\in\mathbb{R}
is complex symplectic, see (
2.38
). Hence

(1.11)

\displaystyle\hat{L}_{j}(d\alpha,M_{E}^{0})=-\hat{L}_{2d+1-j}(d\alpha,M_{E}^{0}),\text{ for }1\leq j\leq 2d.

Proving Anderson localization in the non-perturbative
\hat{L}_{d}(\alpha,M_{E}^{0})>0
regime has been a long standing problem in quasi-periodic Schrödinger operators, while the Anderson model counterpart has been known for over 40 years, see
[
G
,
L
,
KLS
,
MS
]
.
Resolving this problem is one of the main accomplishments of this paper.

Theorem 1.7
.

Let
\beta(\alpha)=0
and
\theta
be such that

(1.12)

\displaystyle\gamma(\alpha,\theta):=\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(-\frac{\log\|2\theta+n\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}}{|n|}\right)=0,

Then
\hat{H}_{\alpha,\theta,0}
has pure point spectrum with exponentially decaying eigenfunctions in

\sigma(\hat{H}_{\alpha,\theta,0})\cap\{E:\,\hat{L}_{d}(d\alpha,M_{E}^{0})>0\}.

We briefly discuss the difficulties. We write
\hat{L}_{j}(d\alpha,M_{E}^{0})
as
\hat{L}_{j}
.
The proof of Anderson localization in the literature relies on the exponential decay of the Green’s function (with Dirichlet boundary condition). In fact, let
\hat{H}_{\alpha,n}(\theta)
be the finite volume operator restricted to
[0,nd-1]
, see (
2.57
).
Let
\tilde{G}_{E,n}(\theta):=(\hat{H}_{\alpha,n}(\theta)-E)^{-1}
be the resolvent. The key to prove localization is to prove exponential decay of
|G_{E,n}(\theta+j\alpha;1,x)|
for
x\sim n/2
. By Cramer’s rule, one has

G_{E,n}(\theta+j\alpha;1,x)=\tilde{\mu}_{n,1,x}(\theta+j\alpha)/\tilde{f}_{E,n}(\theta+j\alpha),

where
\tilde{f}_{E,n}(\theta)=\det(\hat{H}_{\alpha,n}(\theta)-E)
.

In the scalar-valued case,
d=1
, the numerator
\hat{\mu}_{n,1,x}(\theta+j\alpha)
is equal to
\tilde{f}_{E,n-x}(\theta+(x+j)\alpha)
, which is an element (out of four in total) of
M_{n-x,E}^{0}(\theta+(x+j)\alpha)
, and can thus be easily controlled by

\|M_{n-x,E}^{0}(\theta+(x+j)\alpha)\|\leq\exp((n-x)(\hat{L}^{1}+\varepsilon)).

The key for the
d=1
case is to find a lower bound for the denominator
|\tilde{f}_{E,n}(\theta+j\alpha)|
for a properly chosen
j
.
It was proved by Jitomirskaya
[
J1
]
that
\tilde{f}_{E,n}(\theta)
is a polynomial in
\cos(2\pi(\theta+(n-1)\alpha/2))
of degree
n
, hence if it is uniformly small at
(n+1)
uniformly distributed
\theta+j\alpha
’s and at three consecutive scales
n_{0}-2,n_{0}-1,n_{0}
, then

\sup_{n\in\{n_{0}-2,n_{0}-1,n_{0}\}}\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{T}}|\tilde{f}_{E,n}(\theta)|\leq\exp(n(\hat{L}_{1}-\varepsilon)).

However in (and
only in
) the scalar-valued case, we have

\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{T}}\|M_{n_{0},E}^{0}(\theta)\|\leq\max_{n\in\{n_{0}-2,n_{0}-1,n_{0}\}}\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{T}}|\tilde{f}_{E,n}(\theta)|\leq\exp(n(\hat{L}_{1}-\varepsilon)),

which would lead to a contradiction.

In the block-valued case
d\geq 1
, there are significant difficulties in estimating both the numerator and the denominator.
First of all the numerator can no longer be identified with a determinant
\tilde{f}_{E,n}
.
In
[
BJ
,
K
]
and
[
AJ2
]
2
2

2

In
[
AJ2
]
,
d=\infty
, the numerators are estimated through a random walk type expansion, where the expansion is only efficient in the large coupling regime (replacing
2\cos
with
2\lambda^{-1}\cos
and
\lambda
small).
In the non-perturbative regime, a much tighter upper bound is need, in fact we prove

|\mu_{n,1,x}(\theta)|\leq|\hat{v}_{d}|^{nd}\cdot\max\left(e^{d^{-1}x(\hat{L}^{d-1}+\varepsilon)+(n-d^{-1}x)(\hat{L}^{d}+\varepsilon)},e^{d^{-1}x(\hat{L}^{d}+\varepsilon)+(n-d^{-1}x)(\hat{L}^{d+1}+\varepsilon)}\right)

uniformly in
\theta
and
\mu_{n,1,x}(\theta)
is the numerator of the Green’s function for the finite volume operator
P_{\alpha,n}(\theta)
with periodic boundary condition (see (
3.61
)).
We can prove a similar bound for
\tilde{\mu}_{n,1,x}
as well. The reason why we choose the periodic instead Dirichlet boundary condition lies in the estimate of the denominator which we elaborate on below.

For the denominator, we only know that
|\tilde{f}_{E,n}(\theta)|/|\hat{v}_{d}|^{nd}
is the determinant of the upper left
d\times d
corner of
M_{n,E}^{0}(\theta)
, hence it is unclear why
\sup_{\theta}|\tilde{f}_{E,n}(\theta)|/|\hat{v}_{d}|^{nd}
is close to

\sup_{\theta}\|\wedge^{d}M_{n,E}^{0}(\theta)\|=\exp(n(\hat{L}^{d}+o(1))).

One might attempt to argue that

(1.13)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{nd}\log|\tilde{f}_{E,n}(\theta)|=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\log|E-E^{\prime}|\,\hat{\mu}_{n,\alpha,\theta}(\mathrm{d}E^{\prime})\longrightarrow

(1.14)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\int_{\mathbb{R}}\log|E-E^{\prime}|\,\widehat{\mathcal{N}}(\mathrm{d}E^{\prime})=\hat{L}^{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{0})+\log|\hat{v}_{d}|,

where
\hat{\mu}_{n,\alpha,\theta}
is the finite-scale density of states measure (see (
2.58
)) and the second line is the Thouless formula (
2.60
) that was proved in the
d>1
case in
[
CS2
,
KS
,
HP
]
.
However the finite volume density of states measure
\hat{\mu}_{n,\alpha,\theta}
only converges in the weak-
*
sense (when integrated against continuous function of compact support) to the density of states measure
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
, which does not apply when integrated against
f(\cdot)=\log|E-\cdot|
for
E\in\mathbb{R}
.
Therefore the approach (
1.13
) does not lead to the desired result when
E\in\mathbb{R}
. See Remark
3.4
, too.
We resolve this difficulty by studying the Green’s function
G_{E,n}(\theta)=(P_{\alpha,n}(\theta)-E)^{-1}
with a periodic boundary condition.
Let
f_{E,n}(\theta)=\det(P_{\alpha,n}(\theta)-E)
be the denominator of the Green’s function
G_{E,n}(\theta)
. We prove that

\log|f_{E,n}(\theta)|=nd\log|\hat{v}_{d}|+\log|\det(M^{0}_{n,E}(\theta)-I_{2d})|.

We then show that for an admissible sequence of
n
, on a large measure set of
\theta
,

\log|\det(M^{0}_{n,E}(\theta)-I_{2d})|=n(\hat{L}^{d}+o(1)).

The choice of the admissible sequence relies crucially on Lemma 3.2 of
[
GS3
]
, which guarantees that the most expanding
d
-planes of
M_{n,E}^{0}
, respectively of
(M_{n,E}^{0})^{*}
, are not entirely orthogonal to each other.
Our lower bound Lemma
3.2
for the denominator is robust, and can be applied to establish Anderson localization for operators
\hat{H}_{\alpha,\theta}
on strips with general analytic potentials defined on higher-dimensional tori. We will address this problem in an upcoming work. For the cosine potential which arises in this paper, there is a more elementary argument using Herman’s subharmonic argument, see Lemma
3.5
.

A stronger version of
almost localization
holds for arbitrary
\theta
, see Theorem
3.8
.
The link between Theorem
3.8
and Theorem
1.2
is provided by Corollary
1.12
that if
E\in\Sigma_{\alpha,0}
, then the dual Lyapunov exponent satisfies
\hat{L}_{d}(d\alpha,M_{E}^{0})>0
.
This was announced in Jitomirskaya’s 2022 ICM talk, and is indeed a consequence of a non-selfadjoint variant of Haro and Puig’s result on dual Lyapunov exponents, which we elaborate on below.

In
[
HP
]
, Haro and Puig proved the following remarkable theorem concerning Lyapunov exponents of dual models. The techniques in
[
HP
]
are inspired by Johnson’s parametrization of the stable and unstable subspaces in the uniformly hyperbolic setting via the Weyl-Kodaira
M
-matrix, see
[
Joh
,
JPS
,
JONNF
]
.

Theorem 1.8
.

[
HP
]
*Theorem 1.3 and Corollary 1.6
For any irrational
\alpha
,

(1.15)

\displaystyle L^{1}(\alpha,A_{E,0})=\hat{L}^{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{0})+\log|\hat{v}_{-d}|=\sum_{j:\hat{L}_{j}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{0})\geq 0}\hat{L}_{j}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{0})+\log|\hat{v}_{-d}|.

The proof is built on two key ingredients.
One is the Thouless formula, that links the Lyapunov exponent to the density of states measure, for both
H_{\alpha,\theta,\varepsilon=0}
(the selfadjoint Schrödinger operator) and
\hat{H}_{\alpha,\theta,\varepsilon=0}
(the dual model on the strip, which is also selfadjoint).
The second is the fact that the two dual selfadjoint operators share the same density of states measure.

The following non-selfadjoint version of the Haro, Puig formula was announced in Jitomirskaya’s ICM talk in 2022.

Theorem 1.9
.

[Jitomirskaya, ICM talk 2022]
For any irrational
\alpha
,

(1.16)

\displaystyle L^{1}(\alpha,A_{E,\varepsilon})=L^{1}(\alpha,A_{E,0})-\sum_{j:0\leq\hat{L}_{j}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{0})<2\pi\varepsilon}\hat{L}_{j}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{0})+\#\{j:0\leq\hat{L}_{j}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{0})<2\pi\varepsilon\}\cdot(2\pi\varepsilon).

Noting that
\hat{L}_{j}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})=\hat{L}_{j}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{0})-2\pi\varepsilon
, see (
2.49
), and taking Haro and Puig’s formula (which holds at
\varepsilon=0
) into account, we see that Theorem
1.9
in the same as a non-selfadjoint version of Haro and Puig’s formula, see Theorem
1.10
below.

Since the proof of Theorem
1.9
does not seem available in the literature, and because it appears in our resolution of the almost reducibility conjecture, we provide a proof in Section
6
for the reader’s convenience.

Theorem 1.10
.

For any irrational
\alpha
,

(1.17)

\displaystyle L^{1}(\alpha,A_{E,\varepsilon})=\sum_{j:\hat{L}_{j}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})\geq 0}\hat{L}_{j}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})+\log|\hat{v}_{-d}e^{2\pi d\varepsilon}|.

A major difficulty in extending Haro and Puig’s formula to the non-selfadjoint case (i.e., when
\varepsilon\neq 0
) is the lack of spectral theorems. Hence there is no density of states measure, which played a crucial role in Haro and Puig’s original proof.
We overcome this problem by establishing the Aubry duality, by means of the characters on cyclic groups, for finite scale operators with periodic boundary conditions and rational frequencies.
These finite-scale operators naturally have (finite scale) density of states measures via the normalized counting measures of the eigenvalues.
We then prove Thouless formulas for the finite scale rational-frequency operators, which after combining with the duality, lead to (
1.17
) with rational
\alpha
.
Finally, (
1.17
) for irrational
\alpha
follows from rational approximation and continuity of the Lyapunov exponents at irrational frequencies.

It was also addressed by Jitomirskaya in her ICM talk that Theorem
1.9
leads to the following characterization of Avila’s acceleration.

Corollary 1.11
.

For irrational
\alpha
, and any
\varepsilon\geq 0

(1.18)

\displaystyle\kappa(\alpha,A_{E,\varepsilon})=\#\{j:0\leq\hat{L}_{j}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})\leq 2\pi\varepsilon\}.

We give a proof of this in Section
6
.
Clearly, Corollary
1.11
and (
2.49
) lead to the following.

Corollary 1.12
.

If the acceleration of
H_{\alpha,\theta,\varepsilon}
satisfies
\kappa(\alpha,A_{E,\varepsilon})\equiv 0
for
|\varepsilon|\leq\varepsilon_{1}
, then

(1.19)

\displaystyle\hat{L}_{1}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{0})\geq...\geq\hat{L}_{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{0})\geq 2\pi\varepsilon_{1}>0.

In the rest of the paper, we shall omit the dependence on
\varepsilon
in various notations in the
\varepsilon=0
case.

The paper is organized as follows: some preliminary knowledge is recalled in Sec.
2
, the proofs of Theorem
1.7
and almost localization are given in Sec.
3
assuming the numerator bound of the Green’s function established in Sec.
4
and the denominator bound in Sec.
5
. Finally we present the proof of Theorem
1.10
in Sec.
6
.

Notations:
for
x\in\mathbb{R}
, let
\|x\|_{\mathbb{T}}:=\mathrm{dist}(x,\mathbb{Z})
be the torus norm, and
\lfloor x\rfloor=\sup\{m\in\mathbb{Z}:\,m\leq x\}
be the integer part of
x
. If
a\leq Cb
for some constant
C>0
, we shall write
a=O(b)
.
For a matrix
M\in\mathbb{C}^{d_{1}\times d_{2}}
, and the sets of indicies
B_{1}\subset[1,d_{1}]\cap\mathbb{Z},B_{2}\subset[1,d_{2}]\cap\mathbb{Z}
, let
M_{B_{1},B_{2}}
be the submatrix of
M
with row numbers in
B_{1}
and column numbers in
B_{2}
.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Continued fraction expansion

Give
\alpha\in(0,1)
, let
[a_{1},a_{2},...]
be the continued fraction expansion of
\alpha
. For
n\geq 1
, let
p_{n}/q_{n}=[a_{1},a_{2},...,a_{n}]
be the continued fraction approximants.
The following property is well-known for
n\geq 1
,

(2.20)

\displaystyle\|q_{n}\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}=\min_{1\leq k<q_{n+1}}\|k\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}},

The
\beta(\alpha)
exponent measures the exponential closeness of
\alpha
to rational numbers:

(2.21)

\displaystyle\beta(\alpha):=\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{\log q_{n+1}}{q_{n}}=\limsup_{n\to\infty}\left(-\frac{\log\|n\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}}{n}\right).

2.2.
Lyapunov exponents

Let
(\alpha,A)\in(\mathbb{T},C^{\omega}(\mathbb{T},\mathrm{Mat}(k,\mathbb{C})))
.
Let

(2.22)

\displaystyle A_{n}(\alpha,\theta)=A(\theta+(n-1)\alpha)\cdots A(\theta).

Let the finite-scale and infinite-scale Lyapunov exponents be defined as

(2.23)

\displaystyle L_{j,(n)}(\alpha,A):=\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\log\sigma_{j}(A_{n}(\alpha,\theta))\,\mathrm{d}\theta,\text{ for }1\leq j\leq k,

where
\sigma_{j}(A)
is the
j
-th singular value of
A
, and the
j
-th Lyapunov exponent

(2.24)

\displaystyle L_{j}(\alpha,A)=\lim_{n\to\infty}L_{j,(n)}(\alpha,A).

It is easy to see that for
1\leq j\leq k
,

(2.25)

\displaystyle L^{j}_{(n)}(\alpha,A):=\sum_{\ell=1}^{j}L_{\ell,(n)}(\alpha,A)=\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\log\|\wedge^{j}A_{n}(\alpha,\theta)\|\,\mathrm{d}\theta,

where
\wedge^{j}A
is the
j
-th exterior power of
A
. Similarly
L^{j}(\alpha,A)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{j}L_{\ell}(\alpha,A)
.
If
\alpha=p/q\in\mathbb{Q}
, we define

(2.26)

\displaystyle L^{j}(p/q,A,\theta)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\log\|\wedge^{j}A_{n}(p/q,\theta)\|=\frac{1}{q}\log(\rho(\wedge^{j}A_{q}(p/q,\theta))),

where
\rho(A)
is the spectral radius of
A
.
Let
L_{1}(p/q,A,\theta)=L^{1}(p/q,A,\theta)
and

(2.27)

\displaystyle L_{j}(p/q,A,\theta)=L^{j}(p/q,A,\theta)-L^{j-1}(p/q,A,\theta),\text{ for }j\geq 2,

and for
1\leq j\leq k
,

(2.28)

\displaystyle L^{j}(p/q,A)=\int_{\mathbb{T}}L^{j}(p/q,A,\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\theta,\text{ and }L_{j}(p/q,A)=\int_{\mathbb{T}}L_{j}(p/q,A,\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\theta.

2.3.
Transfer matrix of
\hat{H}_{\alpha,\theta,\varepsilon}

As we mentioned in the introduction,
\hat{H}_{\alpha,\theta,\varepsilon}
can be viewed as a block-valued Jacobi matrix:

(2.29)

\displaystyle(\hat{H}_{\alpha,\theta,\varepsilon}\Phi)_{n}=B_{\varepsilon}\Phi_{n+1}+\tilde{B}_{\varepsilon}\Phi_{n-1}+C_{\varepsilon}(\theta+nd\alpha)\Phi_{n},

where
\Phi_{n}=(\phi_{nd+d-1},...,\phi_{nd+1},\phi_{nd})^{T}\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
, the upper triangular matrix

(2.30)

\displaystyle B_{\varepsilon}=\left(\begin{matrix}\hat{v}_{-d}e^{2\pi d\varepsilon}&\hat{v}_{-d+1}e^{2\pi(d-1)\varepsilon}\ &...\ &\hat{v}_{-1}e^{2\pi\varepsilon}\\
&\hat{v}_{-d}e^{2\pi d\varepsilon}&&\hat{v}_{-2}e^{4\pi\varepsilon}\\
\\
&&\ddots\\
&&\\
&&&\hat{v}_{-d}e^{2\pi d\varepsilon}\end{matrix}\right)=e^{2\pi d\varepsilon}F_{\varepsilon}B_{0}F_{\varepsilon}^{-1},

where
F_{\varepsilon}=\mathrm{diag}(e^{2\pi\varepsilon},e^{4\pi\varepsilon},...,e^{2\pi d\varepsilon})
.
The lower triangular matrix

\tilde{B}_{\varepsilon}=e^{-2\pi d\varepsilon}F_{\varepsilon}B_{0}^{*}F_{\varepsilon}^{-1}
,
and

(2.31)

\displaystyle C_{\varepsilon}(\theta)=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}2\cos 2\pi(\theta+(d-1)\alpha)&\hat{v}_{1}e^{-2\pi\varepsilon}&\hat{v}_{2}e^{-4\pi\varepsilon}&...&&\hat{v}_{d-1}e^{-2\pi(d-1)\varepsilon}\\
\hat{v}_{-1}e^{2\pi\varepsilon}&2\cos 2\pi(\theta+(d-2)\alpha)&\hat{v}_{1}e^{-2\pi\varepsilon}&...&&\hat{v}_{d-2}e^{-2\pi(d-2)\varepsilon}\\
\hat{v}_{-2}e^{4\pi\varepsilon}&\hat{v}_{-1}e^{2\pi\varepsilon}&\ddots&\ddots&&\vdots\\
\vdots&&\ddots\\
&&&&&\hat{v}_{1}e^{-2\pi\varepsilon}\\
\hat{v}_{-d+1}e^{2\pi(d-1)\varepsilon}&...&&&\hat{v}_{-1}e^{2\pi\varepsilon}&2\cos 2\pi\theta\end{matrix}\right)

(2.32)

\displaystyle=F_{\varepsilon}C_{0}(\theta)F_{\varepsilon}^{-1}.

In the rest of the paper, we shall write
C_{\varepsilon=0}(\theta)
and
B_{\varepsilon=0}
as
C(\theta)
and
B
, and
M_{E}^{\varepsilon=0}=M_{E}
,
\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon=0}=\hat{A}_{E}
, respectively.
The eigenvalue equation
\hat{H}_{\alpha,\theta,\varepsilon}\phi=E\phi
can be reformulated as:

(2.33)

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}B_{\varepsilon}\Phi_{n+1}\\
\Phi_{n}\end{matrix}\right)=M_{E}^{\varepsilon}(\theta+nd\alpha)\left(\begin{matrix}B_{\varepsilon}\Phi_{n}\\
\Phi_{n-1}\end{matrix}\right),

where

(2.34)

\displaystyle M_{E}^{\varepsilon}(\theta)=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}(E-C_{\varepsilon}(\theta))B_{\varepsilon}^{-1}&-\tilde{B}_{\varepsilon}\\
B_{\varepsilon}^{-1}&0\end{matrix}\right)

(2.35)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}e^{-2\pi d\varepsilon}F_{\varepsilon}(E-C(\theta))B^{-1}F_{\varepsilon}^{-1}&-e^{-2\pi d\varepsilon}F_{\varepsilon}B^{*}F_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\\
e^{-2\pi d\varepsilon}F_{\varepsilon}B^{-1}F_{\varepsilon}^{-1}&0\end{matrix}\right)

(2.36)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-2\pi d\varepsilon}\mathrm{diag}(F_{\varepsilon},F_{\varepsilon})\cdot M_{E}(\theta)\cdot\mathrm{diag}(F_{\varepsilon}^{-1},F_{\varepsilon}^{-1}).

Clearly this implies that for any
1\leq j\leq 2d
,

(2.37)

\displaystyle L_{j}(d\alpha,M_{E}^{\varepsilon})=L_{j}(d\alpha,M_{E})-2\pi d\varepsilon.

It is easy to see that

(2.38)

\displaystyle M_{E}^{*}\cdot\Omega\cdot M_{E}=\Omega,

where

(2.39)

\displaystyle\Omega:=\left(\begin{matrix}0&I_{d}\\
-I_{d}&0\end{matrix}\right).

Hence
M_{E}^{0}
is complex symplectic matrix.
This implies that for any
1\leq j\leq 2d
,

(2.40)

\displaystyle\hat{L}_{j}(d\alpha,M_{E})=-\hat{L}_{2d+1-j}(d\alpha,M_{E}).

Let

\displaystyle M_{n,E}(\theta)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}M_{n,E}^{UL}(\theta)&M_{n,E}^{UR}(\theta)\\
\hline\cr M_{n,E}^{LL}(\theta)&M_{n,E}^{LR}(\theta)\end{array}\right),

where each
M_{n,E}^{\star}
is a
d\times d
block,
\star=UL,UR,LL,LR
.
The following recursive relations hold: for
n=1
,

(2.43)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}M_{1,E}^{UL}(\theta)=-(C(\theta)-E)B^{-1}\\
M_{1,E}^{UR}(\theta)=-B^{*}\\
M_{1,E}^{LL}(\theta)=B^{-1}\\
M_{1,E}^{LR}(\theta)=0\end{cases}

and for
n\geq 2
,

(2.44)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}M_{n,E}^{UL}(\theta)=-M_{n-1,E}^{UL}(\theta+d\alpha)(C(\theta)-E)B^{-1}+M_{n-1,E}^{UR}(\theta+d\alpha)B^{-1}\\
M_{n,E}^{UR}(\theta)=-M_{n-1,E}^{UL}(\theta+d\alpha)B^{*}\\
M_{n,E}^{LL}(\theta)=-M_{n-1,E}^{LL}(\theta+d\alpha)(C(\theta)-E)B^{-1}+M_{n-1,E}^{LR}(\theta+d\alpha)B^{-1}\\
M_{n,E}^{LR}(\theta)=-M_{n-1,E}^{LL}(\theta+d\alpha)B^{*}\end{cases}

The eigenvalue equation
\hat{H}_{\alpha,\theta,\varepsilon}\phi=E\phi
(when viewed as a scalar-valued Hamiltonian) can also be rewritten as:

(2.45)

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}\phi_{n+d}\\
\vdots\\
\phi_{n+1}\\
\phi_{n}\\
\vdots\\
\phi_{n-d+1}\end{matrix}\right)=\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon}(\theta+n\alpha)\left(\begin{matrix}\phi_{n+d-1}\\
\vdots\\
\phi_{n}\\
\phi_{n-1}\\
\vdots\\
\phi_{n-d}\end{matrix}\right),

where

(2.46)

\begin{split}&\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon}(\theta):=\frac{1}{\hat{v}_{-d}e^{2\pi d\varepsilon}}\\
&\left(\begin{matrix}-\hat{v}_{1-d}e^{2\pi(d-1)\varepsilon}&\cdots&-\hat{v}_{-1}e^{2\pi\varepsilon}&E-2\cos(2\pi\theta)&|&-\hat{v}_{1}e^{-2\pi\varepsilon}&\cdots&-\hat{v}_{d-1}e^{-2\pi(d-1)\varepsilon}&-\hat{v}_{d}e^{-2\pi d\varepsilon}\\
\hat{v}_{-d}e^{2\pi d\varepsilon}&&&&|\\
&\ddots&&&|&\\
&&\hat{v}_{-d}e^{2\pi d\varepsilon}&&|&\\
\hline\cr&&&\hat{v}_{-d}e^{2\pi d\varepsilon}&|&\\
&&&&|&\hat{v}_{-d}e^{2\pi d\varepsilon}\\
&&&&|&&\ddots\\
&&&&|&&&\hat{v}_{-d}e^{2\pi d\varepsilon}\end{matrix}\right),\end{split}

is the 1-step transfer matrix.
By (
2.45
) and (
2.33
) we have

(2.47)

\displaystyle M_{E}^{\varepsilon}(\theta)=\mathrm{diag}(B_{\varepsilon},I_{d})\cdot\hat{A}_{d,E}^{\varepsilon}(\alpha,\theta)\cdot\mathrm{diag}(B_{\varepsilon}^{-1},I_{d}).

Recall that
\hat{A}_{d}
signifies a product of
d
factors of
\hat{A}
matrices.
Hence for any
1\leq m\leq 2d
,

(2.48)

\displaystyle\hat{L}_{m}(d\alpha,M_{E}^{\varepsilon})=d\cdot\hat{L}_{m}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon}).

Combining this with (
2.37
) yields

(2.49)

\displaystyle\hat{L}_{m}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})=\hat{L}_{m}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})-2\pi\varepsilon.

2.4.
Avila’s acceleration

Let
(\alpha,A)\in(\mathbb{T},C^{\omega}(\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})))
, and
A_{\varepsilon}(\theta):=A(\theta+i\varepsilon)
.
The (top) Lyapunov exponent
L_{1}(\alpha,A_{\varepsilon})
is a convex and even function in
\varepsilon
.
Avila defined the acceleration to be the right-derivative as follows:

(2.50)

\displaystyle\kappa(\alpha,A_{\varepsilon}):=\lim_{\delta\to 0^{+}}\frac{L_{1}(\alpha,A_{\varepsilon+\delta})-L_{1}(\alpha,A_{\varepsilon})}{2\pi\delta}.

As a cornerstone of his Global theory
[
Av3
]
, he showed that for
A\in\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
and irrational
\alpha
,
\kappa(\alpha,A_{\varepsilon})\in\mathbb{Z}
is always quantized.

2.5.
Uniform upper semi-continuity

The following lemma is an easy corollary of the arguments, essentially a subadditivity argument, in the proof of Lemma 5.1 in
[
AJS
]
.

Lemma 2.1
.

For any small
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
N=N(\varepsilon,\alpha,v,E,d)
such that for any
1\leq j\leq 2d
and
n\geq N
,

(2.51)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\log\left\|\wedge^{j}M_{n,E}(\theta)\right\|\leq\hat{L}_{1}(d\alpha,\wedge^{j}M_{E})+\varepsilon=\hat{L}^{j}(d\alpha,M_{E})+\varepsilon,

uniformly in
\theta\in\mathbb{T}
.

2.6.
Large deviation estimates

The following large deviation estimates play a crucial role in Section
5
.
For Diophantine
\alpha
, a stronger form (with a deviation of size
n^{-\sigma}
) of the following estimate for
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
cocycles was first proved in Lemma 1.1 of
[
BG
]
by Bourgain and Goldstein, and further developed in sharp forms in
[
GS1
,
GS2
]
by Goldstein and Schlag. In this paper we need the following weaker form, but it holds for any
\alpha
such that
\beta(\alpha)=0
.

Lemma 2.2
.

[
HZ
]
*Theorem 1.1
Let

(2.52)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{n,E,\varepsilon}^{d}:=\left\{\theta:\,\frac{1}{n}\log\|\wedge^{d}M_{n,E}(\theta)\|\leq(1-\varepsilon)\cdot\hat{L}^{d}(d\alpha,M_{E})\right\}

be the large deviation set of
\|\wedge^{d}M_{n,E}(\theta)\|
.
For
\alpha
such that
\beta(\alpha)=0
, for any small
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
N=N(\varepsilon,\alpha,\hat{L}_{d}(d\alpha,M_{E}),v,d)>0
and
c_{1}=c_{1}(v,d)>0
, such that for
n\geq N
,

(2.53)

\displaystyle\mathrm{mes}(\mathcal{B}_{n,E,\varepsilon}^{d})\leq e^{-c_{1}n\varepsilon^{2}\cdot\hat{L}^{d}(d\alpha,M_{E})}.

Remark 2.3
.

The above large deviation lemma was stated and proved in
[
HZ
]
for
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
cocycles, but it is clear that the same proof applies to
(d\alpha,\wedge^{d}M_{E})
.

2.7.
Avalanche Principle

The Avalanche Principle was first introduced for
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
cocycles by Goldstein and Schlag in
[
GS1
]
.
Later it was extended to higher-dimensional cocycles in
[
Sch1
,
DK
]
.
The following form is from
[
DK
]
.

Theorem 2.4
.

[
DK
]
*Proposition 2.42
There exists
c_{0},\tilde{C}_{0}>0
such that given
0<\varepsilon<1
,
0<\kappa<c_{0}\varepsilon^{2}
and
g_{0},g_{1},...,g_{n-1}\in\mathrm{Mat}(m,\mathbb{R})\setminus\{0\}
, if

(2.54)

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma_{1}(g_{j})}{\sigma_{2}(g_{j})}>\frac{1}{\kappa},\text{ for all }0\leq j\leq n-1

(2.55)

\displaystyle\frac{\|g_{j}g_{j-1}\|}{\|g_{j}\|\|g_{j-1}\|}>\varepsilon,\text{ for all }1\leq j\leq n-1

then

(2.56)

\displaystyle\left|\log\|g_{n-1}\cdots g_{0}\|+\sum_{j=1}^{n-2}\log\|g_{j}\|-\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\log\|g_{j}g_{j-1}\|\right|\leq\tilde{C}_{0}n\frac{\kappa}{\varepsilon^{2}}.

2.8.
Integrated density of states

Let

(2.57)

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{\alpha,n}(\theta):=\chi_{[0,nd-1]}\hat{H}_{\alpha,\theta,\varepsilon=0}\,\chi_{[0,nd-1]}

be the restriction of the operator to the interval
[0,nd-1]
with Dirichlet boundary condition.
Let
E_{j}(\theta)
,
j=0,1,...,nd-1
be the eigenvalues of
\hat{H}_{\alpha,n}(\theta)
.
Consider the sequence of measures

(2.58)

\displaystyle\hat{\mu}_{n,\alpha,\theta}(\mathrm{d}E):=\frac{1}{nd}\sum_{j=0}^{nd-1}\delta_{E_{j}(\theta)}(\mathrm{d}E).

It is well-known the weak limit exists and is independent of
\theta
(up to measure zero):

(2.59)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\hat{\mu}_{n,\alpha,\theta}(\mathrm{d}E)=\widehat{\mathcal{N}}(\mathrm{d}E).

The distribution function
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}(E)
is called the integrated density of states.

The integrated density of states
\mathcal{N}
are defined analogously for
H_{\alpha,\theta,\varepsilon=0}
(indeed as the
d=1
special case of the above). It is well-known that
\mathcal{N}=\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
, see e.g.
[
HP
]

The Thouless formula relates the Lyapunov exponent to the integrated density of states:

(2.60)

\displaystyle\hat{L}^{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})+\log|\hat{v}_{d}|=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\log|E^{\prime}-E|\,\widehat{\mathcal{N}}(\mathrm{d}E^{\prime}).

The scalar case
d=1
was proved in
[
AS
,
CS1
]
, and the
d>1
case was proved in
[
CS2
,
KS
,
HP
]
.

3.
Anderson localization and almost localization

3.1.
Green’s function

The key to prove Anderson localization is to estimate the decay of Green’s functions.
We consider the finite volume Hamiltonian with periodic boundary conditions. To distinguish
\hat{H}_{\alpha,n}(\theta)
(see (
2.57
)), which exhibits Dirichlet boundary conditions, from the operator with periodic ones, we denote the latter by
P_{\alpha,n}(\theta)
. Moreover, for simplicity we shall omit
\alpha
in the notation in the rest of the section. Thus,

(3.61)

\displaystyle P_{n}(\theta)=\left(\begin{matrix}C(\theta+(n-1)d\alpha)&B^{*}&&&B\\
B&C(\theta+(n-2)d\alpha)&\ddots\\
&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots\\
&&\ddots&\ddots&B^{*}\\
B^{*}&&&B&C(\theta)\end{matrix}\right).

Let

(3.62)

\displaystyle f_{E,n}(\theta):=\det(P_{n}(\theta)-E),

and

(3.63)

\displaystyle G_{E,n}(\theta):=(P_{n}(\theta)-E)^{-1}

be the resolvent, which is also called the finite volume Green’s function.
It follows from Cramer’s rule that

(3.64)

\displaystyle G_{E,n}(\theta;x,y)=\frac{\mu_{n,x,y}(\theta)}{f_{E,n}(\theta)},

where
\mu_{n,x,y}(\theta)
is the determinant of the submatrix of
P_{n}(\theta)-E
obtained by deleting the
x
-th row and
y
-th column.
Let
\hat{u}
be a solution to the eigenvalue equation
\hat{H}_{\alpha,\theta}\hat{u}=E\hat{u}
.
For any
k\in\mathbb{Z}

the following Poisson formula holds for all
k\leq m\leq k+nd-1
:

(3.65)

\displaystyle\hat{u}_{m}=

\displaystyle\sum_{y_{1}=0}^{d-1}G_{E,n}(\theta+k\alpha;m-k,y_{1})\left(B^{*}\cdot\left(\begin{matrix}\hat{u}_{k+nd-1}-\hat{u}_{k+d-1}\\
\vdots\\
\hat{u}_{k+(n-1)d}-\hat{u}_{k}\end{matrix}\right)\right)_{y_{1}}

(3.66)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum_{y_{2}=(n-1)d}^{nd-1}G_{E,n}(\theta+k\alpha;m-k,y_{2})\left(B\cdot\left(\begin{matrix}\hat{u}_{k+d-1}-\hat{u}_{k+nd-1}\\
\vdots\\
\hat{u}_{k}-\hat{u}_{k+(n-1)d}\end{matrix}\right)\right)_{y_{2}-(n-1)d},

in which
(M)_{y}
refers to the element of vector
M
in row
y
.
This implies

(3.67)

\displaystyle|\hat{u}_{m}|\leq

\displaystyle 4d^{3/2}\|B\|\cdot\max_{y\in\{0,...,d-1\}\cup\{(n-1)d,...,nd-1\}}|G_{E,n}(\theta+k\alpha;m-k,y)|\cdot

(3.68)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\cdot\max_{\ell\in\{0,...,d-1\}\cup\{(n-1)d,...,nd-1\}}|\hat{u}_{k+\ell}|

For the remainder of this section, we shall write
\hat{L}^{k}(d\alpha,M_{E}),\hat{L}_{k}(d\alpha,M_{E})
as
\hat{L}^{k},\hat{L}_{k}
, respectively.
We split the estimate of the Green’s function into Lemma
3.1
for the numerator, and Lemma
3.2
for the denominator.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
3d\leq y\leq(n-1)d-1
and
0\leq x\leq d-1
or
(n-1)d\leq x\leq nd-1
. Then
for any
\varepsilon>0
, and uniformly in
\theta\in\mathbb{T}
,

(3.69)

\displaystyle|\mu_{n,x,y}(\theta)|\leq C_{d}\|B^{-1}\|^{2}\cdot|\det B|^{n}\cdot\max\left(e^{\ell(1+\varepsilon)\hat{L}^{d-1}+(n-\ell)(1+\varepsilon)\hat{L}^{d}}+e^{\ell(1+\varepsilon)\hat{L}^{d}+(n-\ell)(1+\varepsilon)\hat{L}^{d-1}}\right),

provided
n>N(\varepsilon,\alpha,E,d,v)
is large enough.
Here
C_{d}
is a constant depending only on
d
.

We defer the proof to Section
4
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
N_{0}=N_{0}(\varepsilon,\alpha,E,d)
and
\kappa_{0}=\kappa_{0}(\varepsilon,\alpha,E,d,N_{0})
be defined as in Lemma
5.3
and
\mathcal{N}:=\{n\geq 8N_{0}:\|nd\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}\leq\kappa_{0}\}
be the
\kappa_{0}
-admissible sequence.
For any
E\in\mathbb{R}
, for any small
0<\varepsilon<\hat{L}_{d}/(11\hat{L}^{d})
, and for
n\geq N(\varepsilon,\alpha,E,d,v)
and
n\in\mathcal{N}
, we have

(3.70)

\displaystyle\mathrm{mes}\left\{\theta\in\mathbb{T}:\frac{1}{n}\log|f_{E,n}(\theta)|<|\det B|+(1-8\varepsilon)\hat{L}^{d}\right\}<\frac{1}{10}.

We present the proof of Lemma
3.2
in Section
5
.
Although Lemma
3.2
does not hold for arbitrary
n
, it holds for all
\kappa_{0}
-admissible
n
’s, which form a positive density subsequence. Indeed, there exists
C_{\kappa_{0},d,\alpha}
such that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a
\kappa_{0}
-admissible
\tilde{n}
such that
|n-\tilde{n}|\leq C_{\kappa_{0},d,\alpha}
.
Hence Lemma
3.2
yields the following.

Corollary 3.3
.

For any
E\in\mathbb{R}
, for any small
0<\varepsilon<\hat{L}_{d}/(11\hat{L}^{d})
, let
N_{0},\kappa_{0}
be as in Lemma
3.2
.
There exists
C_{\kappa_{0},d,\alpha}>0
such that for any
n\geq N(\varepsilon,\alpha,E,d,v)
, there exists
\kappa_{0}
-admissible
\tilde{n}
,
|\tilde{n}-n|\leq C_{\kappa_{0},d,\alpha}
, so that

(3.71)

\displaystyle\mathrm{mes}\left\{\theta\in\mathbb{T}:\frac{1}{\tilde{n}}\log|f_{E,\tilde{n}}(\theta)|<|\det B|+(1-8\varepsilon)\hat{L}^{d}\right\}<\frac{1}{10}.

Remark 3.4
.

The reason why we consider
P_{\alpha,n}(\theta)
with periodic boundary conditions, instead of the finite volume operator
\hat{H}_{\alpha,n}
on
[0,nd-1]
with Dirichlet boundary conditions, lies with the differences between the denominators of their respective Green’s functions. In fact,
as we will show in Lemma
5.1

|f_{E,n}(\theta)|=|\det B|^{n}\cdot|\det(M_{n,E}(\theta)-I_{2d})|,

while

|\det(\hat{H}_{\alpha,n}(\theta)-E)|=|\det B|^{n}\cdot|\det M_{n,E}^{UL}(\theta)|,

see
[
BGV
]
.
To prove non-perturbative Anderson localization, it is crucial to obtain a lower bound

|\det(M_{n,E}(\theta)-I_{2d})|\gtrsim\exp(n(\hat{L}^{d}-\varepsilon))

for certain
\theta
’s, which we establish in Lemma
5.13
along the
\kappa_{0}
-admissible sequence. It appears to be more difficult to obtain a similar result for
|\det M_{n,E}^{UL}(\theta)|
. One may attempt to derive the inequality

|\det M_{n,E}^{UL}(\theta)|\gtrsim\exp(n(\hat{L}^{d}-\varepsilon))

via Thouless’ formula.
For
E\notin\mathbb{R}
, such a lower bound for
|\det M_{n,E}^{UL}(\theta)|
can indeed be deduced directly from the Thouless formula (proved for scalar case
(d=1)
in
[
AS
,
CS1
]
, and operators on the strip in
[
CS2
,
KS
,
HP
]
).
In fact, if
E\notin\mathbb{R}
, then
g(E^{\prime})=\log|E-E^{\prime}|
is a continuous function on
\mathbb{R}
. By the
\sigma^{*}
-convergence of the finite volume density of states measure to the (infinite volume) density states measure
\widehat{\mathcal{N}}
, it follows that

(3.72)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{nd}(\log\left|\det M_{n,E}^{UL}(\theta)\right|+n\log|\det B|)

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{nd}\log|\det(\hat{H}_{\alpha,n}(\theta)-E)|

(3.73)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\log|E-E^{\prime}|\,\widehat{\mathcal{N}}(\mathrm{d}E^{\prime})=\frac{1}{d}\hat{L}^{d}+\log|\hat{v}_{d}|

for a.e.
\theta
.
However this simple proof does not apply to
E\in\mathbb{R}
, since
g
is not continuous.
See also (
1.13
).

We emphasize the robustness of the lower bound in Lemma
3.2
for the denominator. It can be applied to operators
\hat{H}_{\alpha,\theta}
on the strip with general analytic potentials on higher-dimensional tori. We will use it in an upcoming work to address non-perturbative Anderson localization in the general case.
For the special case of cosine potential, a simpler lower bound can be obtained via Herman’s subharmonic argument.

Lemma 3.5
.

For any
n\geq 1
, we have

(3.74)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\log|f_{E,n}(\theta)|\,\mathrm{d}\theta\geq 0=\hat{L}^{d}+d\log|\hat{v}_{d}|.

We present the proof at the end of Section
5
. Note the equality sign above is exactly Haro-Puig’s theorem (see Theorem
1.8
) combined with the subcritical assumption on the Schrödinger cocycle.

3.2.
Almost localization

The proof of almost localization follows the scheme of
[
AJ2
]
, combined with our Lemmas
3.1
and
3.2
.
A minor adjustment is that
[
AJ2
]
deals with
\alpha\in\mathrm{DC}
while we assume a slightly weaker condition
\beta(\alpha)=0
.
The almost localization for the
\beta(\alpha)=0
case was previously addressed for the extended Harper’s model in
[
H
]
.
It should be sufficient to assume that
0\leq\beta(\alpha)<c
for some small constant
c>0
, but we do not pursue this improvement here.

Let
C_{3}>0
be the universal constant in Lemma
3.10
below.
Fix

(3.75)

\displaystyle 0<\varepsilon_{0}<\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})}{30\hat{L}^{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})+100C_{3}d}<\frac{\hat{L}_{d}}{11\hat{L}^{d}}.

Definition 3.1

(
\varepsilon_{0}
-resonance of
\theta
)
.

Let
\alpha,\theta\in\mathbb{T}
and constant
\varepsilon_{0}>0
. We say
k\in\mathbb{Z}
is an
\varepsilon_{0}
-resonance of
\theta
if
\|2\theta-k\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}\leq e^{-\varepsilon_{0}|k|}
and
\|2\theta-k\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}=\min_{|j|\leq k}\|2\theta-j\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}
.

Since
\beta(\alpha)=0
, there exists
C_{0}=C_{0}(\alpha,\varepsilon_{1})>0
such that for
n\neq 0
,

(3.76)

\displaystyle\|n\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geq C_{0}e^{-\varepsilon_{1}|n|}.

In the following we shall fix
\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{0}/1000
.
This implies the following.

Lemma 3.6
.

If
|k|
is large enough, and
\|2\theta-k\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}\leq e^{-\varepsilon_{0}|k|}
, then
\|2\theta-k\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}=\min_{|j|\leq k}\|2\theta-j\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}
.

Proof.

In fact, for
|j|\leq k
and
j\neq k
,

(3.77)

\displaystyle\|2\theta-j\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geq

\displaystyle\|(j-k)\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}-\|2\theta-k\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}

(3.78)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle C_{0}e^{-\varepsilon_{0}|j-k|/1000}-e^{-\varepsilon_{0}|k|}

(3.79)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle C_{0}e^{-\varepsilon_{0}|k|/500}-e^{-\varepsilon_{0}|k|}

(3.80)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle e^{-\varepsilon_{0}|k|}\geq\|2\theta-k\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}},

in which we used (
3.76
) to estimate
\|(j-k)\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}
.
∎

Definition 3.2
.

Let
0=|n_{0}|\leq|n_{1}|\leq|n_{2}|\leq...
be the
\varepsilon_{0}
-resonances of
\theta
. If this sequence is infinite, we
say
\theta
is
\varepsilon_{0}
-resonant, otherwise we say it is
\varepsilon_{0}
-non-resonant. Furthermore, if
\theta
is
\varepsilon_{0}
-non-resonant with a finite sequence of resonances
0=|n_{0}|\leq|n_{1}|\leq...\leq|n_{j}|
, we let
n_{j+1}=\infty
.

Due to
\beta(\alpha)=0
, we can show the following growth of the infinite sequence of
\theta
resonances.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
\beta(\alpha)=0
, then there exists
N_{0}=N_{0}(\alpha,\varepsilon_{0})
, such that for
|n_{j}|>N_{0}
,

(3.81)

\displaystyle|n_{j+1}|>250|n_{j}|

Proof.

By (
3.76
),

(3.82)

\displaystyle e^{-\varepsilon_{0}|n_{j}|}\geq\|2\theta-n_{j}\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geq

\displaystyle\|(n_{j+1}-n_{j})\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}-\|2\theta-n_{j+1}\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}

(3.83)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle C_{0}e^{-\varepsilon_{0}|n_{j+1}-n_{j}|/1000}-e^{-\varepsilon_{0}|n_{j+1}|}

(3.84)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle C_{0}e^{-\varepsilon_{0}|n_{j+1}|/500}-e^{-\varepsilon_{0}|n_{j+1}|}

(3.85)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{C_{0}}{2}e^{-\varepsilon_{0}|n_{j+1}|/500},

which implies the desired bound.
∎

Definition 3.3

(Almost localization)
.

We say the family
\{\hat{H}_{\alpha,\theta}\}_{\theta\in\mathbb{T}}
is
(C_{1},C_{2},\varepsilon_{2})
-almost localized for some constant
C_{1},C_{2},\varepsilon_{2}>0
if for every solution
\hat{u}
of
\hat{H}_{\alpha,\theta}\hat{u}=E\hat{u}
for some energy
E\in\mathbb{R}
, satisfying
\hat{u}_{0}=1
and
|\hat{u}_{k}|\leq 1+|k|
, and for every
C_{1}(1+|n_{j}|)<|k|<C_{1}^{-1}|n_{j+1}|
, the bound
|\hat{u}_{k}|\leq C_{2}e^{-\varepsilon_{2}|k|}
holds, where the
n_{j}
’s are the
\varepsilon_{0}
-resonances of
\theta
.

We will prove the following.

Theorem 3.8
.

There exists a constant
C_{2}=C_{2}(v,d)>0
such that

\{\hat{H}_{\alpha,\theta}\}_{\theta\in\mathbb{T}}
is
(3,C_{2},\hat{L}_{d}/10)
-almost localized.

Proof.

Let
f_{E,N}(\theta)
be defined as in (
3.62
), and we omit the dependence on
E
for simplicity.
The following observation was made for the Harper operator (with Dirichlet boundary conditions) in
[
J1
]
, and extended to the long-range setting in
[
AJ2
]
.
The extension to the periodic boundary condition setting is immediate.

Fact 3.9
.

f_{N}(\theta)=Q_{dN}(\cos(2\pi(\theta+\frac{dN-1}{2}\alpha)))
, where
Q_{k}(x)
denotes a real polynomial in
x
of degree
k
.

Next we introduce of definition of uniformity, introduced in
[
J1
]
, that measures the evenness of distribution of a set in
\mathbb{T}
.

Definition 3.4

(Uniformity)
.

For some
\kappa>0
, we say that a set
\{\theta_{1},\theta_{2},...,\theta_{m+1}\}
is
\kappa
-uniform if

\displaystyle\max_{z\in[0,1]}\max_{j=1,\cdots,m+1}\Bigg|\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=1\\
\ell\neq j\end{subarray}}^{m+1}\frac{z-\cos(2\pi\theta_{\ell})}{\cos(2\pi\theta_{j})-\cos(2\pi\theta_{\ell})}\Bigg|<e^{\kappa m}.

Without loss of generality, assume
3(|n_{j}|+1)<y<|n_{j+1}|/3
and
|n_{j}|>N_{0}(\alpha,\varepsilon_{0})
. The case of negative
y
can be handled similarly. Choose
n
such that

dq_{n}\leq y/8<dq_{n+1}
and let
s
be the largest positive integer satisfying
sdq_{n}\leq y/8
.
Define intervals
I_{1},I_{2}\subset\mathbb{Z}
as follows:

\displaystyle I_{1}=[-2sdq_{n},0]\cap\mathbb{Z}\text{\ \ and\ \ }I_{2}=[y-2sdq_{n}+1,y+2sdq_{n}]\cap\mathbb{Z},\,\,

\displaystyle\mathrm{if}\ n_{j}<0

\displaystyle I_{1}=[-1,2sdq_{n}-1]\cap\mathbb{Z}\text{\ \ and\ \ }I_{2}=[y-2sdq_{n}+1,y+2sdq_{n}]\cap\mathbb{Z},\,\,

\displaystyle\mathrm{if}\ n_{j}\geq 0

The key in choosing
I_{1},I_{2}
is to guarantee that
-n_{j}\notin(I_{1}\cup I_{2})+(I_{1}\cup I_{2})
.
Let
6sq_{n}=N
whence
|I_{1}|+|I_{2}|=6dsq_{n}+1=dN+1
.

Lemma 3.10
.

The set

\left\{\theta+j\alpha-\left\lfloor\frac{dN-1}{2}\right\rfloor\alpha+\frac{dN-1}{2}\alpha\right\}_{j\in I_{1}\cup I_{2}}\subset\mathbb{T}

is
(C_{3}d\,\varepsilon_{0})
-uniform for some absolute constant
C_{3}\geq 1
and
y>y_{0}(\alpha,\varepsilon_{0},C_{\kappa_{0}})
.

For
d=1
, when
N-1
is even, this is Lemma 4.3 of
[
H
]
. In the general case, the proof requires a slight modification, which we present below.

Proof.

We treat the
n_{j}>0
case in detail and leave the other case to the reader. Note that for
y
large,
n
is also large.
Set
z=\cos(2\pi\xi)
, and denote

(3.86)

\displaystyle\tilde{\theta}_{j}:=\theta+j\alpha-\left\lfloor\frac{dN-1}{2}\right\rfloor\alpha+\frac{dN-1}{2}\alpha.

Note that since

(3.87)

\displaystyle\max_{z\in[0,1]}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in I_{1}\cup I_{2}\\
j\neq-1\end{subarray}}\left|\frac{z-\cos(2\pi\tilde{\theta}_{-1})}{\cos(2\pi\tilde{\theta}_{j})-\cos(2\pi\tilde{\theta}_{-1})}\right|

(3.88)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in I_{1}\cup I_{2}\\
j\neq-1\end{subarray}}|\cos(2\pi\tilde{\theta}_{j})-\cos(2\pi\tilde{\theta}_{-1})|^{-1}

(3.89)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in I_{1}\cup I_{2}\\
j\neq-1\end{subarray}}\left(\|\tilde{\theta}_{j}-\tilde{\theta}_{-1}\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{-1}\cdot\|\tilde{\theta}_{j}+\tilde{\theta}_{-1}\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{-1}\right)

We use (
3.76
) with
\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{0}/10
to estimate

(3.90)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\theta}_{j}-\tilde{\theta}_{-1}\|_{\mathbb{T}}=\|(j+1)\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geq C_{\varepsilon_{0}}e^{-\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{10}|j+1|}\geq C_{\varepsilon_{0}}e^{-\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{10}(y+2sdq_{n}+1)}\geq C_{\varepsilon_{0}}e^{-\frac{3}{10}\varepsilon_{0}dN},

where we used
y+2sdq_{n}\leq 18sdq_{n}=3dN
.
Regarding the term

(3.91)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\theta}_{j}+\tilde{\theta}_{-1}\|_{\mathbb{T}}=

\displaystyle\|2\theta+(j-1)\alpha-2\left\lfloor(dN-1)/2\right\rfloor\alpha+(dN-1)\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}

we distinguish into two cases.

Case 1.

Suppose

\|2\theta+(j-1)\alpha-2\left\lfloor(dN-1)/2\right\rfloor\alpha+(dN-1)\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}<e^{-\varepsilon_{0}|j-1-2\left\lfloor(dN-1)/2\right\rfloor+(dN-1)|}
.
Then by Lemma
3.6
, we have
-n_{j}<-1\leq j-1-2\left\lfloor(dN-1)/2\right\rfloor+(dN-1)=-n_{p}
for some
p\leq n-1
.
This implies

(3.92)

\displaystyle\|2\theta+(j-1)\alpha-2\left\lfloor(dN-1)/2\right\rfloor\alpha+(dN-1)\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}=

\displaystyle\|2\theta-n_{p}\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}

(3.93)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\|(n_{j}-n_{p})\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}-\|2\theta-n_{j}\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}

(3.94)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle C_{\varepsilon_{0}}e^{-\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{10}|n_{j}-n_{p}|}-e^{-\varepsilon_{0}|n_{j}|}

(3.95)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle C_{\varepsilon_{0}}e^{-\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{5}|n_{j}|}-e^{-\varepsilon_{0}|n_{j}|}

(3.96)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle C_{\varepsilon_{0}}e^{-\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{4}|n_{j}|}

(3.97)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle C_{\varepsilon_{0}}e^{-\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{4}dN},

where in the last inequality we used
|n_{j}|<y/3\leq dN
.

Case 2.

Suppose
\|2\theta+(j-1)\alpha-2\left\lfloor(dN-1)/2\right\rfloor\alpha+(dN-1)\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geq e^{-\varepsilon_{0}|j-1-2\left\lfloor(dN-1)/2\right\rfloor+(dN-1)|}
. Then
we directly bound

(3.98)

\displaystyle\|2\theta+(j-1)\alpha-2\left\lfloor(dN-1)/2\right\rfloor\alpha+(dN-1)\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geq

\displaystyle e^{-\varepsilon_{0}|j-1-2\left\lfloor(dN-1)/2\right\rfloor+(dN-1)|}

(3.99)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle e^{-3\varepsilon_{0}dN},

where we used
j-1-2\left\lfloor(dN-1)/2\right\rfloor+(dN-1)\leq y+2sdq_{n}\leq 3dN
.
Taking (
3.87
), (
3.90
), (
3.91
), (
3.92
) and (
3.98
) into account, it is enough to prove the claimed result with
I_{1}=[-1,2sdq_{n}-1]
replaced with
I_{1}=[0,2sdq_{n}-1]
.

Lemma 3.11
.

[
AJ1
]

Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\mathbb{Q}
,
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
0\leq j_{0}\leq q_{n}-1
be such that
|\sin\pi(x+j_{0}\alpha)|=\inf_{0\leq j\leq q_{n}-1}|\sin\pi(x+j\alpha)|
, then for some absolute constant
C_{4}>0
,

-C_{4}\log q_{n}\leq\sum_{0\leq j\leq q_{n}-1,j\neq j_{0}}\log|\sin\pi(x+j\alpha)|+(q_{n}-1)\log 2\leq C_{4}\log q_{n}

For any
j\in I_{1}\cup I_{2}
, it suffices to estimate

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in I_{1}\cup I_{2},\ \ell\neq j}\left(\log{|\cos(2\pi\xi)-\cos(2\pi\tilde{\theta}_{\ell})|}-\log{|\cos(2\pi\tilde{\theta}_{j})-\cos(2\pi\tilde{\theta}_{\ell})|}\right)=:\sum_{1}-\sum_{2}.

Lemma
3.11
reduces this problem to estimating the minimal terms. We first bound
\sum_{1}
:

\displaystyle\sum_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell\in I_{1}\cup I_{2},\ell\neq j}\log|\cos(2\pi\xi)-\cos(2\pi\tilde{\theta}_{\ell})|

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell\in I_{1}\cup I_{2},\ell\neq j}\log|\sin{(\pi(\xi+\tilde{\theta}_{\ell}))}|+\sum_{\ell\in I_{1}\cup I_{2},\ell\neq j}\log|\sin(\pi(\xi-\tilde{\theta}_{\ell}))|+(|I_{1}|+|I_{2}|-1)\log 2

(3.100)

\displaystyle=:\sum_{1,+}+\sum_{1,-}+dN\log 2.

Dividing
I_{1}\cup I_{2}=\bigcup_{k=1}^{6sd}T_{k}
into
6sd
small intervals of integers, each of length
q_{n}
, and denoting

(3.102)

\displaystyle|\sin(\pi(\xi+\tilde{\theta}_{\ell_{k}}))|:=\min_{\ell\in T_{k}}|\sin(\pi(\xi+\tilde{\theta}_{\ell}))|,

one has

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in I_{1}\cup I_{2},\ell\neq j}\log|\sin(\pi(\xi+\tilde{\theta}_{\ell}))|\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{6sd}\sum_{\ell\in T_{k},\ell\neq\ell_{k}}\log|\sin(\pi(\xi+\tilde{\theta}_{\ell}))|

(3.103)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 6sd(C_{4}\log q_{n}-(q_{n}-1)\log 2),

where we used Lemma
3.11
in the last inequality.
Similarly, replacing
\xi
with
-\xi
,

(3.104)

\displaystyle\sum_{1,-}\leq 6sq(C_{4}\log q_{n}-(q_{n}-1)\log 2).

Combining (
3.2
) with (
3.2
) and (
3.104
), we arrive at

\displaystyle\sum_{1}\leq

\displaystyle 12sd(C_{4}\log q_{n}-(q_{n}-1)\log 2)+dN\log 2

(3.105)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle-6sdq_{n}\log 2+\varepsilon_{0}sdq_{n},

for
n
large.
Next, we estimate
\sum_{2}
:

\displaystyle\sum_{2}=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in I_{1}\cup I_{2},\ell\neq j}\log|\cos(2\pi\tilde{\theta}_{j})-\cos(2\pi\tilde{\theta}_{\ell})|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in I_{1}\cup I_{2},\ell\neq j}\log|\sin(\pi(\tilde{\theta}_{j}+\tilde{\theta}_{\ell}))|+\sum_{\ell\in I_{1}\cup I_{2},\ell\neq j}\log|\sin(\pi(\tilde{\theta}_{j}-\tilde{\theta}_{\ell}))|+dN\log 2

(3.106)

\displaystyle=:

\displaystyle\sum_{2,+}+\sum_{2,-}+dN\log 2.

Let
T_{1}=[0,q_{n}-1]\cap\mathbb{Z}
be the left-most small interval in
I_{1}\cup I_{2}=\cup_{k=1}^{6sd}T_{k}
, and for
k\geq 1
, let
T_{k+1}
be on the right-hand side of
T_{k}
.
Then
\bigcup_{k=1}^{2sd}T_{k}=I_{1}
and
\bigcup_{k=2sd+1}^{6sd}T_{k}=I_{2}
.
We also group
T_{k}
’s into intervals of length
sq_{n}
, for
1\leq h\leq 6d
, by defining

(3.107)

\displaystyle R_{h}=\bigcup_{k=(h-1)s+1}^{hs}T_{k}.

Denote the smallest term in each
T_{k}
, for
1\leq k\leq 6sd
, by:

(3.108)

\displaystyle|\sin(\pi(\tilde{\theta}_{j}+\tilde{\theta}_{\ell_{k}^{+}}))|=\min_{\ell\in T_{k}}|\sin(\pi(\tilde{\theta}_{j}+\tilde{\theta}_{\ell}))|,

and

(3.109)

\displaystyle|\sin(\pi(\tilde{\theta}_{j}-\tilde{\theta}_{\ell_{k}^{-}}))|=\min_{\ell\in T_{k}}|\sin(\pi(\tilde{\theta}_{j}-\tilde{\theta}_{\ell}))|.

Denote the smallest term in each
R_{h}
, for
1\leq h\leq 6d
, by:

(3.110)

\displaystyle|\sin(\pi(\tilde{\theta}_{j}+\tilde{\theta}_{\tilde{\ell}_{h}^{+}}))|=\min_{\ell\in R_{h}}|\sin(\pi(\tilde{\theta}_{j}+\tilde{\theta}_{\ell}))|,

and

(3.111)

\displaystyle|\sin(\pi(\tilde{\theta}_{j}-\tilde{\theta}_{\tilde{\ell}_{h}^{-}}))|=\min_{\ell\in R_{h}}|\sin(\pi(\tilde{\theta}_{j}-\tilde{\theta}_{\ell}))|.

We now establish the following estimate for
\sum_{2,+}
.

Lemma 3.12
.

For
n
large,

(3.112)

\displaystyle\sum_{2,+}\geq-300\varepsilon_{0}d^{2}sq_{n}-6sdq_{n}\log 2.

Proof.

We bound

(3.113)

\displaystyle\sum_{2,+}=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{6sd}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\in T_{k}\\
\ell\neq j\end{subarray}}\log|\sin(\pi(\tilde{\theta}_{j}+\tilde{\theta}_{\ell}))|

(3.114)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{6sd}\sum_{\ell\in T_{k}}\log|\sin(\pi(\tilde{\theta}_{j}+\tilde{\theta}_{\ell}))|

(3.115)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{6sd}\log|\sin(\pi(\tilde{\theta}_{j}+\tilde{\theta}_{\ell_{k}^{+}}))|-(6sd)(C_{4}\log q_{n}+(q_{n}-1)\log 2)

(3.116)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{6sd}\log(\|\tilde{\theta}_{j}+\tilde{\theta}_{\ell_{k}^{+}}\|_{\mathbb{T}})-6sdq_{n}\log 2-\varepsilon_{0}sdq_{n},

for
n
large, where we used Lemma
3.11
in the second to last inequality, and
|\sin(\pi x)|\geq 2|x|
in the final one.
As far as the minimal terms at
\ell_{k}^{+}
,
1\leq k\leq 6sd
are concerned, we now establish the following result.

Lemma 3.13
.

(3.117)

\displaystyle\min_{k=1,\ldots,6sd}\;\|\tilde{\theta}_{j}+\tilde{\theta}_{\ell_{k}^{+}}\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geq e^{-36\varepsilon_{0}dsq_{n}}.

Proof.

Within the proof, for
1\leq k\leq 6sd
, let

(3.118)

\displaystyle m_{k}:=j+\ell_{k}^{+}-2\lfloor(dN-1)/2\rfloor+(dN-1)

for simplicity. Then
\tilde{\theta}_{j}+\tilde{\theta}_{\ell_{k}^{+}}=2\theta+m_{k}\alpha
.
Note that since
j,\ell_{k}^{+}\in I_{1}\cup I_{2}
, one has

(3.119)

\displaystyle-n_{j}<-3\leq m_{k}\leq 2y+4sdq_{n}\leq\begin{cases}\frac{5}{2}y<\frac{5}{6}|n_{j+1}|\\
16(s+1)dq_{n}+4sdq_{n}\leq 36sdq_{n}\end{cases}

Next, we prove Lemma
3.13
by contradiction. Suppose for some
k
that

(3.120)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\theta}_{j}+\tilde{\theta}_{\ell_{k}^{+}}\|_{\mathbb{T}}=\left\|2\theta+m_{k}\alpha\right\|_{\mathbb{T}}\leq e^{-36\varepsilon_{0}dsq_{n}}

Then by (
3.119
), we conclude that

(3.121)

\displaystyle\left\|2\theta+m_{k}\alpha\right\|_{\mathbb{T}}\leq e^{-36\varepsilon_{0}dsq_{n}}\leq e^{-\varepsilon_{0}|m_{k}|}.

Furthermore, for any
0\leq|m|\leq 6sdq_{n}
,
by (
3.119
)

(3.122)

\displaystyle|m-m_{k}|\leq 42sdq_{n}.

Hence by (
3.76
) with
\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{0}/10
, one obtains

(3.123)

\displaystyle\|2\theta+m\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geq

\displaystyle\left\|(m-m_{k})\alpha\right\|_{\mathbb{T}}-\|2\theta+m_{k}\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}

(3.124)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle C_{\varepsilon_{0}}e^{-\frac{21}{5}\varepsilon_{0}sdq_{n}}-e^{-36\varepsilon_{0}dsq_{n}}

(3.125)

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle e^{-36\varepsilon_{0}dsq_{n}}

(3.126)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\|2\theta+m_{k}\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}},

for
n
large enough.
Combining (
3.121
) with (
3.123
), we see that
(-m_{k})
must be an
\varepsilon_{0}
-resonance.

Since
3|n_{j}|<y<8(s+1)dq_{n}\leq 16sdq_{n}
, we obtain
|n_{j}|\leq 6sdq_{n}
.
Hence by (
3.123
) with
m=-n_{j}
, we infer that

(3.127)

\displaystyle\|2\theta-n_{j}\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}>\|2\theta+m_{k}\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}.

This implies
-m_{k}=n_{p}
for some
p\geq j+1
. However, this leads to a contradiction with (
3.119
) via
|m_{k}|<|n_{j+1}|
.
∎

With Lemma
3.13
, we conclude the following.

Lemma 3.14
.

For any
1\leq h\leq 6d
,

(3.128)

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell_{k}^{+}\in R_{h}}\log(\|\tilde{\theta}_{j}+\tilde{\theta_{\ell_{k}^{+}}}\|)\geq-40\varepsilon_{0}dsq_{n}.

Proof.

We divide the argument into two cases.

Case 1
. Suppose
2\|\tilde{\theta}_{j}+\tilde{\theta}_{\tilde{\ell}_{h}^{+}}\|_{\mathbb{T}}\leq|\sin(\pi(\tilde{\theta}_{j}+\tilde{\theta}_{\tilde{\ell}_{h}^{+}}))|\leq e^{-2\varepsilon_{0}q_{n}}
.
For
\ell_{k}^{+},\tilde{\ell}_{h}^{+}\in R_{h}
and
\ell_{k}^{+}\neq\tilde{\ell}_{h}^{+}
, we have
|\ell_{k}^{+}-\tilde{\ell}_{h}^{+}|\leq sq_{n}
.
Since
sdq_{n}\leq y/8<dq_{n+1}
, in particular
sq_{n}<q_{n+1}
, it follows
by (
2.20
) and (
3.76
) with
\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{0}
that

(3.129)

\displaystyle\|(\ell_{k}^{+}-\tilde{\ell}_{h}^{+})\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geq\|q_{n}\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geq C_{\varepsilon_{0}}e^{-\varepsilon_{0}q_{n}}.

This implies, by the triangle inequality, that

(3.130)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\theta}_{j}+\tilde{\theta}_{\ell_{k}^{+}}\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geq

\displaystyle\|(\ell_{k}^{+}-\tilde{\ell}_{h}^{+})\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}-\|\tilde{\theta}_{j}+\tilde{\theta}_{\tilde{\ell}_{h}^{+}}\|_{\mathbb{T}}

(3.131)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle C_{\varepsilon_{0}}e^{-\varepsilon_{0}q_{n}}-\frac{1}{2}e^{-2\varepsilon_{0}q_{n}}

(3.132)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle e^{-2\varepsilon_{0}q_{n}},

for
n
large.
Combining (
3.130
) with Lemma
3.13
, yields

(3.133)

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell_{k}^{+}\in R_{h}}\log(\|\tilde{\theta}_{j}+\tilde{\theta}_{\ell_{k}^{+}}\|_{\mathbb{T}})\geq

\displaystyle s\log 2+(s-1)\log(e^{-2\varepsilon_{0}q_{n}})+\log(e^{-36\varepsilon_{0}dsq_{n}})

(3.134)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle-40\varepsilon_{0}dsq_{n}.

Case 2
. Assume
\pi\|\tilde{\theta}_{j}+\tilde{\theta}_{\tilde{\ell}_{h}^{+}}\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geq|\sin(\pi(\tilde{\theta}_{j}+\tilde{\theta}_{\tilde{\ell}_{h}^{+}}))|>e^{-2\varepsilon_{0}q_{n}}
.
Then for any
\ell_{k}^{+}\in R_{h}
,

\pi\|\tilde{\theta}_{j}+\tilde{\theta}_{\ell_{k}^{+}}\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geq|\sin(\pi(\tilde{\theta}_{j}+\tilde{\theta}_{\ell_{k}^{+}}))|\geq e^{-2\varepsilon_{0}q_{n}}.

This implies

(3.135)

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell_{k}^{+}\in R_{h}}\log(\|\tilde{\theta}_{j}+\tilde{\theta}_{\ell_{k}^{+}}\|_{\mathbb{T}})\geq

\displaystyle s\log 2+s\log(e^{-2\varepsilon_{0}q_{n}})\geq-40\varepsilon_{0}dsq_{n}.

The claimed result follows by combining (
3.133
) with (
3.135
).
∎

Lemma
3.14
implies

(3.136)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{6sd}\log(\|\tilde{\theta}_{j}+\tilde{\theta}_{\ell_{k}^{+}}\|_{\mathbb{T}})\geq-240\varepsilon_{0}d^{2}sq_{n}.

Lemma
3.12
follows by plugging (
3.136
) into (
3.113
), whence

(3.137)

\displaystyle\sum_{2,+}\geq-300\varepsilon_{0}d^{2}sq_{n}-6dsq_{n}\log 2,

as claimed.
∎

It remains to estimate
\sum_{2,-}
.
Similar to (
3.113
), one has

(3.138)

\displaystyle\sum_{2,-}\geq\sum_{k=1}^{6sd}\log(\|\tilde{\theta}_{j}-\tilde{\theta}_{\ell_{k}^{-}}\|_{\mathbb{T}})-6sdq_{n}\log 2-\varepsilon_{0}sdq_{n}.

We will prove the following.

Lemma 3.15
.

For
n
large,

(3.139)

\displaystyle\sum_{2,-}\geq-300\varepsilon_{0}d^{2}sq_{n}-6sdq_{n}\log 2.

Proof.

We have

Lemma 3.16
.

(3.140)

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell_{k}^{-}\in R_{h},\ell_{k}^{-}\neq j}\log(\|\tilde{\theta}_{j}-\tilde{\theta}_{\ell_{k}^{-}}\|_{\mathbb{T}})\geq-40\varepsilon_{0}sdq_{n}.

Proof.

We split the argument into two different cases depending on whether or not
j\in R_{h}
.

Case 1
. If
j\in R_{h}
, then
\tilde{\ell}_{h}^{-}=j
.

For
\ell_{k}^{-}\in R_{h}
and
\ell_{k}^{-}\neq j
, we note that

0\leq|\ell_{k}^{-}-j|\leq sq_{n}<q_{n+1}
.
Hence by (
2.20
) and (
3.76
),

(3.141)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\theta}_{j}-\tilde{\theta}_{\ell_{k}^{-}}\|_{\mathbb{T}}=\|(\ell_{k}^{-}-j)\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geq\|q_{n}\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geq C_{\varepsilon_{0}}e^{-\varepsilon_{0}q_{n}}.

This implies that

(3.142)

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell_{k}^{-}\in R_{h},\ell_{k}^{-}\neq j}\log(\|\tilde{\theta}_{j}-\tilde{\theta}_{\ell_{k}^{-}}\|_{\mathbb{T}})\geq(s-1)\log(C_{\varepsilon_{0}}e^{-\varepsilon_{0}q_{n}})\geq-40dsq_{n}.

Case 2
. Assume
j\notin R_{h}
.

Case 2.1
. Assume further that

2\|\tilde{\theta}_{j}-\tilde{\theta}_{\tilde{\ell}_{h}^{-}}\|\leq|\sin(\pi(\tilde{\theta}_{j}-\tilde{\theta}_{\tilde{\ell}_{h}^{-}}))|\leq e^{-2\varepsilon_{0}q_{n}}
.

For
\ell_{k}^{-}\in R_{h}
and
\ell_{k}^{-}\neq\tilde{\ell}_{h}^{-}
,
analogously to (
3.141
) one has

(3.143)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\theta}_{j}-\tilde{\theta}_{\ell_{k}^{-}}\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geq

\displaystyle\|(\ell_{k}^{-}-\tilde{\ell}_{h}^{-})\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}-e^{-2\varepsilon_{0}q_{n}}

(3.144)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle C_{\varepsilon_{0}}e^{-\varepsilon_{0}q_{n}}-e^{-2\varepsilon_{0}q_{n}}

(3.145)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle e^{-2\varepsilon_{0}q_{n}},

for
n
large.
Also since
|j-\tilde{\ell}_{h}^{-}|\leq y+2sdq_{n}+1\leq 20dsq_{n}
, we infer by (
3.76
) that

(3.146)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\theta}_{j}-\tilde{\theta}_{\tilde{\ell}_{h}^{-}}\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geq\|(j-\tilde{\ell}_{h}^{-})\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geq C_{\varepsilon_{0}}e^{-20\varepsilon_{0}dsq_{n}}.

This implies that

(3.147)

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell_{k}^{-}\in R_{h}}\log(\|\tilde{\theta}_{j}-\tilde{\theta}_{\ell_{k}^{-}}\|_{\mathbb{T}})\geq(s-1)\log(e^{-2\varepsilon_{0}q_{n}})+\log(e^{-20\varepsilon_{0}dsq_{n}})\geq-40\varepsilon_{0}dsq_{n}.

Case 2.2
. Suppose
\pi\|\tilde{\theta}_{j}-\tilde{\theta}_{\tilde{\ell}_{h}^{-}}\|\geq|\sin(\pi(\tilde{\theta}_{j}-\tilde{\theta}_{\tilde{\ell}_{h}^{-}}))|>e^{-2\varepsilon_{0}q_{n}}
.
Thus, for any
\ell_{k}^{-}\in R_{h}
,

\pi\|\tilde{\theta}_{j}-\tilde{\theta}_{\ell_{k}^{-}}\|\geq|\sin(\pi(\tilde{\theta}_{j}-\tilde{\theta}_{\ell_{k}^{-}}))|\geq e^{-2\varepsilon_{0}q_{n}}.

This implies

(3.148)

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell_{k}^{-}\in R_{h}}\|\tilde{\theta}_{j}-\tilde{\theta}_{\ell_{k}^{-}}\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geq s\log(\pi^{-1}e^{-2\varepsilon_{0}q_{n}})>-40dsq_{n}.

Combining the cases above, we have proved Lemma
3.16
.
∎

Lemma
3.15
follows from plugging Lemma
3.16
into (
3.138
).
∎

Finally, combining (
3.2
), (
3.2
) with Lemmas
3.12
and
3.15
, we obtain

(3.149)

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell\in I_{1}\cup I_{2},\ \ell\neq j}\left(\log{|\cos(2\pi\xi)-\cos(2\pi\tilde{\theta}_{\ell})|}-\log{|\cos(2\pi\tilde{\theta}_{j})-\cos(2\pi\tilde{\theta}_{\ell})|}\right)\leq 650\varepsilon_{0}d^{2}sq_{n}.

This establishes Lemma
3.10
with
C_{3}=110
.
∎

Lemma
3.10
implies the following. Recall that
\hat{L}^{d}=\hat{L}(d\alpha,M_{E})=d\cdot\hat{L}^{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})
.

Lemma 3.17
.

If
y>y_{1}(\alpha,\varepsilon_{0},v,E)
, then there exists
j\in I_{1}\cup I_{2}
such that

(3.150)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{dN}\log\left|f_{N}\left(\theta+j\alpha-\left\lfloor\frac{dN-1}{2}\right\rfloor\alpha\right)\right|\geq\hat{L}^{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})+\log|\hat{v}_{d}|-2C_{3}d\,\varepsilon_{0}.

Proof.

Assuming the lemma is false,
let
\tilde{\theta}_{j}
be defined as in (
3.86
).
If (
3.150
) fails for all
j\in I_{1}\cup I_{2}
, then

(3.151)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{dN}\log\left|f_{N}\left(\theta+j\alpha-\left\lfloor\frac{dN-1}{2}\right\rfloor\alpha\right)\right|

(3.152)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\frac{1}{dN}\log\left|Q_{dN}(\cos(2\pi\tilde{\theta}_{j}))\right|<\hat{L}^{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})+\log|\hat{v}_{d}|-2C_{3}d\,\varepsilon_{0}.

Note that
Q_{dN}(x)
is a polynomial in
x
of degree
dN\leq dN+1=|I_{1}|+|I_{2}|
.
Hence by the Lagrange interpolation theorem, for any
x\in[0,1]
,

(3.153)

\displaystyle Q_{dN}(x)=\sum_{j\in I_{1}\cup I_{2}}Q_{dN}(\cos(2\pi\tilde{\theta}_{j}))\cdot\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in I_{1}\cup I_{2}\\
m\neq j\end{subarray}}\frac{x-\cos(2\pi\tilde{\theta}_{m})}{\cos(2\pi\tilde{\theta}_{j})-\cos(2\pi\tilde{\theta}_{m})}.

By Lemma
3.10
, we infer that

(3.154)

\displaystyle\max_{x\in[0,1]}\max_{j\in I_{1}\cup I_{2}}\left|\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in I_{1}\cup I_{2}\\
m\neq j\end{subarray}}\frac{x-\cos(2\pi\tilde{\theta}_{m})}{\cos(2\pi\tilde{\theta}_{j})-\cos(2\pi\tilde{\theta}_{m})}\right|\leq e^{C_{3}\varepsilon_{0}d^{2}N}.

Combining (
3.153
) with (
3.151
) and (
3.154
), we conclude that

(3.155)

\displaystyle\max_{\theta\in\mathbb{T}}|f_{N}(\theta)|=\max_{x\in[0,1]}|Q_{dN}(x)|\leq e^{dN(\hat{L}^{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})+\log|\hat{v}_{d}|-C_{3}d\,\varepsilon_{0})}.

However, combining Lemma
3.5
with (
2.48
), there exists
\theta
such that

(3.156)

\displaystyle|f_{N}(\theta)|\geq|\hat{v}_{d}|^{dN}e^{dN(\hat{L}^{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})-\frac{C_{3}d\,\varepsilon_{0}}{2})},

which is a contradiction.
∎

Lemma
3.17
implies the following result.

Corollary 3.18
.

If
y>y_{1}(\alpha,\varepsilon_{0},v,E)
, then there exists
j\in I_{2}
such that

(3.157)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{dN}\log\left|f_{N}\left(\theta+j\alpha-\left\lfloor\frac{dN-1}{2}\right\rfloor\alpha\right)\right|\geq\hat{L}^{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})+\log|\hat{v}_{d}|-2C_{3}d\,\varepsilon_{0}.

Proof.

It suffices to show that (
3.157
) does not hold for any
j\in I_{1}
. Thus, assume that (
3.157
) does hold for some
j_{1}\in I_{1}
.
Let

(3.158)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}x_{1}:=j_{1}-\left\lfloor\frac{dN-1}{2}\right\rfloor\\
x_{2}:=x_{1}+dN-1\end{cases}

We estimate the Green’s function,

(3.159)

\displaystyle G_{N}\left(\theta+x_{1}\alpha;-x_{1},m\right)=\frac{\mu_{N,-x_{1},m}(\theta+x_{1}\alpha)}{f_{N}(\theta+x_{1}\alpha)},

for
0\leq m\leq d-1
.
By Lemma
3.1
, and using
|\det B|=|\hat{v}_{d}|^{dN}
, one has

(3.160)

\displaystyle|\mu_{N,-x_{1},m}(\theta+x_{1}\alpha)|\leq C_{d}\|B^{-1}\|\cdot|\hat{v}_{d}|^{dN}\cdot

(3.161)

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\max\left(e^{|x_{1}|(1+\varepsilon_{0})\hat{L}^{d-1}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})+|x_{2}|(1+\varepsilon_{0})\hat{L}^{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})},e^{|x_{1}|(1+\varepsilon_{0})\hat{L}^{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})+|x_{2}|(1+\varepsilon_{0})\hat{L}^{d-1}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})}\right)

Combining this with the lower bound in (
3.157
) for
j=j_{1}
yields

(3.162)

\displaystyle|G_{N}(\theta+x_{1}\alpha;-x_{1},m)|

(3.163)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C_{d}\|B^{-1}\|\max\left(e^{-|x_{1}|\hat{L}_{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})},e^{-|x_{2}|\hat{L}_{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})}\right)\cdot e^{\varepsilon_{0}(\hat{L}^{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})+2C_{3}d)dN}.

Similarly, for any
0\leq m\leq d-1
,

(3.164)

\displaystyle|G_{N}(\theta+x_{1}\alpha;-x_{1},dN-1-m)|

(3.165)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C_{d}\|B^{-1}\|\max\left(e^{-|x_{1}|\hat{L}_{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})},e^{-|x_{2}|\hat{L}_{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})}\right)\cdot e^{\varepsilon_{0}(\hat{L}^{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})+2C_{3}d)dN}.

Using the Poisson formula (
3.67
), and bounding
|\hat{u}_{k}|\leq 1+|k|
and
\|B\|\leq C_{v}
,
\|B^{-1}\|\leq C_{v}|\hat{v}_{d}|^{-1}
, we infer that

(3.166)

\displaystyle|\hat{u}_{0}|\leq

\displaystyle C_{d,v}|\hat{v}_{d}|^{-2}\max\left(e^{-|x_{1}|\hat{L}_{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})},e^{-|x_{2}|\hat{L}_{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})}\right)\cdot

(3.167)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\cdot\max(|x_{1}|,|x_{2}|)\cdot e^{\varepsilon_{0}(\hat{L}^{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})+2C_{3}d)dN}

Clearly,

dsq_{n}\leq\min(|x_{1}|,|x_{2}|)\leq 5dsq_{n}.

Hence, we can further bound (
3.166
) by

(3.168)

\displaystyle|\hat{u}_{0}|

\displaystyle\leq C_{d,v}\,|\hat{v}_{d}|^{-2}e^{-dsq_{n}(\hat{L}_{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})-6\varepsilon_{0}\hat{L}^{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})-20C_{3}d\,\varepsilon_{0})}.

With the choice of
\varepsilon_{0}
in (
3.75
), we arrive a contradiction with the assumption that
\hat{u}_{0}=1
by sending
n\to\infty
.
∎

Corollary
3.18
implies the following bound:

(3.169)

\displaystyle|\hat{u}_{y}|\leq C_{d,v}\,|\hat{v}_{d}|^{-2}e^{-\frac{y}{8}(\hat{L}_{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})-6\varepsilon_{0}\hat{L}^{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})-20C_{3}d\,\varepsilon_{0})}\leq C_{d,v}|\hat{v}_{d}|^{-2}e^{-\frac{y}{10}\hat{L}_{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})},

which we used (
3.75
) in the last inequality.
The proof is similar to arguments in the proof of Corollary
3.18
, after replacing the role of
\hat{u}_{0}
with
\hat{u}_{y}
and using

y\geq 8dsq_{n}
.
This finishes the proof of almost localization.
∎

3.3.
Localization

Theorem
1.7
follows from Theorem
3.8
by noting that any
\theta
with
\gamma(\alpha,\theta)=0
is
\varepsilon_{0}
-nonresonant for any
\varepsilon_{0}>0
.

Remark 3.19
.

For
\alpha
satisfying a stronger Diophantine condition

(3.170)

\displaystyle\alpha\in\mathrm{DC}:=\bigcup_{c>0}\bigcup_{b>1}\left\{\alpha\in\mathbb{T}:\|n\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geq\frac{c}{|n|^{b}}\right\}

one can give another proof of localization by combining the large deviation theorem with the zero count of
\det(P_{\alpha,n}(\theta)-E)
as in
[
HS
]
. This approach will allow us to prove a non-perturbative localization for a.e.
\alpha\in\mathrm{DC}
for a general class of operators obtained by replacing the cosine potential in
\hat{H}_{\alpha,\theta}
with arbitrary analytic potentials.
We will address this problem in an upcoming work.

4.
Numerator upper bound for periodic boundary conditions

This section is devoted to the proof of Lemma
3.1
.
We consider the finite volume Hamiltonian with periodic boundary conditions. To distinguish it from
\hat{H}_{\alpha,n}(\theta)
, which satisfies Dirichlet boundary conditions, we denote the periodic Hamiltonian by
P_{\alpha,n}(\theta)
. We shall omit
\alpha
from the notation for simplicity, and denote
C(\theta+jd\alpha)-E
by
C_{j}
. Thus,

(4.171)

\displaystyle P_{n}(\theta)-E=\left(\begin{matrix}C_{n-1}&B^{*}&&&B\\
B&C_{n-2}&\ddots\\
&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots\\
&&\ddots&\ddots&B^{*}\\
B^{*}&&&B&C_{0}\end{matrix}\right).

Let
P_{n}(\theta;x,y)
be the submatrix of
P_{n}(\theta)-E
obtained by deleting the
x-
th row and
y-
th column.
We treat the case
0\leq x\leq d-1
in detail, and briefly go through the other case at the end of this section.

Case 1
. Suppose
0\leq x\leq d-1
and
3d\leq y\leq(n-1)d-1
.
We write
y=\ell d+r
for some
\ell\in[3,n-2]
and
r\in[0,d-1]
.
Let

\displaystyle R_{x,y}:=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}P_{n}(\theta)-E&{\bf e}_{dn,x}\\
\hline\cr{\bf e}_{dn,y}^{*}&0\end{array}\right),

in which
{\bf e}_{m,j}^{*}=(\delta_{j}(m-1),...,\delta_{j}(1),\delta_{j}(0))
and
\delta_{j}(k)
is the Kronecker delta.
Clearly

(4.174)

\displaystyle|\mu_{n,x,y}(\theta)|=|\det(P_{n}(\theta;x,y)-E)|=|\det R_{x,y}|

Next, we carry out elementary row operations to reduce
R_{x,y}
.
These operations do not alter
|\det R_{x,y}|
.
We have

\displaystyle\qquad R_{x,y}=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c}C_{n-1}&B^{*}&&&&&&&B&\\
\hline\cr B&\ddots&\ddots&&&&&&&\\
\hline\cr&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&&&&&&\\
\hline\cr&&\ddots&\ddots&B^{*}&&&&&\\
\hline\cr&&&B&C_{\ell}&B^{*}&&&&\\
\hline\cr&&&&B&C_{\ell-1}&\ddots&&&\\
\hline\cr&&&&&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&&\\
\hline\cr&&&&&&\ddots&\ddots&B^{*}&\\
\hline\cr B^{*}&&&&&&&B&C_{0}&{\bf e}_{d,x}\\
\hline\cr&&&&{\bf e}_{d,r}^{*}&&&&&\end{array}\right)

(4.185)

\displaystyle=:

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}\text{Row}_{1}\\
\text{Row}_{2}\\
\vdots\\
\text{Row}_{n+1}\end{matrix}\right)

In the following we also denote
M_{E,k}(\theta+jd\alpha)
by
M_{k}(j)
for simplicity.
We use the following row operations.

Row operations on
\text{Row}_{1}

Replacing the block-valued
\text{Row}_{1}
in
(
4
),

\text{Row}_{1}\longrightarrow\text{Row}_{1}-C_{n-1}\cdot B^{-1}\cdot\text{Row}_{2},

yields a new row as follows

\text{Row}_{1}^{(1)}=(0,B^{*}-C_{n-1}B^{-1}C_{n-2},-C_{n-1}B^{-1}B^{*},0,...,0,B,0).

Appealing to (
2.43
) and (
2.44
), it is easy to see that

\text{Row}_{1}^{(1)}=(0,-M_{2}^{UL}(n-2)B,\,-M_{2}^{UR}(n-2),0,...,0,B,0).

Replacing

\text{Row}_{1}^{(1)}\longrightarrow\text{Row}_{1}^{(1)}+M_{2}^{UL}(n-2)\cdot\text{Row}_{3},

yields the new row

\text{Row}_{1}^{(2)}=(0,0,-M_{2}^{UR}(n-2)+M_{2}^{UL}(n-2)C_{n-3},\,M_{2}^{UL}(n-2)B^{*},0,...,0,B,0).

Appealing to (
2.43
) and (
2.44
), it is easy to see that

\text{Row}_{1}^{(2)}=(0,0,-M_{3}^{UL}(n-3)B,\,-M_{3}^{UR}(n-3),0,...,0,B,0).

One can iterate this process, and after replacing

\text{Row}_{1}^{(n-3)}\longrightarrow\text{Row}_{1}^{(n-3)}+M_{n-2}^{UL}(2)\cdot\text{Row}_{n-1},

we arrive at

\text{Row}_{1}^{(n-2)}=(0,...,0,-M_{n-1}^{UL}(1)B,\,B-M_{n-1}^{UR}(1),0).

Row operations on
\text{Row}_{2}

Replacing

\text{Row}_{2}\longrightarrow\text{Row}_{2}-C_{n-2}\cdot B^{-1}\cdot\text{Row}_{3},

yields the following new row

(4.186)

\displaystyle\text{Row}_{2}^{(1)}=

\displaystyle(B,0,B^{*}-C_{n-2}B^{-1}C_{n-3},-C_{n-2}B^{-1}B^{*},0,...,0)

(4.187)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(B,0,-M_{2}^{UL}(n-3)B,-M_{2}^{UR}(n-3),0,...,0).

Repeating this process, and after replacing

\text{Row}_{2}^{(n-4)}\longrightarrow\text{Row}_{2}^{(n-4)}+M_{n-3}^{UL}(2)\cdot\text{Row}_{n-1},

we arrive at

(4.188)

\displaystyle\text{Row}_{2}^{(n-3)}=(B,0,...,0,-M_{n-2}^{UL}(1)B,-M_{n-2}^{UR}(1),0).

Next, we perform row reduction on
\text{Row}_{n-\ell+1}
. Recall that
3\leq\ell\leq n-2
.

Row operations on
\text{Row}_{n-\ell+1}
(the row containing
C_{\ell-1}
).

Replacing

\text{Row}_{n-\ell+1}\longrightarrow\text{Row}_{n-\ell+1}-C_{\ell-1}B^{-1}\cdot\text{Row}_{n-\ell+2},

yields a new row as follows

(4.189)

\displaystyle\text{Row}_{n-\ell+1}^{(1)}=

\displaystyle(0,...,0,B,0,B^{*}-C_{\ell-1}B^{-1}C_{\ell-2},-C_{\ell-1}B^{-1}B^{*},0,...,0)

(4.190)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(0,...,0,B,0,-M_{2}^{UL}(\ell-2)B,\,-M_{2}^{UR}(\ell-2),0,...,0).

where the matrix
B
is the
(n-\ell)-
th entry (from the left) of the block-valued vector above, and we have used (
2.43
) and (
2.44
) to obtain the second line.
Repeating this process, and after replacing

\text{Row}_{n-\ell+1}^{(\ell-3)}\longrightarrow\text{Row}_{n-\ell+1}^{(\ell-3)}+M_{\ell-2}^{UL}(2)\cdot\text{Row}_{n-1},

we arrive at

\text{Row}_{n-\ell+1}^{(\ell-2)}=(0,...,0,B,0,...,0,-M_{\ell-1}^{UL}(1)B,-M_{\ell-1}^{UR}(1),0).

At this point,
R_{x,y}
becomes

\displaystyle R^{(1)}_{x,y}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c}0&0&0&\cdots&&&&&\cdots&0&-M_{n-1}^{UL}(1)B&B-M_{n-1}^{UR}(1)&0\\
\hline\cr B&0&0&\cdots&&&&&\cdots&0&-M_{n-2}^{UL}(1)B&-M_{n-2}^{UR}(1)&0\\
\hline\cr 0&B&C_{n-3}&B^{*}&&&&&&&&&\\
\hline\cr\vdots&&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&&&&&&&&\\
\hline\cr&&&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&&&&&&&\\
\hline\cr\vdots&&&&B&C_{\ell}&B^{*}&&&&&&\\
\hline\cr 0&\cdots&&\cdots&0&B&0&0&\cdots&0&-M_{\ell-1}^{UL}(1)B&-M_{\ell-1}^{UR}(1)&0\\
\hline\cr\vdots&&&&&0&B&C_{\ell-2}&B^{*}&&&0&0\\
\hline\cr&&&&&&&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&&\vdots&\vdots\\
\hline\cr\vdots&&&&&&&&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&0&\vdots\\
\hline\cr 0&&&&&&&&&\ddots&\ddots&B^{*}&0\\
\hline\cr B^{*}&0&\cdots&&&&&&\cdots&0&B&C_{0}&{\bf e}_{d,x}\\
\hline\cr 0&\cdots&&\cdots&0&{\bf e}_{d,r}^{*}&0&\cdots&&\cdots&0&0&0\end{array}\right)

The first, second,
(n-\ell+1)
-th,
n
-th, and
(n+1)
-th rows are

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}\text{Row}_{1}^{(n-2)}\\
\text{Row}_{2}^{(n-3)}\\
\text{Row}_{n-\ell+1}^{(\ell-2)}\\
\text{Row}_{n}\\
\text{Row}_{n+1}\end{matrix}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccccccccc}0&0&\cdots&0&0&0&\cdots&0&-M_{n-1}^{UL}(1)B&B-M_{n-1}^{UR}(1)&0\\
B&0&\cdots&0&0&0&\cdots&0&-M_{n-2}^{UL}(1)B&-M_{n-2}^{UR}(1)&0\\
0&0&\cdots&0&B&0&\cdots&0&-M_{\ell-1}^{UL}(1)B&-M_{\ell-1}^{UR}(1)&0\\
B^{*}&0&\cdots&0&0&0&\cdots&0&B&C_{0}&{\bf e}_{d,x}\\
0&0&\cdots&0&{\bf e}_{d,r}^{*}&0&\cdots&0&0&0&0\end{array}\right),

in which only columns
1
,
n-\ell
,
n-1
,
n
,
n+1
are non-vanishing.
Let

\displaystyle S_{1}:=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}0&0&-M_{n-1}^{UL}(1)B&B-M_{n-1}^{UR}(1)&0\\
B&0&-M_{n-2}^{UL}(1)B&-M_{n-2}^{UR}(1)&0\\
0&B&-M_{\ell-1}^{UL}(1)B&-M_{\ell-1}^{UR}(1)&0\\
B^{*}&0&B&C_{0}&{\bf e}_{d,x}\\
0&{\bf e}^{*}_{d,r}&0&0&0\end{array}\right)

be the non-vanishing
(4d+1)\times(4d+1)
submatrix of rows
1
,
2
,
n-\ell+1
,
n
and
n+1
.
Let

\displaystyle S_{2}:=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccccccccccccccc}B&C_{n-3}&B^{*}&&&&&&&&&&&&&\\
&B&C_{n-4}&\ddots&&&&&&&&&&&&\\
&&\ddots&\ddots&&&&&&&&&&&&\\
\\
&&&&&\ddots&\ddots&&&&&&&&&\\
&&&&&\ddots&C_{\ell+2}&B^{*}&&&&&&&&\\
&&&&&&B&C_{\ell+1}&0&&&&&&&\\
&&&&&&&B&B^{*}&0&&&&&&\\
&&&&&&&&B&C_{\ell-2}&B^{*}&&&&&\\
&&&&&&&&&B&\ddots&\ddots&&&&\\
&&&&&&&&&&\ddots&&&&&\\
\\
&&&&&&&&&&&&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\\
&&&&&&&&&&&&&B&C_{3}&B^{*}\\
&&&&&&&&&&&&&&B&C_{2}\\
&&&&&&&&&&&&&&&B\end{array}\right),

which is the submatrix of
R^{(1)}_{x,y}
after deleting rows
1
,
2
,
n-\ell+1
,
n
,
n+1
and columns
1
,
n-\ell
,
n-1
,
n
,
n+1
.
Hence

(4.230)

\displaystyle|\det R^{(1)}_{x,y}|=|\det S_{1}|\cdot|\det S_{2}|=|\det B|^{n-4}\cdot|\det S_{1}|,

where we used
\det S_{2}=(\det B)^{n-4}
.
It suffices to compute
\det S_{1}
.
Expanding determinants and using that
M_{k-2}^{UL}(1)=BM_{k-1}^{LL}(1)
,
M_{k-2}^{UR}(1)=BM_{k-1}^{LR}(1)
, we have

\displaystyle|\det S_{1}|=

\displaystyle\left|\det\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}0&0&-M_{n-1}^{UL}(1)B&B-M_{n-1}^{UR}(1)&0\\
B&0&-BM_{n-1}^{LL}(1)B&-BM_{n-1}^{LR}(1)&0\\
0&B&-M_{\ell-1}^{UL}(1)B&-M_{\ell-1}^{UR}(1)&0\\
B^{*}&0&B&C_{0}&{\bf e}_{d,x}\\
0&{\bf e}^{*}_{d,r}&0&0&0\end{array}\right)\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\det B|\cdot\left|\det\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}0&0&-M_{n-1}^{UL}(1)&B-M_{n-1}^{UR}(1)&0\\
B&0&-BM_{n-1}^{LL}(1)&-BM_{n-1}^{LR}(1)&0\\
0&B&-M_{\ell-1}^{UL}(1)&-M_{\ell-1}^{UR}(1)&0\\
