Moreover,

(5.541)

\displaystyle\frac{\|g_{2^{k}N_{0},3}(\theta)g_{2^{k}N_{0},2}(\theta)\|}{\sigma_{1}(g_{2^{k}N_{0},3}(\theta))\cdot\sigma_{1}(g_{2^{k}N_{0},2}(\theta))}

\displaystyle\geq e^{-2^{k+3}N_{0}\varepsilon\hat{L}^{d}}e^{-4\tilde{C}_{0}c_{0}}\geq e^{-4N_{1}\varepsilon\hat{L}^{d}}e^{-4\tilde{C}_{0}c_{0}}=:\tilde{\varepsilon}_{2^{k}N_{0}}^{(1)}

(5.542)

\displaystyle\frac{\|g_{2^{k}N_{0},2}(\theta)\tilde{g}_{n_{0},1}(\theta)\|}{\sigma_{1}(g_{2^{k}N_{0},2}(\theta))\cdot\sigma_{1}(\tilde{g}_{n_{0},1}(\theta))}

\displaystyle\geq e^{-2N_{1}\varepsilon\hat{L}^{d}}\geq\tilde{\varepsilon}_{2^{k}N_{0}}^{(1)}

(5.543)

\displaystyle\frac{\|\tilde{g}_{n_{0},4}(\theta)g_{2^{k}N_{0},3}(\theta)\|}{\sigma_{1}(\tilde{g}_{n_{0},4}(\theta))\cdot\sigma_{1}(g_{2^{k}N_{0},3}(\theta))}

\displaystyle\geq e^{-2N_{1}\varepsilon\hat{L}^{d}}\geq\tilde{\varepsilon}_{2^{k}N_{0}}^{(1)},

where we used
2^{k+1}N_{0}\leq N_{1}
in the first inequality.
Clearly,

(5.544)

\displaystyle\tilde{\kappa}_{2^{k}N_{0}}^{(1)}\cdot(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{2^{k}N_{0}}^{(1)})^{-2}<c_{0}.

Therefore, Theorem
2.4
implies that

(5.545)

\displaystyle\log\|\tilde{g}_{n_{0},4}(\theta)g_{2^{k}N_{0},3}(\theta)g_{2^{k}N_{0},2}(\theta)\tilde{g}_{n_{0},1}(\theta)\|

(5.546)

\displaystyle\qquad\geq\log\|\tilde{g}_{n_{0},4}(\theta)g_{2^{k}N_{0},3}(\theta)\|+\log\|g_{2^{k}N_{0},3}(\theta)g_{2^{k}N_{0},2}(\theta)\|+\log\|g_{2^{k}N_{0},2}(\theta)\tilde{g}_{n_{0},1}(\theta)\|

(5.547)

\displaystyle\qquad-\log\|g_{2^{k}N_{0},3}(\theta)\|-\log\|g_{2^{k}N_{0},2}(\theta)\|-4\tilde{C}_{0}\tilde{\kappa}_{2^{k}N_{0}}^{(1)}\cdot(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{2^{k}N_{0}}^{(1)})^{-2}

(5.548)

\displaystyle\qquad\geq 2N_{1}(1-\varepsilon)\hat{L}^{d}-2^{k+3}N_{0}\varepsilon\hat{L}^{d}-8\tilde{C}_{0}c_{0}

(5.549)

\displaystyle\qquad\geq 2N_{1}(1-3\varepsilon)\hat{L}^{d}-8\tilde{C}_{0}c_{0}.

Finally, to estimate the measure, recall
n_{0}\geq 2^{k}N_{0}
and
N_{1}\geq 2^{k+1}N_{0}
whence

(5.550)

\displaystyle\mathrm{mes}(\mathcal{G}_{2^{k}N_{0}}^{(1)})\geq

\displaystyle\mathrm{mes}(\mathcal{G}_{2^{k-1}N_{0}}^{(1)})-2e^{-c_{1}\varepsilon^{2}n_{0}}-2e^{-c_{1}\varepsilon^{2}N_{1}}

(5.551)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 1-5\sum_{\ell=0}^{k-1}e^{-2^{\ell}c_{1}\varepsilon^{2}N_{0}\hat{L}^{d}}-2e^{-2^{k}c_{1}\varepsilon^{2}N_{0}\hat{L}^{d}}-2e^{-c_{1}\varepsilon^{2}n_{0}\hat{L}^{d}}-2e^{-c_{1}\varepsilon^{2}N_{1}\hat{L}^{d}}

(5.552)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 1-5\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}e^{-2^{\ell}c_{1}\varepsilon^{2}N_{0}\hat{L}^{d}}>\frac{19}{20},

where we used (
5.511
) with
m=k
and (
5.429
).
This proves the claimed result.
∎

As a corollary of Lemma
5.3
, we now establish

Corollary 5.9
.

Under the same conditions as in Lemma
5.3
, for large enough
\kappa_{0}
-admissible
n
, with
n\geq 8N_{0}
, and for any
\theta\in(\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{d}_{n,E,\varepsilon})^{c}
, we have that

(5.553)

\displaystyle|\langle v_{1,n}(\theta)\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{d,n}(\theta),w_{1,n}(\theta)\wedge\cdots\wedge w_{d,n}(\theta)\rangle|=|\det(\langle v_{j,n}(\theta),w_{k,n}(\theta)\rangle)_{1\leq j,k\leq d}|\geq e^{-6n\varepsilon\cdot\hat{L}^{d}}.

Proof.

Towards a contradiction, suppose that for some
\theta\in(\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{d}_{n,E,\varepsilon})^{c}
one has

(5.554)

\displaystyle|\langle v_{1,n}(\theta)\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{d,n}(\theta),w_{1,n}(\theta)\wedge\cdots\wedge w_{d,n}(\theta)\rangle|

(5.555)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=|\det(\langle v_{j,n}(\theta),w_{k,n}(\theta)\rangle)_{1\leq j,k\leq d}|\leq e^{-6n\varepsilon\cdot\hat{L}^{d}}.

We expand

(5.556)

\displaystyle w_{1,n}(\theta)\wedge\cdots\wedge w_{d,n}(\theta)=\sum_{1\leq j_{1}<...<j_{d}\leq 2d}a_{j_{1},...,j_{d}}(\theta)\cdot v_{j_{1},n}(\theta)\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{j_{d},n}(\theta),

in which

(5.557)

\displaystyle a_{j_{1},...,j_{d}}(\theta)=\langle w_{1,n}(\theta)\wedge\cdots\wedge w_{d,n}(\theta),v_{j_{1},n}(\theta)\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{j_{d},n}(\theta)\rangle.

For arbitrary
1\leq j_{1}<...<j_{d}\leq 2d
, there is the trivial bound

(5.558)

\displaystyle|a_{j_{1},...,j_{d}}(\theta)|\leq 1.

By assumption (
5.554
), one has

(5.559)

\displaystyle|a_{1,...,d}(\theta)|\leq e^{-6n\varepsilon\cdot\hat{L}^{d}}.

We also require the following two uniform upper bounds.

Lemma 5.10
.

For
n
large enough, and for any
1\leq k\leq 2d-1
,
k\neq d
, one has that uniformly in
\theta\in\mathbb{T}
,

(5.560)

\displaystyle\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\sigma_{j_{\ell}}(M_{n,E}(\theta))\leq e^{n\cdot(1+\varepsilon)\hat{L}^{d-1}}.

Here
\varepsilon>0
is arbitrary but fixed.

Proof.

Clearly, by Lemma
2.1
,

(5.561)

\displaystyle\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\sigma_{j_{\ell}}(M_{n,E}(\theta))\leq\prod_{j=1}^{k}\sigma_{j}(M_{n,E}(\theta))\leq e^{n(1+\varepsilon)\cdot\hat{L}^{k}}\leq e^{n(1+\varepsilon)\cdot\hat{L}^{d-1}},

where we used that for any
k\neq d
,
\hat{L}^{k}\leq\hat{L}^{d-1}
due to
\hat{L}_{d}>0
and
\hat{L}_{d+1}<0
.
∎

Lemma 5.11
.

For
(j_{1},...,j_{d})\neq(1,...,d)
, and for
n
large enough,

(5.562)

\displaystyle\prod_{\ell=1}^{d}\sigma_{j_{\ell}}(M_{n,E}(\theta))\leq e^{n(1+\varepsilon)\cdot\hat{L}^{d-1}}.

uniformly in
\theta\in\mathbb{T}
.

Proof.

If
\sigma_{j_{d}}(M_{n,E}(\theta))<1
, then

(5.563)

\displaystyle\prod_{\ell=1}^{d}\sigma_{j_{\ell}}(M_{n,E}(\theta))<\prod_{\ell=1}^{d-1}\sigma_{j_{\ell}}(M_{n,E}(\theta))\leq e^{n(1+\varepsilon)\cdot\hat{L}^{d-1}},

by Lemma
5.10
. On the other hand,
if
\sigma_{j_{d}}(M_{n,E}(\theta))\geq 1
, then

(5.564)

\displaystyle\prod_{\ell=1}^{d}\sigma_{j_{\ell}}(M_{n,E}(\theta))\leq\prod_{\ell=1}^{j_{d}}\sigma_{\ell}(M_{n,E}(\theta))\leq e^{n(1+\varepsilon)\cdot\hat{L}^{d-1}},

where we used
j_{d}>d
and Lemma
5.10
.
∎

By Lemmas
2.1
,
2.2
,
5.11
and estimates (
5.558
) and (
5.554
), one has for
n
large enough,

(5.565)

\displaystyle\|(\wedge^{d}M_{n,E}^{2}(\theta))(v_{1,n}(\theta)\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{d,n}(\theta))\|

(5.566)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{d}\sigma_{j}(M_{n,E}(\theta))\|(\wedge^{d}M_{n,E}(\theta)(w_{1,n}(\theta))\wedge\cdots\wedge w_{d,n}(\theta))\|

(5.567)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle e^{n(1+\varepsilon)\cdot\hat{L}^{d}}\sum_{1\leq j_{1}<...<j_{d}\leq 2d}|a_{j_{1},...,j_{d}}(\theta)|\prod_{\ell=1}^{d}\sigma_{j_{\ell}}(M_{n,E}(\theta))

(5.568)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{n(1+\varepsilon)\cdot\hat{L}^{d}}\cdot\left(|a_{1,...,d}(\theta)|\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{d}\sigma_{j}(M_{n,E}(\theta))+\sum_{(j_{1},...,j_{d})\neq(1,...,d)}|a_{j_{1},...,j_{d}}(\theta)|\cdot\prod_{\ell=1}^{d}\sigma_{j_{\ell}}(M_{n,E}(\theta))\right)

(5.569)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle e^{n(1+\varepsilon)\cdot\hat{L}^{d}}\cdot\left(e^{n(1-5\varepsilon)\cdot\hat{L}^{d}}+C_{d}\cdot e^{n(1+\varepsilon)\cdot\hat{L}^{d-1}}\right)

(5.570)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle e^{2n(1-2\varepsilon)\cdot\hat{L}^{d}},

where we used (
5.421
).
This leads to a contradiction with
\theta\in(\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{d}_{n,E,\varepsilon})^{c}
.
∎

Combining Corollary
5.9
with (
5.426
), we infer the following result.

Corollary 5.12
.

Under the same conditions as in Lemma
5.3
, for large enough
\kappa_{0}
-admissible
n
, with
n\geq 8N_{0}
, and for any
\theta\in(\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{d}_{n,E,\varepsilon})^{c}\cap(\mathcal{B}_{n,E,\varepsilon}^{d})^{c}
, one has

(5.571)

\displaystyle|\langle v_{d+1,n}(\theta)\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{2d,n}(\theta),w_{d+1,n}(\theta)\wedge\cdots\wedge w_{2d,n}(\theta)\rangle|

(5.572)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=|\det(\langle v_{d+j,n}(\theta),w_{d+k,n}(\theta)\rangle)_{1\leq j,k\leq d}|\geq e^{-6n\varepsilon\cdot\hat{L}^{d}}.

The goal of the remainder of this section is to prove the following lemma.

Lemma 5.13
.

Under the same conditions as in Lemma
5.3
, for large enough
\kappa_{0}
-admissible
n
, with
n\geq 8N_{0}
, for any
\theta\in(\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{d}_{n,E,\varepsilon})^{c}\cap(\mathcal{B}_{n,E,\varepsilon}^{d})^{c}
, one has

(5.573)

\displaystyle|\det(M_{n,E}(\theta)-I_{2d})|\geq e^{n(1-8\varepsilon)\cdot\hat{L}^{d}}.

Proof.

Let
U_{v,n}(\theta)=(v_{1,n}(\theta),...,v_{2d,n}(\theta))
and
U_{w,n}(\theta)=(w_{1,n}(\theta),...,w_{2d,n}(\theta))
,
and

D_{n}(\theta)=\mathrm{diag}(\sigma_{1}(M_{n,E}(\theta)),...,\sigma_{2d}(M_{n,E}(\theta)))

The singular value decomposition
M_{n,E}(\theta)U_{v,n}(\theta)=U_{w,n}(\theta)D_{n}(\theta)
yields

(5.574)

\displaystyle\det(M_{n,E}(\theta)-I_{2d})=\det(D_{n}(\theta)-U_{w,n}^{*}(\theta)U_{v,n}(\theta)).

Note that

(5.575)

\displaystyle U_{w,n}^{*}(\theta)U_{v,n}(\theta)=\left(\begin{matrix}w_{1,n}^{*}(\theta)\\
\vdots\\
w_{2d,n}^{*}(\theta)\end{matrix}\right)\cdot(v_{1,n}(\theta),...,v_{2d,n}(\theta))

\displaystyle=\left(\langle w_{j,n}(\theta),v_{k,n}(\theta)\rangle\right)_{1\leq j,k\leq 2d}=:Q_{n}(\theta).

Hence,

(5.576)

\displaystyle|\det(D_{n}(\theta)-U_{w,n}^{*}(\theta)U_{v,n}(\theta))|

(5.577)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|\det(-Q_{n}(\theta))|

(5.578)

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=1}^{2d}\,\,\sum_{1\leq j_{1}<...<j_{k}\leq 2d}\,\,\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\sigma_{j_{\ell}}(M_{n,E}(\theta))\cdot\left|\det(Q_{n}(\theta))_{\{1,...,2d\}\setminus\{j_{1},...,j_{k}\},\{1,...,2d\}\setminus\{j_{1},...,j_{k}\}}\right|,

recall that for a matrix
M
,
M_{B_{1},B_{2}}
refers to the submatrix with row numbers in the set
B_{1}
and column numbers taken from the set
B_{2}
.
By Hadamard’s inequality,

(5.579)

\displaystyle|\det Q_{n}(\theta)|\leq\prod_{\ell=1}^{2d}\|(Q_{n}(\theta))_{\ell}\|\leq 1,

and

(5.580)

\displaystyle\left|\det(Q_{n}(\theta))_{\{1,...,2d\}\setminus\{j_{1},...,j_{k}\},\{1,...,2d\}\setminus\{j_{1},...,j_{k}\}}\right|\leq\prod_{\ell\notin\{j_{1},..,j_{k}\}}\|(Q_{n}(\theta))_{\ell}\|\leq 1

where
(Q_{n}(\theta))_{\ell}
refers to the
\ell
-th column of
Q_{n}(\theta)
. Moreover, we used the following bound

(5.581)

\displaystyle\|(Q_{n}(\theta))_{\ell}\|\leq\|v_{\ell,n}(\theta)\|=1.

Corollary
5.12
implies that

(5.582)

\displaystyle\left|\det(Q_{n}(\theta))_{\{d+1,...,2d\},\{d+1,...,2d\}}\right|

(5.583)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\langle w_{d+1,n}(\theta)\wedge\cdots\wedge w_{2d,n}(\theta),v_{d+1,n}(\theta)\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{2d,n}(\theta)\rangle\right|\geq e^{-6n\varepsilon\cdot\hat{L}^{d}}.

Combining the estimates (
5.579
), (
5.580
), (
5.582
) with (
5.576
), and using Lemmas
5.10
,
5.11
and Corollary
5.12
, we infer that for
\theta\in(\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{n,E,\varepsilon}^{d})^{c}\cap(\mathcal{B}^{d}_{n,E,\varepsilon})^{c}

(5.584)

\displaystyle|\det(M_{n}(\theta)-I_{2d})|\geq

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{d}\sigma_{j}(M_{n}(\theta))\cdot e^{-6n\varepsilon\cdot\hat{L}^{d}}-C_{d}\,\sup_{k=0,\ldots,2d}\,\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j_{1}<...<j_{k}\leq 2d\\
(j_{1},...,j_{k})\neq(1,...,d)\end{subarray}}\,\prod_{\ell=1}^{k}\sigma_{j_{\ell}}(M_{n,E}(\theta))

(5.585)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle e^{n(1-\varepsilon)\cdot\hat{L}^{d}}\cdot e^{-6n\varepsilon\cdot\hat{L}^{d}}-C_{d}\,e^{n(1+\varepsilon)\cdot\hat{L}^{d-1}}

(5.586)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle e^{n(1-8\varepsilon)\cdot(\hat{L}^{d}-\varepsilon)},

where we used (
5.421
).
This is the claimed result.
∎

Alternatively, Lemma
5.13
can also be proved via Schur complements.
Lemma
3.2
follows immediately from combining Lemmas
5.1
and
5.13
with the measure estimates of
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{d}_{n,E,\varepsilon}
in
Lemma
5.3
and
\mathcal{B}^{d}_{n,E,\varepsilon}
in Lemma
2.2
. ∎

5.1.
Proof of Lemma
3.5

Let
z=e^{2\pi i\theta}
and
F_{E,n}(z)=f_{E,n}(\theta)
, and
g_{E,n}(z)=z^{nd}F_{E,n}(z)
.
It is easy to check that
|g_{E,n}(0)|=1
.
Let
\mathcal{C}
be the unit circle.
In the following, we use Herman’s subharmonic argument:

(5.587)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{nd}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\log|f_{E,n}(\theta)|\,\mathrm{d}\theta=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{nd}\int_{\mathcal{C}}\log|F_{E,n}(z)|\,\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{2\pi iz}

(5.588)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{nd}\int_{\mathcal{C}}\log|z^{nd}F_{E,n}(z)|\,\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{2\pi iz}

(5.589)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{nd}\int_{\mathcal{C}}\log|g_{E,n}(z)|\,\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{2\pi iz}

(5.590)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{nd}\log|g_{E,n}(0)|=0.

It remains to recall that by Haro-Puig’s result, see Theorem
1.8
, for
E\in\sum_{\alpha,0}
in the subcritical regime,

(5.591)

\displaystyle 0=L(\alpha,A_{E})=\hat{L}^{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})+\log|\hat{v}_{d}|.

This proves Lemma
3.5
. ∎

6.
Non-selfadjoint Haro-Puig formula

6.1.
Proof of Corollary
1.12

We shall write
\hat{L}_{j}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})
as
\hat{L}_{j}(\varepsilon)
for simplicity.
By Theorem
1.10
and (
2.49
) one has that

(6.592)

\displaystyle L(\alpha,A_{E,\varepsilon})=

\displaystyle\sum_{j:\hat{L}_{j}(\varepsilon)\geq 0}\hat{L}_{j}(\varepsilon)+\log|\hat{v}_{-d}e^{2\pi d\varepsilon}|

(6.593)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j:\hat{L}_{j}(0)\geq 2\pi\varepsilon}(\hat{L}_{j}(0)-2\pi\varepsilon)+\log|\hat{v}_{-d}|+2\pi d\varepsilon

(6.594)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle L(\alpha,A_{E,0})-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq d\\
0\leq\hat{L}_{j}(0)<2\pi\varepsilon\end{subarray}}\hat{L}_{j}(0)+2\pi\varepsilon\cdot\#\{j:1\leq j\leq d,\,0\leq\hat{L}_{j}<2\pi\varepsilon\}

(6.595)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle L(\alpha,A_{E,0})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq d\\
0\leq\hat{L}_{j}(0)<2\pi\varepsilon\end{subarray}}(2\pi d\varepsilon-\hat{L}_{j}(0))

This can be further be simplified in the form:

(6.596)

\displaystyle L(\alpha,A_{E,\varepsilon})=

\displaystyle L(\alpha,A_{E,0})+\int_{0}^{2\pi\varepsilon}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq d\\
0\leq\hat{L}_{j}(0)<2\pi\varepsilon\end{subarray}}\chi_{[\hat{L}_{j}(0),2\pi\varepsilon]}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y

(6.597)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle L(\alpha,A_{E,0})+\int_{0}^{2\pi\varepsilon}\#\{j:1\leq j\leq d,\,0\leq\hat{L}_{j}(0)<y\}\,\mathrm{d}y.

This implies

(6.598)

\displaystyle\kappa(\alpha,A_{E,\varepsilon})=\#\{j:1\leq j\leq d,\,0\leq\hat{L}_{j}(0)\leq 2\pi d\varepsilon\},

whence, in particular,

(6.599)

\displaystyle\kappa(\alpha,A_{E,0})=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\#\{1\leq j\leq 2d\;:\;\hat{L}_{j}(0)=0\}.

This is the claimed result. ∎

The remainder of this section consists of a proof of Theorem
1.10
.

6.2.
Preparation

In the following, let
(\alpha,A)\in\mathbb{T}\times C^{\omega}(\mathbb{T},\mathrm{Mat}(d,\mathbb{C}))
be an analytic cocycle.
The following lemma and theorems from
[
AJS
]
play an important role in our proof. All fractions
p/q
are reduced (in lowest terms), and we may assume in addition that
d|q
(we will need this at some point below). Indeed, if
|\frac{p_{n}}{q_{n}}-\alpha|\to 0
as
n\to\infty
for an irrational
\alpha
, then also
|\frac{dp_{n}+1}{dq_{n}}-\alpha|\to 0
. No prime dividing
d
can divide
dp_{n}+1
, so the denominator remains divisible by
d
after removing common factors.

Definition 6.1
.

[
AJS
]

We say
(\alpha,A)
is
k
-regular if
t\to L^{k}(\alpha,A(\cdot+it))
is an affine function of
t
in a neighborhood of
0
.

We will only use the definition of
1
-regular for the cocycle
(\alpha,A_{E,\varepsilon})\in\mathbb{T}\times C^{\omega}(\mathbb{T},\mathrm{Mat}(2,\mathbb{C}))
.

Lemma 6.1
.

[
AJS
]
*Lemma 5.1
If
L^{k}(\alpha,A)>-\infty
, then for arbitrary small
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and
p/q
sufficiently close to
\alpha
, one has uniformly for all
\theta
that

(6.600)

\displaystyle L^{k}(p/q,A,\theta)\leq L^{k}(\alpha,A)+\varepsilon_{0}.

Theorem 6.2
.

[
AJS
]
*Theorem 5.2
Assume
L^{k}(\alpha,A)>-\infty
and that
(\alpha,A)
is
k
-regular. Then for arbitrary small
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and
p/q
sufficiently close to
\alpha
, one has uniformly for all
\theta\in\mathbb{T}
,

(6.601)

\displaystyle|L^{k}(p/q,A,\theta)-L^{k}(\alpha,A)|\leq\varepsilon_{0}.

Theorem 6.3
.

[
AJS
]
*Theorem 1.5
The functions
(\mathbb{T},C^{\omega}(\mathbb{T},\mathrm{Mat}(d,\mathbb{C})))\ni(\alpha,A)\to L_{k}(\alpha,A)\in[-\infty,\infty)
are continuous at any
(\alpha^{\prime},A^{\prime})
with
\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}
.

The case of
L_{k}=-\infty
does not arise here.
Since there are only finitely many
\varepsilon
’s such that
(\alpha,A_{E,\varepsilon})
is
not

1
-regular, it suffices to prove Theorem
1.10
for any
\varepsilon
at which
(\alpha,A_{E,\varepsilon})
is
1
-regular.
Also, due to (
2.49
), there are only finitely many values of
\varepsilon
for which some of the exponents
\hat{L}_{j}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})
vanish. Hence it suffices to assume that
none
of the
\hat{L}_{E}^{\varepsilon}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})
are zero. The identity (
1.17
) for arbitrary
\varepsilon
will then follow from continuity in
\varepsilon
by Theorem
6.3
.
We will thus make the following assumption below.

Assumption 6.4
.

Let
\varepsilon
be such that
(\alpha,A_{E,\varepsilon})
is
1
-regular.

6.3.
Proof of Theorem
1.10

The goal is to prove the following rational frequency
\alpha=p/q
version of Theorem
1.10
.
Let
k_{0}\in\{1,...,2d\}
be such that

(6.602)

\displaystyle\hat{L}_{1}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})\geq\cdots\geq\hat{L}_{k_{0}}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})>0>\hat{L}_{k_{0}+1}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})\geq\cdots\geq\hat{L}_{2d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon}),

assuming that
\hat{L}_{1}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})>0
. We allow for all exponents to be positive here. We will deal with the remaining case
\hat{L}_{1}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})<0
at the end of this section (the vanishing case can be ignored, see above).

Lemma 6.5
.

For any
\varepsilon
such that
(\alpha,A_{E}^{\varepsilon})
is
1
-regular and for arbitrary small
\varepsilon_{0}
such that

0<\varepsilon_{0}<\frac{1}{10}\min(\hat{L}_{k_{0}}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon}),-\hat{L}_{k_{0}+1}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})),

the following holds for some constant
C>0
:

(6.603)

\displaystyle\big|L^{1}(\alpha,A_{E,\varepsilon})-\hat{L}^{k_{0}}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})-\log|\hat{v}_{d}e^{2\pi d\varepsilon}|\big|\leq C\varepsilon_{0}\left(L^{k_{0}}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})+\big|\log|\hat{v}_{-d}e^{2\pi d\varepsilon}|\big|\right).

Clearly, Theorem
1.10
follows from taking
\varepsilon_{0}\to 0^{+}
in Lemma
6.5
.
To prove Lemma
6.5
, we consider the following three periodic operators acting on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{q})
:

(6.604)

\displaystyle(H_{p/q,\theta,\varepsilon}u)_{[n]}

\displaystyle=u_{[n+1]}+u_{[n-1]}+v(\theta+i\varepsilon+np/q)u_{[n]},

(6.605)

\displaystyle(\tilde{H}_{p/q,\theta,\varepsilon}u)_{[n]}

\displaystyle=e^{2\pi i\theta}u_{[n+1]}+e^{-2\pi i\theta}u_{[n-1]}+v(np/q+i\varepsilon)u_{[n]},

and

(6.606)

\displaystyle(\hat{H}_{p/q,\theta,\varepsilon}\phi)_{[n]}=\sum_{|k|=1}^{d}\hat{v}_{k}e^{-2\pi k\varepsilon}\phi_{[n-k]}+2\cos(2\pi(\theta+np/q))\phi_{[n]}.

It is clear that we have

Fact 6.6
.

H_{p/q,0,\varepsilon}=\tilde{H}_{p/q,0,\varepsilon}
.

We will work with the Fourier transform on the cyclic group
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
. We write the characters on this finite Abelian group in the form
\chi_{m}(n)=e^{-2\pi imnp/q}
, Haar measure being counting measure. Note that
np
for
1\leq n\leq q
is just a relabeling of the congruence classes modulo
q
. The Fourier transform is thus defined in the form

(6.607)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{F}\phi)_{[n]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{q}}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}}e^{2\pi imnp/q}\phi_{[m]},

(6.608)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{F}^{-1}u)_{[n]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{q}}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}}e^{-2\pi imnp/q}u_{[m]},

and gives a unitary transformation on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{q})
.
Lemma
6.7
establishes Aubry duality for the periodic operators by means of this Fourier transform.

Lemma 6.7
.

\mathcal{F}\tilde{H}_{p/q,\theta,\varepsilon}\mathcal{F}^{-1}=\hat{H}_{p/q,\theta,\varepsilon}.

Proof.

For simplicity, we write
\hat{H}_{p/q,\theta,\varepsilon}
as
\hat{H}
. Then

(6.609)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{F}\hat{H}\mathcal{F}^{-1}u)_{[n]}

(6.610)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{q}}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}}e^{2\pi imnp/q}(\hat{H}\mathcal{F}^{-1}u)_{[m]}

(6.611)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{q}}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}}e^{2\pi imnp/q}\left(\sum_{|k|=1}^{d}\hat{v}_{k}e^{-2\pi k\varepsilon}(\mathcal{F}^{-1}u)_{[m-k]}+2\cos(2\pi(\theta+mp/q))(\mathcal{F}^{-1}u)_{[m]}\right)

(6.612)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{q}\sum_{m,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}}\sum_{|k|=1}^{d}e^{2\pi imnp/q}e^{-2\pi i\ell(m-k)p/q}\,\hat{v}_{k}\,e^{-2\pi k\varepsilon}u_{[\ell]}

(6.613)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{q}\sum_{m,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{q}}e^{2\pi imnp/q}e^{-2\pi im\ell p/q}(e^{2\pi i(\theta+mp/q)}+e^{-2\pi i(\theta+mp/q)})u_{[\ell]}

(6.614)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{|k|=1}^{d}\hat{v}_{k}e^{-2\pi k\varepsilon}e^{2\pi inkp/q}u_{[n]}+e^{2\pi i\theta}u_{[n+1]}+e^{-2\pi i\theta}u_{[n-1]}

(6.615)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle v(np/q+i\varepsilon)u_{[n]}+e^{2\pi i\theta}u_{[n+1]}+e^{-2\pi i\theta}u_{[n-1]}

(6.616)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\tilde{H}_{p/q,\theta,\varepsilon}u)_{[n]},

as claimed.
∎

For
E\in\mathbb{C}
, let
D_{p/q,E,\varepsilon}(\theta):=\det(H_{p/q,\theta,\varepsilon}-E)
and define
\tilde{D}
,
\hat{D}
analogously for the operators
\tilde{H}
and
\hat{H}
, respectively. The Aubry duality
Lemma
6.7
implies the following invariance property.

Corollary 6.8
.

For any
E\in\mathbb{C}
and any
\theta\in\mathbb{T}
, we have

(6.617)

\displaystyle\hat{D}_{p/q,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)=\tilde{D}_{p/q,E,\varepsilon}(\theta).

By inspection we have

Fact 6.9
.

For any
E\in\mathbb{C}
,
D_{p/q,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)
is a
(p/q)
-periodic (hence
(1/q)
-periodic) function in
\theta
. Analogous results hold for
\tilde{D}
and
\hat{D}
.

The following Chamber’s type formula is an easy consequence of Fact
6.9
.

Lemma 6.10
.

For any
E\in\mathbb{C}
, we have

(6.618)

\displaystyle\hat{D}_{p/q,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)=\tilde{D}_{p/q,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)=a(p/q,E,\varepsilon)+2(-1)^{q+1}\cos(2\pi q\theta),

where
a(p/q,E,\varepsilon)
is independent of
\theta
.
This, combined with Fact
6.6
implies that

(6.619)

\displaystyle\hat{D}_{p/q,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)=\tilde{D}_{p/q,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)=D_{p/q,E,\varepsilon}(0)+(-1)^{q+1}(2\cos(2\pi q\theta)-2).

Proof.

The
\hat{D}=\tilde{D}
part is due to Corollary
6.8
.
The term of highest degree (in
e^{2\pi i\theta}
) in
\tilde{D}_{p/q,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)
equals
(-1)^{q+1}e^{2\pi iq\theta}
, and that of lowest degree is
(-1)^{q+1}e^{-2\pi iq\theta}
.
The lemma now follows from Fact
6.9
.
∎

Combining (
6.619
), Corollary
6.8
with Jensen’s formula implies the following.

Lemma 6.11
.

Denote the two zeros of

z^{2}+((-1)^{q+1}D_{p/q,E,\varepsilon}(0)-2)z+1=0

by
z_{1},z_{2}
, respectively. Then
for any
E\in\mathbb{C}
,

(6.620)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}\log|\hat{D}_{p/q,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|\,\mathrm{d}\theta=\log\max(|z_{1}|,|z_{2}|,1).

Next, we compare the right-hand side of (
6.620
) to
qL^{1}(\alpha,A_{E,\varepsilon})
for all large
q
. Note that
A_{E,\varepsilon}\in\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
implies that
L^{1}(\alpha,A_{E,\varepsilon})\geq 0
.

Lemma 6.12
.

If
(\alpha,A_{E,\varepsilon})
is
1
-regular, for some constant
C>0
, we have

(6.621)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{q}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\log|\hat{D}_{p/q,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|\,\mathrm{d}\theta-L^{1}(\alpha,A_{E,\varepsilon})\right|\leq C\varepsilon_{0}

provided
q
is large.

Proof.

First, simple considerations yield

(6.622)

\displaystyle\left|\max(|z_{1}|,|z_{2}|)-|D_{p/q,E,\varepsilon}(0)|\right|\leq 3.

Hence by Lemma
6.11
, we have

(6.623)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{q}\log\left(\max(|D_{p/q,E,\varepsilon}(0)|-3,1\right)\leq\frac{1}{q}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\log|\hat{D}_{p/q,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|\,\mathrm{d}\theta\leq\frac{1}{q}\log(|D_{p/q,E,\varepsilon}(0)|+3).

Next, we relate
D_{p/q,E,\varepsilon}(0)
to
L^{1}(\alpha,A_{E,\varepsilon})
.
Let

|\lambda_{1,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|\geq|\lambda_{1,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|^{-1}=|\lambda_{2,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|

be the eigenvalues of

A_{q,E,\varepsilon}(p/q,\theta)
.
Applying (
2.26
) to
A=A_{E,\varepsilon}
, we have

(6.624)

\displaystyle L^{1}(p/q,A_{E,\varepsilon},\theta)=\frac{1}{q}\log|\lambda_{1,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|.

The following lemma relates
D_{p/q,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)
to
A_{q,E,\varepsilon}(p/q,\theta)
.

Lemma 6.13
.

We have for all
\theta\in\mathbb{T}

(6.625)

\displaystyle|D_{p/q,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|=|\det(A_{q,E,\varepsilon}(p/q,\theta)-I_{2})|=|\lambda_{1,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)-1|\cdot|\lambda_{2,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)-1|.

Proof.

The proof is very similar to that of Lemma
5.1
.
As in the previous sections, we perform elementary row operations (which leave the determinant invariant), on the
q\times q
matrix

(6.626)

\displaystyle H_{p/q,\theta,\varepsilon}-E=\left(\begin{matrix}v_{q-1}&1&&&&&1\\
1&v_{q-2}&\ddots&&&&\\
&\ddots&\ddots&&&&\\
\\
&&&&&\ddots&\\
&&&&\ddots&\ddots&1\\
1&&&&&1&v_{0}\end{matrix}\right)=:\left(\begin{matrix}\text{Row}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\text{Row}_{q}\end{matrix}\right)

where we write
v(\theta+i\varepsilon+jp/q)-E
as
v_{j}
for simplicity.
We also denote
A_{n,E,\varepsilon}(p/q,\theta+jp/q):=A_{n}(j)
, and

(6.627)

\displaystyle A_{n}(j)=\left(\begin{matrix}A_{n}^{11}(j)\,&A_{n}^{12}(j)\\
A_{n}^{21}(j)&A_{n}^{22}(j)\end{matrix}\right).

Our calculations are based on the following recursive relations:

(6.628)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}A_{k}^{11}(q-k)=-v_{q-k}A_{k-1}^{11}(q-k+1)+A_{k-1}^{12}(q-k+1)\\
A_{k}^{12}(q-k)=-A_{k-1}^{11}(q-k+1)\\
A_{1}^{11}(k)=-v_{k}\\
A_{1}^{12}(k)=-1\end{cases}

Replacing
\text{Row}_{1}
with
\text{Row}_{1}-v_{q-1}\cdot\text{Row}_{2}
yields

(6.629)

\displaystyle\text{Row}_{1}^{(1)}=

\displaystyle(\begin{matrix}0,&1-v_{q-1}v_{q-2},&-v_{q-1},&0,\cdots,&0,&1\end{matrix})

(6.630)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\begin{matrix}0,&-A_{2}^{11}(q-2),&-A_{2}^{12}(q-2),&0,\cdots,&0,&1\end{matrix})

Replacing
\text{Row}_{1}^{(1)}
with
\text{Row}_{1}^{(1)}+A_{2}^{11}(q-2)\cdot\text{Row}_{3}
yields

(6.631)

\displaystyle\text{Row}_{1}^{(2)}=

\displaystyle(\begin{matrix}0,&0,&-A_{2}^{12}(q-2)+v_{q-3}A_{2}^{11}(q-2),&A_{2}^{11}(q-2),&0,\dots,&0,&1\end{matrix})

(6.632)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\begin{matrix}0,&0,&-A_{3}^{11}(q-3),&-A_{3}^{12}(q-3),&0,\cdots,&0,&1\end{matrix})

We repeat this process until

(6.633)

\displaystyle\text{Row}_{1}^{(q-2)}=(\begin{matrix}0,\cdots&0,&-A_{q-1}^{11}(1),&1-A_{q-1}^{12}(1)\end{matrix}).

Next, we carry out an analogous row reduction on the second row.
Replacing
\text{Row}_{2}
with
\text{Row}_{2}-v_{q-2}\cdot\text{Row}_{3}
yields

(6.634)

\displaystyle\text{Row}_{2}^{(1)}=(\begin{matrix}1,&0,&-A_{2}^{11}(q-3),&-A_{2}^{12}(q-3)\,,&0,\cdots,&0\end{matrix}).

We repeat this process until

(6.635)

\displaystyle\text{Row}_{2}^{(q-3)}=(\begin{matrix}1,&0,\cdots,&0,&-A_{q-2}^{11}(1),&-A_{q-2}^{12}(1))\end{matrix}.

Note that the first, second and last rows, respectively, of the matrix we started with now read

(6.636)

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}\text{Row}_{1}^{(q-2)}\\
\text{Row}_{2}^{(q-3)}\\
\text{Row}_{q}\end{matrix}\right)=\left(\begin{matrix}0&0&\cdots&0&-A^{11}_{q-1}(1)&1-A^{12}_{q-1}(1)\\
1&0&\cdots&0&-A^{11}_{q-2}(1)&-A^{12}_{q-2}(1)\\
1&0&\cdots&0&1&v_{0}\end{matrix}\right).

We expand the determinant of the transformed matrix
H_{p/q,\theta,\varepsilon}-E
along the second column using the identities
A_{q-2}^{11}(1)=A_{q-1}^{21}(1)
,
A_{q-2}^{12}(1)=A_{q-1}^{22}(1)
. This yields

(6.637)

\displaystyle|D_{p/q,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|=

\displaystyle\left|\det\left(\begin{matrix}0&-A_{q-1}^{11}(1)&1-A_{q-1}^{12}(1)\\
1&-A_{q-2}^{11}(1)&-A_{q-2}^{12}(1)\\
1&1&v_{0}\end{matrix}\right)\right|

(6.638)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\det\left(\begin{matrix}0&-A_{q-1}^{11}(1)&1-A_{q-1}^{12}(1)\\
1&-A_{q-1}^{21}(1)&-A_{q-1}^{22}(1)\\
1&1&v_{0}\end{matrix}\right)\right|

(6.639)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\det\left(\begin{matrix}0&0&-A_{q-1}^{11}(1)&1-A_{q-1}^{12}(1)\\
0&1&-A_{q-1}^{21}(1)&-A_{q-1}^{22}(1)\\
1&0&0&-1\\
0&1&1&v_{0}\end{matrix}\right)\right|

(6.640)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\det\left(\begin{matrix}\left(\begin{matrix}0&0\\
0&1\end{matrix}\right)&-A_{q-1}(1)+\left(\begin{matrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{matrix}\right)\\
I_{2}&-(A_{1}(0))^{-1}\end{matrix}\right)\right|

(6.641)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\det\left(\left(\begin{matrix}0&0\\
0&1\end{matrix}\right)-\left(A_{q-1}(1)-\left(\begin{matrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{matrix}\right)\right)A_{1}(0)\right)\right|

(6.642)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\det(A_{q}(0)-I_{2})\right|,

as claimed.
∎

By Assumption
6.4
,
(\alpha,A_{E,\varepsilon})
is 1-regular, hence by Theorem
6.2
and (
6.624
), for
p/q
sufficiently close to
\alpha
,

(6.643)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{q}\log|\lambda_{1,E,\varepsilon}(0)|-L^{1}(\alpha,A_{E,\varepsilon})\right|\leq\varepsilon_{0}.

This implies, by
\lambda_{1,E,\varepsilon}\lambda_{2,E,\varepsilon}=1
, that

(6.644)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{q}\log|\lambda_{2,E,\varepsilon}(0)|+L^{1}(\alpha,A_{E,\varepsilon})\right|\leq\varepsilon_{0}.

Hence by Lemma
6.13
, we have for all sufficiently large
q

(6.645)

\left|\frac{1}{q}\log|D_{p/q,E,\varepsilon}(0)|-L^{1}(\alpha,A_{E,\varepsilon})\right|\leq C\varepsilon_{0}.

Combining the above with (
6.623
) yields Lemma
6.12
.
∎

Finally it remains to relate
\hat{D}_{p/q,E,\varepsilon}
to the dual Lyapunov exponents.
As stated, the following argument requires
q
to be divisible by
d
(see above why can assume this).

Lemma 6.14
.

For
q=rd
with
r\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

(6.646)

\displaystyle\log|\hat{D}_{p/q,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|=

\displaystyle\log|\det(M_{r,E}^{\varepsilon}(p/r,\theta)-I_{2d})|+q\log|\hat{v}_{-d}e^{2\pi d\varepsilon}|

(6.647)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log|\det(\hat{A}_{q,E}^{\varepsilon}(p/q,\theta)-I_{2d})|+q\log|\hat{v}_{-d}e^{2\pi d\varepsilon}|.

Proof.

(
6.647
) follows from (
6.646
), due to (
2.47
).
The proof of (
6.646
) is similar to those of Lemmas
6.13
and
5.1
, and is based on elementary row operations.
Consider the operator

(6.648)

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{p/q,\theta,\varepsilon}-E=\left(\begin{matrix}C_{r-1}&\tilde{B}&&&&&B\\
B&C_{r-2}&\ddots&&&&\\
&\ddots&\ddots&&&&\\
\\
&&&&\ddots&\ddots&\\
&&&&\ddots&C_{1}&\tilde{B}\\
\tilde{B}&&&&&B&C_{0}\end{matrix}\right)=:\left(\begin{matrix}\text{Row}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\text{Row}_{r}\end{matrix}\right),

where
C_{j}=C_{\varepsilon}(\theta+jp/r)-E
and
B=B_{\varepsilon}
,
\tilde{B}=\tilde{B}_{\varepsilon}
. Denote
M_{r,E}^{\varepsilon}(p/r,\theta+jp/r)=:M_{r}(j)
, and

(6.649)

\displaystyle M_{r}(j)=\left(\begin{matrix}M_{r}^{11}(j)&M_{r}^{12}(j)\\
M_{r}^{21}(j)&M_{r}^{22}(j)\end{matrix}\right).

We will use the following recursive relations repeatedly:

(6.650)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}M_{r}^{11}(r-k)=-M_{r-1}^{11}(r-k+1)C_{r-k}B^{-1}+M_{r-1}^{12}(r-k+1)B^{-1}\\
M_{r}^{12}(r-k)=-M_{r-1}^{11}(r-k+1)\tilde{B}\\
M_{1}^{11}(k)=-C_{k}B^{-1}\\
M_{1}^{12}(k)=-\tilde{B}\end{cases}

Replacing
\text{Row}_{1}
with
\text{Row}_{1}-C_{r-1}B^{-1}\cdot\text{Row}_{2}
yields

(6.651)

\displaystyle\text{Row}_{1}^{(1)}=

\displaystyle(\begin{matrix}0,&\tilde{B}-C_{r-1}B^{-1}C_{r-2},&-C_{r-1}B^{-1}\tilde{B},&0,\cdots,&0,&B\end{matrix})

(6.652)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\begin{matrix}0,&-M_{2}^{11}(r-2)B,&-M_{2}^{12}(r-2),&0,\cdots,&0,&B).\end{matrix}

Replacing
\text{Row}_{1}^{(1)}
with
\text{Row}_{1}^{(1)}+M_{2}^{11}(r-2)\cdot\text{Row}_{3}
yields

(6.653)

\displaystyle\text{Row}_{1}^{(2)}=

\displaystyle(\begin{matrix}0,&0,&-M_{2}^{12}(r-2)+M_{2}^{11}(r-2)C_{r-3},&M_{2}^{11}(r-2)\tilde{B},&0,\cdots,&0,&B\end{matrix})

(6.654)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\begin{matrix}0,&0,&-M_{3}^{11}(r-3)B,&-M_{3}^{12}(r-3),&0,\cdots,&0,&B\end{matrix}).

Repeating this process we arrive at

(6.655)

\displaystyle\text{Row}_{1}^{(r-2)}=(\begin{matrix}0,\cdots,&0,&-M_{r-1}^{11}(1)B,&B-M_{r-1}^{12}(1)\end{matrix}).

Next we replace
\text{Row}_{2}
with
\text{Row}_{2}-C_{r-2}B^{-1}\cdot\text{Row}_{3}
, which yields

(6.656)

\displaystyle\text{Row}_{2}^{(1)}=

\displaystyle(\begin{matrix}B,&0,&\tilde{B}-C_{r-2}B^{-1}C_{r-3},&-C_{r-2}B^{-1}\tilde{B},&0,\cdots,&0\end{matrix})

(6.657)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\begin{matrix}B,&0,&-M_{2}^{11}(r-3)B,&-M_{2}^{12}(r-3),&0,\cdots,&0\end{matrix})

Repeating this process we obtain

(6.658)

\displaystyle\text{Row}_{2}^{(r-3)}=(\begin{matrix}B,&0,\cdots&0,&-M_{r-2}^{11}(1)B,&-M_{r-2}^{12}(1)\end{matrix})

The first, second and last rows are

(6.659)

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}\text{Row}_{1}^{(q-2)}\\
\text{Row}_{2}^{(q-3)}\\
\text{Row}_{r}\end{matrix}\right)=\left(\begin{matrix}0&0&\cdots&0&-M_{r-1}^{11}(1)B&B-M_{r-1}^{12}(1)\\
B&0&\cdots&0&-M_{r-2}^{11}(1)B&-M_{r-2}^{12}(1)\\
\tilde{B}&0&\cdots&0&B&C_{0}\end{matrix}\right)

Expanding determinants and using that
M_{r-2}^{11}(1)=BM_{r-1}^{21}(1)
,
M_{r-2}^{12}(1)=BM_{r-1}^{22}(1)
, we have

(6.660)

\displaystyle|\hat{D}_{p/q,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|=

\displaystyle|\det B|^{r-2}\cdot\left|\det\left(\begin{matrix}0&-M_{r-1}^{11}(1)B&B-M_{r-1}^{12}(1)\\
B&-M_{r-2}^{11}(1)B&-M_{r-2}^{12}(1)\\
\tilde{B}&B&C_{0}\end{matrix}\right)\right|

(6.661)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\det B|^{r-2}\cdot\left|\det\left(\begin{matrix}0&-M_{r-1}^{11}(1)B&B-M_{r-1}^{12}(1)\\
B&-BM_{r-1}^{21}(1)B&-BM_{r-1}^{22}(1)\\
\tilde{B}&B&C_{0}\end{matrix}\right)\right|

(6.662)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\det B|^{r-1}\cdot|\det\tilde{B}|\cdot\left|\det\left(\begin{matrix}0&-M_{r-1}^{11}(1)&B-M_{r-1}^{12}(1)\\
B&-BM_{r-1}^{21}(1)&-BM_{r-1}^{22}(1)\\
I_{2}&\tilde{B}^{-1}&\tilde{B}^{-1}C_{0}\end{matrix}\right)\right|

(6.663)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\det B|^{r-1}\cdot|\det\tilde{B}|\cdot\left|\det\left(\begin{matrix}0&0&-M_{r-1}^{11}(1)&B-M_{r-1}^{12}(1)\\
0&I_{d}&-M_{r-1}^{21}(1)&-M_{r-1}^{22}(1)\\
I_{d}&0&0&-B\\
0&I_{d}&\tilde{B}^{-1}&\tilde{B}^{-1}C_{0}\end{matrix}\right)\right|

(6.664)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\det B|^{r-1}\cdot|\det\tilde{B}|\cdot\left|\det\left(\begin{matrix}\left(\begin{matrix}0&0\\
0&I_{d}\end{matrix}\right)&-M_{r-1}(1)+\left(\begin{matrix}0&B\\
0&0\end{matrix}\right)\\
I_{2d}&-M_{1}(0)^{-1}\end{matrix}\right)\right|

(6.665)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\det B|^{r-1}\cdot|\det\tilde{B}|\cdot|\det M_{1}(0)^{-1}|\cdot\left|\det\left(\left(\begin{matrix}0&0\\
0&I_{d}\end{matrix}\right)-M_{r}(0)+\left(\begin{matrix}0&B\\
0&0\end{matrix}\right)M_{1}(0)\right)\right|

(6.666)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\det B|^{r}|\det(M_{r}(0)-I_{2d})|.

This implies the claimed result by noting
|\det B|=|\hat{v}_{-d}e^{2\pi d\varepsilon}|^{d}
.
∎

Taking Lemmas
6.12
and
6.14
into account, Lemma
6.5
will follow from the following.

Lemma 6.15
.

Under the assumptions of Lemma
6.5
one has

(6.667)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{q}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\log|\det(\hat{A}_{q,E}^{\varepsilon}(p/q,\theta)-I_{2d})|\,\mathrm{d}\theta-\hat{L}^{k_{0}}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})\right|

(6.668)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C\varepsilon_{0}\left(q^{-1}\varepsilon_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2}}+\hat{L}^{k_{0}}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})+\left|\log|\hat{v}_{-d}e^{2\pi d\varepsilon}|\right|\right)

for all sufficiently large
q
.

Proof.

Let

|\hat{\lambda}_{1,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|\geq\cdots\geq|\hat{\lambda}_{2d,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|

be the eigenvalues of
\hat{A}_{q,E}^{\varepsilon}(p/q,\theta)
.
We need the following elementary estimate.

Lemma 6.16
.

For any
1\leq m\leq 2d
, we have

(6.669)

\displaystyle\hat{L}^{m}(p/q,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon},\theta)=\frac{1}{q}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\log|\hat{\lambda}_{j,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|.

Proof.

Note by (
6.624
) the claimed is true for
m=1
. Let

(6.670)

\displaystyle\hat{A}_{q,E}^{\varepsilon}(p/q,\theta)=U_{q}^{-1}(\theta)B_{q}(\theta)U_{q}(\theta),

be the Jordan form of
\hat{A}_{q,E}^{\varepsilon}(p/q,\theta)
, where we omit the dependence of
U
and
B
on
E,\varepsilon
for simplicity.
Clearly
U_{q},U_{q}^{-1},B_{q}
are all continuous in
\theta
.
Thus there exists some constants
C_{m}>0
such that

(6.671)

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{T}}\max(\|\wedge^{m}U_{q}^{-1}(\theta)\|,\|\wedge^{m}U_{q}(\theta)\|)\leq C_{m}.

It is also easy to see that

(6.672)

\displaystyle\|\wedge^{m}(B_{q}(\theta))^{k}\|=\prod_{j=1}^{m}|\hat{\lambda}_{j,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|^{k}

Combining (
6.672
) with (
6.671
), we have for any
k\geq 1
,

(6.673)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k}\log\|\wedge^{m}(\hat{A}_{q,E}^{\varepsilon}(p/q,\theta))^{k}\|

(6.674)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k}\left(\log\|\wedge^{m}U_{q}^{-1}(\theta)\|+\log\|\wedge^{m}(B_{q}(\theta))^{k}\|+\log\|(\wedge^{m}U_{q}(\theta))^{k}\|\right)

(6.675)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{m}\log|\hat{\lambda}_{j,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|+\frac{2C_{m}}{k},

which implies

(6.676)

\displaystyle\hat{L}^{m}(p/q,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon},\theta)\leq\frac{1}{q}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\log|\hat{\lambda}_{j,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|.

This other direction of the inequality can be proved similarly.
∎

Lemma 6.17
.

If
p/q
is sufficiently close to
\alpha
, then for any
1\leq j_{1}<j_{2}<...<j_{m}\leq 2d
such that
(j_{1},...,j_{m})\neq(1,...,k_{0})
, one has

(6.677)

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{T}}\frac{1}{q}\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\log|\hat{\lambda}_{j_{\ell},E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|\leq\max(\hat{L}^{k_{0}-1}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon}),\hat{L}^{k_{0}+1}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon}))+\varepsilon_{0},

and

(6.678)

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{T}}\frac{1}{q}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{0}}\log|\hat{\lambda}_{j,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|\leq\hat{L}^{k_{0}}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})+\varepsilon_{0}.

Proof.

Case 1.
If
m\neq k_{0}
, we estimate by Lemmas
6.1
and
6.16
that uniformly in
\theta

(6.679)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{q}\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\log|\hat{\lambda}_{j_{\ell},E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|\leq\frac{1}{q}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\log|\hat{\lambda}_{j,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|=\hat{L}^{m}(p/q,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon},\theta)\leq\hat{L}^{m}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})+\varepsilon_{0}.

Case 2.1.
If
m=k_{0}
and
|\hat{\lambda}_{j_{k_{0}},E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|\leq 1
, we estimate

(6.680)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{q}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{0}}\log|\hat{\lambda}_{j_{\ell},E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|\leq\frac{1}{q}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{0}-1}\log|\hat{\lambda}_{j_{\ell},E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|\leq\hat{L}^{k_{0}-1}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})+\varepsilon_{0},

where we used the bound in Case 1.

Case 2.2.
If
m=k_{0}
and
|\hat{\lambda}_{j_{k_{0}},E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|>1
. We have
|\hat{\lambda}_{j,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|>1
for any
1\leq j\leq k_{0}
. Since
(j_{1},...,j_{k_{0}})\neq(1,...,k_{0})
, we have
j_{k_{0}}\geq k_{0}+1
.
We estimate, in this case,

(6.681)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{q}\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{0}}\log|\hat{\lambda}_{j_{\ell},E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|\leq\frac{1}{q}\sum_{j=1}^{j_{k_{0}}}\log|\hat{\lambda}_{j,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|\leq\hat{L}^{j_{k_{0}}}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})+\varepsilon_{0},

where we used again the estimate in Case 1.
Finally, note that due to the definition of
k_{0}
, we have

(6.682)

\displaystyle\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}m=1,\ldots,2d\\
m\neq k_{0}\end{subarray}}\hat{L}^{m}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})\leq\max(\hat{L}^{k_{0}-1}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon}),\hat{L}^{k_{0}+1}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})),

which proves (
6.677
). Finally,
(
6.678
) follows directly from Lemmas
6.16
and
6.1
.
∎

Let

(6.683)

\displaystyle\delta:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(\hat{L}^{k_{0}}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})-\max(\hat{L}^{k_{0}-1}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon}),\hat{L}^{k_{0}+1}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon}))\right)

(6.684)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\min(\hat{L}_{k_{0}}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon}),-\hat{L}_{k_{0}+1}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon}))>5\varepsilon_{0},

and introduce the
good set
for fixed
q

(6.685)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{q}=\mathcal{G}:=\left\{\theta\in\mathbb{T}:\frac{1}{q}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{0}}\log|\hat{\lambda}_{j,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|>\hat{L}^{k_{0}}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})-\delta\right\}.

Lemma 6.18
.

The Lebesgue measure of
\mathcal{G}
satisfies

(6.686)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{G}|\geq 1-\frac{2\varepsilon_{0}}{\delta+\varepsilon_{0}}.

Proof.

By Lemma
6.17
, we have

(6.687)

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta}\frac{1}{q}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{0}}\log|\hat{\lambda}_{j,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|\leq\hat{L}^{k_{0}}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})+\varepsilon_{0}.

Hence by the definition of
\mathcal{G}
in (
6.685
) and (
6.687
), we have

(6.688)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{G}|\cdot(\hat{L}^{k_{0}}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})+\varepsilon_{0})+(1-|\mathcal{G}|)\cdot(\hat{L}^{k_{0}}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})-\delta)\geq

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}\hat{L}^{k_{0}}(p/q,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon},\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\theta

(6.689)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hat{L}^{k_{0}}(p/q,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})>\hat{L}^{k_{0}}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})-\varepsilon_{0}.

This implies

(6.690)

\displaystyle|\mathcal{G}|\geq 1-\frac{2\varepsilon_{0}}{\delta+\varepsilon_{0}},

as desired.
∎

On the good set we have the following approximation result.

Lemma 6.19
.

If
p/q
is sufficiently close to
\alpha
, then

(6.691)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{q}\int_{\mathcal{G}}\sum_{j=1}^{2d}\log|\hat{\lambda}_{j,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)-1|\,\mathrm{d}\theta=\frac{1}{q}\int_{\mathcal{G}}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{0}}\log|\hat{\lambda}_{j,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|\,\mathrm{d}\theta+O(1/q).

Proof.

For each
\theta\in\mathcal{G}
, for
q
large, we estimate by Lemma
6.17
that

(6.692)

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{2d}|\hat{\lambda}_{j,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)-1|\geq

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{k_{0}}|\hat{\lambda}_{j,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1}<j_{2}<...<j_{m}\\
(j_{1},...,j_{m})\neq(1,...,k_{0})\end{subarray}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}|\hat{\lambda}_{j_{\ell},E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|

(6.693)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\prod_{j=1}^{k_{0}}|\hat{\lambda}_{j,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|.

Analogously, for all
\theta\in\mathcal{G}
we have the upper bound

(6.694)

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{2d}|\hat{\lambda}_{j,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)-1|\leq 2\prod_{j=1}^{k_{0}}|\hat{\lambda}_{j,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|.

The result follows by (
6.692
) and (
6.694
).
∎

Next, we estimate

Lemma 6.20
.

Under the assumptions of Lemma
6.5
,

(6.695)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{q}\int_{\mathcal{G}^{c}}\log|\det(\hat{A}_{q,E}^{\varepsilon}(p/q,\theta)-I_{2d})|\,\mathrm{d}\theta\right|\leq C\varepsilon_{0}\left(q^{-1}\varepsilon_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2}}+\hat{L}^{k_{0}}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})+\big|\log|\hat{v}_{d}e^{2\pi d\varepsilon}|\big|\right).

Proof.

By Lemma
6.17
, we have

(6.696)

\displaystyle|\det(\hat{A}_{q,E}^{\varepsilon}(p/q,\theta)-I_{2d})|\leq\sum_{1\leq j_{1}<...<j_{m}\leq 2d}\,\,\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}|\hat{\lambda}_{j_{\ell},E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|\leq C_{d}\exp(q\cdot(\hat{L}^{k_{0}}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})+\varepsilon_{0})).

This implies, after combining with (
6.690
),

(6.697)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{q}\int_{\mathcal{G}^{c}}\log|\det(\hat{A}_{q,E}^{\varepsilon}(p/q,\theta)-I_{2d})|\,\mathrm{d}\theta\leq

\displaystyle|\mathcal{G}^{c}|\cdot(\hat{L}^{k_{0}}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})+2\varepsilon_{0})

(6.698)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\varepsilon_{0}(\hat{L}^{k_{0}}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})+2\varepsilon_{0}).

By Lemmas
6.14
,
6.10
, and (
6.690
), we have

(6.699)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{q}\int_{\mathcal{G}^{c}}\log|\det(\hat{A}_{q,E}^{\varepsilon}(p/q,\theta)-I_{2d})|\,\mathrm{d}\theta

(6.700)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{q}\int_{\mathcal{G}^{c}}\left(\log|\hat{D}_{p/q,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|-q\log|\hat{v}_{-d}e^{2\pi d\varepsilon}|\right)\,\mathrm{d}\theta

(6.701)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{q}\int_{\mathcal{G}^{c}}\log|D_{p/q,E,\varepsilon}(0)+2(-1)^{q+1}\cos(2\pi q\theta)|\,\mathrm{d}\theta-|\mathcal{G}^{c}|\cdot\log|\hat{v}_{-d}e^{2\pi d\varepsilon}|

(6.702)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle-C\varepsilon_{0}\left|\log|\hat{v}_{-d}e^{2\pi d\varepsilon}|\right|

provided
|D_{p/q,E,\varepsilon}(0)|\geq 3
. On the other hand, if
|D_{p/q,E,\varepsilon}(0)|\leq 3
, then

(6.703)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{q}\int_{\mathcal{G}^{c}}\log|D_{p/q,E,\varepsilon}(0)+2(-1)^{q+1}\cos(2\pi q\theta)|\,\mathrm{d}\theta\geq

(6.704)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{q}|\mathcal{G}^{c}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\big(\log|D_{p/q,E,\varepsilon}(0)+2(-1)^{q+1}\cos(2\pi\theta)|\big)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\theta\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\geq-Cq^{-1}\sqrt{\varepsilon_{0}},

as desired.
∎

We also have

Lemma 6.21
.

(6.705)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{q}\int_{\mathcal{G}^{c}}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{0}}\log|\hat{\lambda}_{j,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|\,\mathrm{d}\theta-|\mathcal{G}^{c}|\cdot\hat{L}^{k_{0}}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})\right|\leq 2\varepsilon_{0}.

Proof.

For simplicity, we write
g(\theta):=\frac{1}{q}\log\sum_{j=1}^{k_{0}}|\hat{\lambda}_{j,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|
and
\hat{L}^{k_{0}}:=\hat{L}^{k_{0}}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})
.
By Lemmas
6.16
and
6.17
, we have

(6.706)

\displaystyle\hat{L}^{k_{0}}-\varepsilon_{0}\leq\int_{\mathbb{T}}g(\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\theta\leq\int_{\mathcal{G}^{c}}g(\theta)\,\mathrm{d}\theta+|\mathcal{G}|\cdot(\hat{L}^{k_{0}}+\varepsilon_{0}),

which implies

(6.707)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{G}^{c}}(g(\theta)-\hat{L}^{k_{0}})\,\mathrm{d}\theta\geq-2\varepsilon_{0}.

We also have by (
6.690
) and Lemma
6.17
that

(6.708)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{G}^{c}}(g(\theta)-\hat{L}^{k_{0}})\,\mathrm{d}\theta\leq\varepsilon_{0}\cdot|\mathcal{G}^{c}|\leq\frac{2\varepsilon_{0}^{2}}{\delta+\varepsilon_{0}}.

This proves the claimed result.
∎

Finally, combining (
6.691
) with Lemmas
6.20
and
6.21
, we have

(6.709)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{q}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\log|\det(\hat{A}_{q,E}^{\varepsilon}(p/q,\theta)-I_{2d})|\,\mathrm{d}\theta-\hat{L}^{k_{0}}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})\right|

(6.710)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{q}\int_{\mathcal{G}}\sum_{j=1}^{2d}\log|\hat{\lambda}_{j,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)-1|\,\mathrm{d}\theta-\frac{1}{q}\int_{\mathcal{G}}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{0}}\log|\hat{\lambda}_{j,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|\,\mathrm{d}\theta\right|

(6.711)

\displaystyle+\left|\frac{1}{q}\int_{\mathcal{G}^{c}}\log|\det(\hat{A}_{q,E}^{\varepsilon}(p/q,\theta)-I_{2d})|\,\mathrm{d}\theta\right|+\left|\frac{1}{q}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{0}}\log|\hat{\lambda}_{j,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|\,\mathrm{d}\theta-\hat{L}^{k_{0}}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E,\varepsilon})\right|

(6.712)

\displaystyle+\left|\frac{1}{q}\int_{\mathcal{G}^{c}}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{0}}\log|\hat{\lambda}_{j,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|\,\mathrm{d}\theta-|\mathcal{G}^{c}|\cdot\hat{L}^{k_{0}}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})\right|+|\mathcal{G}^{c}|\cdot\hat{L}^{k_{0}}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})

(6.713)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\varepsilon_{0}(q^{-1}\varepsilon_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2}}+\hat{L}^{k_{0}}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})+\big|\log|\hat{v}_{-d}e^{2\pi d\varepsilon}|\big|).

Combining Lemmas
6.12
,
6.14
,
6.15
, we have proved Lemma
6.5
.
∎

It remains to deal with the case
\hat{L}^{1}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}^{\varepsilon})<0
. By Lemmas
6.1
and
6.15
, there exists
\delta>0
so that uniformly in
\theta
, and provided
p/q
is close to
\alpha
, one has

-\delta\geq\frac{1}{q}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\log|\hat{\lambda}_{j,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|

for all
1\leq m\leq 2d
.
Hence, for all
1\leq j_{1}\leq\ldots\leq j_{m}\leq 2d
we have

\sup_{\theta}\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}|\hat{\lambda}_{j_{\ell},E,\varepsilon}(\theta)|\leq e^{-\delta q}

Therefore,

\prod_{j=1}^{2d}|\hat{\lambda}_{j,E,\varepsilon}(\theta)-1|=1+O_{d}(e^{-\delta q})

and

\frac{1}{q}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\log|\det(\hat{A}_{q,E}^{\varepsilon}(p/q,\theta)-I_{2d})|\,\mathrm{d}\theta=O_{d}(e^{-\delta q})

We now conclude Theorem
1.10
from Lemmas
6.12
and
6.14
as before.

Appendix A
Proof of Theorem
1.2

The argument closely follows
[
AJ2
]
, with a simplification from
[
Av2
]
.
Throughout this section we write
\hat{L}^{j}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E}),\hat{L}_{j}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})
as
\hat{L}^{j}(\hat{A}_{E}),\hat{L}_{j}(\hat{A}_{E})
for simplicity.

A.1.
Preparation

Theorem A.1
.

[
AJ2
]
*Theorem 3.3
If
E\in\sigma(H_{\alpha,\theta})
, then there exist
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
and a bounded solution of
\hat{H}_{\alpha,\theta}\hat{u}=E\hat{u}
with

\hat{u}_{0}=1
and
|\hat{u}_{n}|\leq 1
.

Given Fourier coefficients
\hat{w}=(\hat{w}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
and an interval
I\subset\mathbb{Z}
, we let
w_{I}=\sum_{k\in I}\hat{w}_{k}e^{2\pi ikx}
. The length of the interval
I=[a,b]
is
|I|=b-a
.
We will say that a trigonometrical polynomial
p:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{C}
has
essential degree at most

k
if its
Fourier coefficients outside an interval
I
of length
k
vanish. We let
\frac{p_{n}}{q_{n}}
be the convergents of
\alpha
.

Theorem A.2
.

[
AJ2
]
*Theorem 6.1
Let
1\leq r\leq\lfloor q_{n+1}/q_{n}\rfloor
. If
p
has essential degree at most
k=rq_{n}-1
and
x_{0}\in\mathbb{T}
,
then for some absolute constant
C

(A.1)

\displaystyle\|p\|_{0}\leq Cq_{n+1}^{Cr}\sup_{0\leq j\leq k}|p(x_{0}+j\alpha)|.

A.2.
Proof of Theorem
1.2

Fix
E\in\sigma(H_{\alpha,\theta})
, and let
\theta(E)
be as in Theorem
A.1
. Let
\varepsilon_{0}
be such that
0<\varepsilon_{0}<\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{30\hat{L}^{d}(\hat{A}_{E})+100C_{3}d}
where
C_{3}
is the absolute constant in Lemma
3.10
. Finally, let
\{n_{j}\}
be the
\varepsilon_{0}
-resonances of
\theta(E)
and
\hat{u}
be the almost localized solution as in Theorem
3.8
. Hence for
3(|n_{j}|+1)<|n|<|n_{j+1}|/3
and some
C_{2}=C_{2}(v,d)>0
, one has

(A.2)

\displaystyle|\hat{u}_{n}|\leq C_{2}e^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{10}|n|}.

For simplicity we shall write
\theta(E)
as
\theta
for the remainder of this appendix.

The cocycle
(\alpha,A_{E})
being subcritical, which precisely means that
L(\alpha,A_{E,\eta})=0
for all
|\eta|\leq\hat{L}_{d}/(2\pi)
, implies via Lemma
2.1
the following subexponential growth

(A.3)

\displaystyle\|A_{k,E}\|_{\frac{\hat{L}_{d}}{2\pi}}\leq C_{\delta}e^{\delta|k|},

for arbitrary
\delta>0
.
Henceforth, we fix
\delta=\min(\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{5000},\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{25})
.
Theorem
A.3
improves this subexponential bound to a polynomial one.

Theorem A.3
.

For some absolute constants
C_{4}>0
, and
C_{5}=C_{5}(\alpha,v,E,\varepsilon_{0})>0
,

(A.4)

\displaystyle\|A_{k,E}\|_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}\leq C_{5}(1+|k|)^{C_{4}},

for any
k\geq 1
.

Proof.

The proof proceeds by constructing an
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
almost conjugacy of
A_{E}(x)
.
Thus, let
n
be such that

(A.5)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}9|n_{j}|<n<|n_{j+1}|/9,\text{ for some }j,\\
n=rq_{m}-1<q_{m+1},\text{ for some }r,m\geq 1\end{cases}

To see that such an
n
exists, note that Lemma
3.7
implies that

(A.6)

\displaystyle\frac{|n_{j+1}|}{81|n_{j}|}>3,

Moreover, for any
r,m\geq 1
, one has

(A.7)

\displaystyle\frac{(r+1)q_{m}-1}{rq_{m}-1}\leq 2.

Let
I=[x_{1},x_{2}]:=[-\lfloor n/2\rfloor+1,\lfloor n/2\rfloor]\cap\mathbb{Z}
, and define

(A.8)

\displaystyle U^{I}(x)=\left(\begin{matrix}e^{2\pi i\theta}u^{I}(x)\\
u^{I}(x-\alpha)\end{matrix}\right).

By direct computation one obtains

(A.9)

\displaystyle A_{E}(x)U^{I}(x)=e^{2\pi i\theta}U^{I}(x+\alpha)+G^{I}(x),

where

(A.10)

\displaystyle\|G^{I}(x)\|_{\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{40\pi}}\leq C_{2}e^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{40}n}.

We select
U^{I}(x)
as the first column of our putative conjugacy matrix. In order to construct the second column, we first establish a lower bound on
\|U^{I}(x)\|
.

Lemma A.4
.

For
n>n_{0}(\alpha,v,E,\varepsilon_{0})
satisfying (
A.5
), we have

(A.11)

\displaystyle\inf_{|\mathrm{Im}(x)|\leq\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{40\pi}}\|U^{I}(x)\|\geq e^{-2\delta n}.

Proof.

If the lemma fails, then there exists
x_{0}
with

|\mathrm{Im}(x_{0})|\leq\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{40\pi}
so that

(A.12)

\displaystyle\|U^{I}(x_{0})\|<e^{-2\delta n}.

Iterating (
A.9
) yields

(A.13)

\displaystyle e^{2\pi i\ell\theta}U^{I}(x_{0}+\ell\alpha)=

\displaystyle A_{\ell,E}(x_{0})U_{I}(x_{0})

(A.14)

\displaystyle-\sum_{m=1}^{\ell}e^{2\pi i(m-1)\theta}A_{\ell-m,E}(x_{0}+m\alpha)G^{I}(x_{0}+(m-1)\alpha).

Combining the above with (
A.3
), (
A.12
) and (
A.10
), we infer that for
0\leq\ell\leq n
,

(A.15)

\displaystyle|u^{I}(x_{0}+\ell\alpha)|\leq\|U^{I}(x_{0}+\ell\alpha)\|\leq C_{\delta}e^{\delta n}e^{-2\delta n}+\sum_{m=1}^{\ell}C_{\delta}C_{2}e^{\delta(\ell-m)}e^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{40}n}\leq C_{\delta}C_{2}e^{-\delta n}.

By Theorem
A.2
, we finally arrive at the uniform bound

(A.16)

\displaystyle\|u^{I}(x+i\mathrm{Im}(x_{0}))\|_{\mathbb{T}}\leq Cq_{m+1}^{Cr}\sup_{0\leq\ell\leq n}|u^{I}(x_{0}+\ell\alpha)|\leq C_{\delta}C_{2}e^{-\delta n/2},

where we used that
q_{m+1}\leq C_{\delta}e^{\delta q_{m}/(2C)}
, see (
3.76
).
Clearly, (
A.16
) contradicts

\int_{\mathbb{T}}u(x+i\mathrm{Im}(x_{0}))\,\mathrm{d}x=\hat{u}_{0}=1

for
n
large enough.
∎

The now invoke the following lemma to construct the second column of our conjugacy matrix.

Lemma A.5
.

[
Av2
]
*Lemma 4.2
Let
V:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{C}^{2}
be analytic in
|\mathrm{Im}(x)|\leq\eta
. Assume

\delta_{1}<\|V(x)\|<\delta_{2}^{-1}
holds on
|\mathrm{Im}(x)|<\eta
. Then there exists
M:\mathbb{T}\to\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{C})
analytic on
|\mathrm{Im}(x)|<\eta
with the first column equals to
V
and
\|M\|_{\eta}\leq C\delta_{1}^{-2}\delta_{2}^{-1}(1-\log(\delta_{1}\delta_{2}))
.

Let
M^{I}(x)
be the matrix obtained by means of Lemma
A.5
whose first column equals
U^{I}
.
By Lemma
A.4
and the fact that
|\hat{u}_{k}|\leq 1
, for any
|\mathrm{Im}(x)|\leq\frac{\delta}{2\pi}
,

(A.17)

\displaystyle e^{-2\delta n}\leq\|U^{I}(x)\|\leq e^{\delta n}.

By Lemma
A.5
,

(A.18)

\displaystyle\|M^{I}\|_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}=\|(M^{I})^{-1}\|_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}\leq e^{6\delta n}.

and by (
A.9
),

(A.19)

\displaystyle A_{E}(x)M^{I}(x)=M^{I}(x+\alpha)\left(\begin{matrix}e^{2\pi i\theta}&a_{12}(x)\\
0&e^{-2\pi i\theta}+a_{22}(x)\end{matrix}\right)+\left(\begin{matrix}G^{I}(x)&0\end{matrix}\right).

Thus,

\displaystyle M^{I}(x+\alpha)^{-1}A_{E}(x)M^{I}(x)=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}e^{2\pi i\theta}&a_{12}(x)\\
0&e^{-2\pi i\theta}+a_{22}(x)\end{matrix}\right)+M^{I}(x+\alpha)^{-1}\left(\begin{matrix}G^{I}(x)&0\end{matrix}\right)

(A.20)

\displaystyle=:

\displaystyle\left(\begin{matrix}e^{2\pi i\theta}&a_{12}(x)\\
0&e^{-2\pi i\theta}+a_{22}(x)\end{matrix}\right)+\left(\begin{matrix}a_{11}(x)&0\\
a_{21}(x)&0\end{matrix}\right)

In view of (
A.10
) and (
A.18
),

(A.21)

\displaystyle\max(\|a_{11}\|_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}},\|a_{21}\|_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}})\leq\|M^{I}(x+\alpha)^{-1}\left(\begin{matrix}G^{I}(x)&0\end{matrix}\right)\|_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}\leq e^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{100}n},

whence

(A.22)

\displaystyle\|a_{12}\|_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}\leq\|(M^{I})^{-1}\|_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}\cdot\|A_{E}\|_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}\cdot\|M^{I}\|_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}+\max(\|a_{11}\|_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}},\|a_{21}\|_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}})\leq C_{\delta}e^{12\delta n}.

Passing to determinants in (
A.2
), and using (
A.21
), we conclude that

(A.23)

\displaystyle(e^{2\pi i\theta}+a_{11}(x))(e^{-2\pi i\theta}+a_{22}(x))-a_{12}(x)a_{21}(x)=1,

which implies

(A.24)

\displaystyle(e^{2\pi i\theta}+a_{11}(x))a_{22}(x)=a_{12}(x)a_{21}(x)-e^{-2\pi i\theta}a_{11}(x).

Combining the above equation with the bounds in (
A.21
) and (
A.22
) implies that

(A.25)

\displaystyle\|a_{22}\|_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}\leq e^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{200}n}.

Let

(A.26)

\displaystyle\Phi^{I}(x)=M^{I}(x)\left(\begin{matrix}e^{\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{400}n}&0\\
0&e^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{400}n}\end{matrix}\right).

Then by (
A.2
), (
A.21
), (
A.22
) and (
A.25
),

(A.27)

\displaystyle\Phi^{I}(x+\alpha)^{-1}A_{E}(x)\Phi^{I}(x)=\left(\begin{matrix}e^{2\pi i\theta}&0\\
0&e^{-2\pi i\theta}\end{matrix}\right)+H(x),

where

(A.28)

\displaystyle\|H\|_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}\leq

\displaystyle\max(\|a_{12}\|_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{200}},\|a_{11}\|_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}},\|a_{22}\|_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}},\|a_{21}\|_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}e^{\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{200}n})

(A.29)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{\delta}e^{12\delta n}e^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{200}n}\leq e^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{400}n}.

Moreover, by (
A.18
) and (
A.26
),

(A.30)

\displaystyle\|\Phi^{I}\|_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}=\|(\Phi^{I})^{-1}\|_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}\leq\|M^{I}\|_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}e^{\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{400}}\leq e^{\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{200}}.

In view of (
A.27
), this implies that for all
1\leq s\leq e^{\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{800}n}
one has

(A.31)

\displaystyle\|A_{s,E}(x)\|_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}\leq 2\|\Phi^{I}\|_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}\cdot\|(\Phi^{I})^{-1}\|_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}\leq 2e^{\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{100}n}.

There exists an absolute constant
c_{4}>0
, such that for
s>s(\alpha,v,E,\varepsilon_{0})
large enough, there exists
n
, with
9|n_{j}|<n<|n_{j+1}|/9
, and satisfying the requirement of Lemma
A.4
, such that
c_{4}n<\frac{800}{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}\log s<n
.
Thus by (
A.31
), we finally obtain

(A.32)

\displaystyle\|A_{s,E}\|_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}\leq C_{5}(1+|s|)^{8/c_{4}},

as claimed.
∎

Next, let
J=[-\lfloor|n_{j+1}|/9\rfloor,\lfloor|n_{j+1}|/9\rfloor]\cap\mathbb{Z}
, and define

(A.33)

\displaystyle U^{J}(x)=\left(\begin{matrix}e^{2\pi i\theta}u^{J}(x)\\
u^{J}(x-\alpha)\end{matrix}\right).

By the
almost localization
Theorem
3.8
, one has

(A.34)

\displaystyle A_{E}(x)U^{J}(x)=e^{2\pi i\theta}U^{J}(x+\alpha)+G^{J}(x),\text{ with }\|G^{J}\|_{\frac{\hat{L}_{d}}{32\pi}}\leq e^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{150}|n_{j+1}|},

and

(A.35)

\displaystyle\|U^{J}\|_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}\leq e^{3\delta|n_{j}|}.

Let
M_{J}(x)
be the matrix with the first column equal to
U^{J}(x)
, and the second column equal to
\overline{U^{J}(x)}
, respectively.
By the preceding,

(A.36)

\displaystyle A_{E}(x)M_{J}(x)=M_{J}(x+\alpha)\left(\begin{matrix}e^{2\pi i\theta}&0\\
0&e^{-2\pi i\theta}\end{matrix}\right)+H(x),

where
H(x)=\left(\begin{matrix}G(x)&\overline{G(x)}\end{matrix}\right)
satisfies
\|H\|_{\frac{\hat{L}_{d}}{32\pi}}\leq e^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{150}|n_{j+1}|}.

We also define
\tilde{U}^{J}(x):=e^{\pi in_{j}x}U^{J}(x)
.
When
n_{j}
is even,
\tilde{U}^{J}
is well-defined on
\mathbb{T}
, and when
n_{j}
is odd,
\tilde{U}^{J}(x+1)=-\tilde{U}^{J}(x)
.
By (
A.34
), with
\tilde{\theta}:=\theta-n_{j}\alpha/2
,

(A.37)

\displaystyle A_{E}(x)\tilde{U}^{J}(x)=e^{2\pi i\tilde{\theta}}\tilde{U}^{J}(x+\alpha)+\tilde{G}^{J}(x),\text{ where }\|\tilde{G}^{J}\|_{\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{32\pi}}\leq e^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{200}|n_{j+1}|}.

By Theorem
3.8
, we also have

(A.38)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{U}^{J}\|_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}\leq e^{4\delta|n_{j}|}.

Let
\tilde{M}_{J}(x)
be the matrix with columns
\tilde{U}^{J}(x)
and
\overline{\tilde{U}^{J}(x)}
.
Clearly,

(A.39)

\displaystyle|\det\tilde{M}_{J}(x)|=e^{-2\pi n_{j}\mathrm{Im}(x)}|\det M_{J}(x)|.

Theorem A.6
.

Let
L=\|2\theta-n_{j}\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{-1}
. Then for
j>j_{0}(\alpha,v,E,\varepsilon_{0})
,

(A.40)

\displaystyle|\det M_{J}(x)|\geq L^{-5C_{4}},\text{ for }|\mathrm{Im}(x)|\leq\frac{\delta}{2\pi},

where
C_{4}
is the constant in Theorem
A.3
.

Proof.

We first establish the following estimates on
L
.

Lemma A.7
.

For
j>j_{0}(\alpha,\hat{L}_{d})
,

(A.41)

\displaystyle e^{\varepsilon_{0}|n_{j}|}\leq L\leq e^{\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{4000C_{4}}|n_{j+1}|}.

Proof.

For
\varepsilon_{1}=\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{4000C_{4}}
,
we have by (
3.76
) that for
n>N(\alpha,\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E}))
,

(A.42)

\displaystyle C(\alpha,\hat{L}_{d})e^{-2\varepsilon_{1}|n_{j+1}|}

\displaystyle\leq C(\alpha,\hat{L}_{d})e^{-\varepsilon_{1}|n_{j+1}-n_{j}|}\leq\|(n_{j+1}-n_{j})\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}

(A.43)

\displaystyle\leq 2\|2\theta-n_{j}\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}=2L^{-1}\leq 2e^{-\varepsilon_{0}|n_{j}|}.

This implies the claimed result.
∎

We now prove Theorem
A.6
by contradiction, based on the next lemma.

Lemma A.8
.

For any
\kappa\in\mathbb{C}
such that
|\kappa|=1
,

(A.44)

\displaystyle\inf_{x:|\mathrm{Im}(x)|\leq\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}\|\tilde{U}^{J}(x)-\kappa\cdot\overline{\tilde{U}^{J}(x)}\|\geq L^{-4C_{4}}.

Proof.

Suppose for some
x_{0}
,
|\mathrm{Im}(x_{0})|\leq\frac{\delta}{2\pi}
, and some constant
|\kappa|=1
we have

(A.45)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{U}^{J}(x_{0})-\kappa\cdot\overline{\tilde{U}^{J}(x_{0})}\|<L^{-4C_{4}}.

By (
A.37
), Theorem
A.3
and Lemma
A.7
, for all
0\leq|\ell|\leq L^{2}
one concludes that

\displaystyle\|\tilde{U}^{J}(x_{0}+\ell\alpha)e^{2\pi i\ell\tilde{\theta}}-\kappa\cdot\overline{\tilde{U}^{J}(x_{0}+\ell\alpha)}e^{-2\pi i\ell\tilde{\theta}}\|

(A.46)

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\leq\left\|\sum_{m=0}^{\ell-1}A_{\ell-m,E}(x_{0}+m\alpha)\tilde{G}^{J}(x_{0}+m\alpha)-\kappa\sum_{m=0}^{\ell-1}A_{\ell-m,E}(x_{0}+m\alpha)\overline{\tilde{G}^{J}(x_{0}+m\alpha)}\right\|

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,+\|A_{\ell,E}(x_{0})\|L^{-4C_{4}}

(A.47)

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,\leq C_{5}L^{2C_{4}+3}e^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{200}|n_{j+1}|}+C_{5}L^{-2C_{4}}<L^{-C_{4}}.

Setting
\ell=\ell_{0}=\lfloor L/2\rfloor
in (
A.2
), we conclude that

(A.48)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{U}^{J}(x_{0}+\ell_{0}\alpha)e^{2\pi i\ell_{0}\tilde{\theta}}-\kappa\cdot\overline{\tilde{U}^{J}(x_{0}+\ell_{0}\alpha)}e^{-2\pi i\ell_{0}\tilde{\theta}}\|<L^{-C_{4}}.

Writing
\ell_{0}=L/2+\delta
with
|\delta|\leq 1/2
, and
2\theta-n_{j}\alpha=m_{0}\pm\|2\theta-n_{j}\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}
, we infer that

(A.49)

\displaystyle 2\pi\ell_{0}\tilde{\theta}=\pi(L/2+\delta)(2\theta-n_{j}\alpha)=\pi\ell_{0}m_{0}\pm\frac{\pi}{2}\pm\pi\delta L^{-1}=\pi\ell_{0}m_{0}\pm\frac{\pi}{2}+O(L^{-1}).

Plugging (
A.49
) into (
A.48
), and combining the resulting estimate with (
A.38
), yields

\displaystyle\|\tilde{U}^{J}(x_{0}+\ell_{0}\alpha)+\kappa\cdot\overline{\tilde{U}^{J}(x_{0}+\ell_{0}\alpha)}\|

\displaystyle\leq L^{-C_{4}}+L^{-1}\|\tilde{U}^{J}\|_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}\leq L^{-C_{4}}+L^{-1}e^{4\delta|n_{j}|}

(A.50)

\displaystyle<L^{-1/2}.

For
|\ell|\leq L^{1/2}
, we have in analogy to (
A.49
) that

(A.51)

\displaystyle 2\pi\ell\tilde{\theta}=\pi\ell m_{0}+O(L^{-1/2}).

Plugging (
A.51
) into (
A.37
) yields

\displaystyle\|\tilde{U}^{J}(x_{0}+\ell\alpha)-\kappa\cdot\overline{\tilde{U}^{J}(x_{0}+\ell\alpha)}\|

\displaystyle\leq L^{-C_{4}}+L^{-1/2}\|\tilde{U}^{J}\|_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}\leq L^{-C_{4}}+L^{-1/2}e^{4\delta|n_{j}|}

(A.52)

\displaystyle<L^{-1/3}.

Next, we let
\tilde{n}
be such that

(A.53)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}9|n_{j}|<\tilde{n}<\min(27|n_{j}|,|n_{j+1}|/9)\\
\tilde{n}=rq_{m}-1<q_{m+1},\text{ for some }r,m\geq 1\end{cases}

Let
\tilde{I}=[-\lfloor\tilde{n}/2\rfloor+1,\lfloor\tilde{n}/2\rfloor]\cap\mathbb{Z}
and define

(A.54)

\displaystyle U^{\tilde{I}}(x)=\left(\begin{matrix}e^{2\pi i\theta}u^{\tilde{I}}(x)\\
u^{\tilde{I}}(x-\alpha)\end{matrix}\right),

as well as
\tilde{U}^{\tilde{I}}(x)=e^{\pi in_{j}x}U^{\tilde{I}}(x)
.
Then by Theorem
3.8
, we have

(A.55)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{U}^{J}-\tilde{U}^{\tilde{I}}\|_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}\leq e^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{2}|n_{j}|}

Combining the above with (
A.2
) yields

(A.56)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{U}^{\tilde{I}}(x_{0}+\ell\alpha)-\kappa\cdot\overline{\tilde{U}^{\tilde{I}}(x_{0}+\ell\alpha)}\|\leq L^{-1/3}+2e^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{2}|n_{j}|}.

Denoting

(A.57)

\displaystyle g(x)=e^{-\pi n_{j}\mathrm{Im}(x_{0})}e^{2\pi in_{j}x}\sum_{|k|\leq 9|n_{j}|}e^{-2\pi k\mathrm{Im}(x_{0})}\left(\hat{u}_{k}e^{2\pi ikx}-\kappa\,\overline{\hat{u}_{k}}e^{-2\pi ikx}\right),

we obtain from (
A.56
) that

(A.58)

\displaystyle|g(\mathrm{Re}(x_{0})+\ell\alpha)|=

\displaystyle\left|e^{\pi in_{j}(x_{0}+\ell\alpha)}u^{\tilde{I}}(x_{0}+\ell\alpha)-\kappa\;\overline{e^{\pi in_{j}(x_{0}+\ell\alpha)}u^{\tilde{I}}(x_{0}+\ell\alpha)}\right|

(A.59)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\tilde{U}^{\tilde{I}}(x_{0}+\ell\alpha)-\kappa\cdot\overline{\tilde{U}^{\tilde{I}}(x_{0}+\ell\alpha)}\|\leq L^{-1/3}+e^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{2}|n_{j}|}

for any
|\ell|\leq L^{1/2}
.
Lemma
A.7
implies that
L^{1/2}>e^{\varepsilon_{0}|n_{j}|}>\tilde{n}
, whence by Theorem
A.2
,

(A.60)

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}|g(x)|\leq Cq_{m+1}^{Cr}(L^{-1/3}+e^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}}{2}|n_{j}|})<L^{-1/4}+e^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{4}|n_{j}|}.

Here we used that
q_{m+1}<C_{\varepsilon_{0}}e^{\varepsilon_{0}q_{m}/350C}
, see (
3.76
), and
\tilde{n}=rq_{m}-1<27|n_{j}|
.
This implies

(A.61)

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}\|\tilde{U}^{\tilde{I}}(x+\mathrm{Im}(x_{0}))-\kappa\cdot\overline{\tilde{U}^{\tilde{I}}}(x+\mathrm{Im}(x_{0}))\|\leq 2\sup_{x\in\mathbb{T}}|g(x)|\leq 2L^{-1/4}+2e^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{4}|n_{j}|},

and in particular

(A.62)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{U}^{\tilde{I}}(x_{0}+\ell_{0}\alpha)-\kappa\cdot\overline{\tilde{U}^{\tilde{I}}(x_{0}+\ell_{0}\alpha)}\|\leq 2L^{-1/4}+2e^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{4}|n_{j}|}.

Combining (
A.2
) with (
A.55
), we have

(A.63)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{U}^{\tilde{I}}(x_{0}+\ell_{0}\alpha)+\kappa\cdot\overline{\tilde{U}^{\tilde{I}}(x_{0}+\ell_{0}\alpha)}\|\leq L^{-1/2}+e^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{2}n}.

Finally combining (
A.63
) with (
A.62
), we conclude that

(A.64)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{U}^{\tilde{I}}(x_{0}+\ell_{0}\alpha)\|\leq 2L^{-1/4}+2e^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{4}|n_{j}|}.

This leads to a contradiction with Lemma
A.4
which implies

(A.65)

\displaystyle\inf_{|\mathrm{Im}(x)|\leq\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}\|\tilde{U}^{\tilde{I}}(x)\|\geq e^{-2\delta\tilde{n}}e^{-\pi|n_{j}\mathrm{Im}(x)|}\geq e^{-55\delta|n_{j}|}>L^{-1/2}+e^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{2}|n_{j}|},

where we used Lemma
A.7
and
\tilde{n}<27|n_{j}|
.
∎

Next, we return to the estimate of
\det M_{J}
.
Combining (
A.55
) with (
A.65
), we obtain

(A.66)

\displaystyle\inf_{|\mathrm{Im}(x)|\leq\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}\left(\max(|e^{\pi in_{j}x}u^{J}(x)|,|e^{\pi in_{j}x}u^{J}(x-\alpha)|)\right)\geq\inf_{|\mathrm{Im}(x)|\leq\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}\|U^{J}(x)\|\geq

\displaystyle e^{-55\delta|n_{j}|}-e^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{2}|n_{j}|}

(A.67)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle e^{-60\delta|n_{j}|}.

For an arbitrary
x
with
|\mathrm{Im}(x)|\leq\frac{\delta}{2\pi}
, suppose we have (the other case is similar)

(A.68)

\displaystyle|e^{\pi in_{j}x}u^{J}(x)|\geq e^{-60\delta|n_{j}|}.

Setting
\kappa:=\overline{e^{\pi in_{j}x}u^{J}(x)}/(e^{\pi in_{j}x}u^{J}(x))
, which satisfies
|\kappa|=1
, one has

(A.69)

\displaystyle|\det\tilde{M}_{J}(x)|=

\displaystyle|\det(\begin{matrix}\tilde{U}^{J}(x),\,\,\,\overline{\tilde{U}^{J}(x)}-\kappa\cdot\tilde{U}^{J}(x)\end{matrix})|=|e^{\pi in_{j}x}u^{J}(x)|\cdot\|\tilde{U}^{J}(x)-\overline{\kappa}\cdot\overline{\tilde{U}^{J}(x)}\|

(A.70)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle L^{-4C_{4}}e^{-60\delta|n_{j}|},

where we used (
A.68
) and Lemma
A.8
in the last line.
The claimed result on
|\det M_{J}(x)|
follows by combining (
A.39
) with Lemma
A.7
.
∎

Next, we construct an
\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
conjugacy.
Let
S(x)=\mathrm{Re}\,U^{J}(x)
and
T(x)=\mathrm{Im}\,U^{J}(x)
, and define

W_{1}(x)
to be the matrix with columns
S(x)
and
T(x)
.
Thus

(A.71)

\displaystyle M_{J}(x)=W_{1}(x)\left(\begin{matrix}1\ &1\\
i&-i\end{matrix}\right).

Clearly (
A.35
) implies

(A.72)

\displaystyle\|W_{1}\|_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}\leq e^{3\delta|n_{j}|}.

By Theorem
A.6
,
\det W_{1}(x)\neq 0
for
|\mathrm{Im}(x)|\leq\frac{\delta}{2\pi}
. Without loss of generality we assume
\det W_{1}(x)>0
on
\{x:|\mathrm{Im}(x)|\leq\frac{\delta}{2\pi}\}
(otherwise we take
W_{1}(x)
be the matrix with columns
S(x)
and
-T(x)
). By (
A.36
), we conclude that

(A.73)

\displaystyle\|A_{E}(x)W_{1}(x)-W_{1}(x+\alpha)R_{-\theta}\|_{\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{32\pi}}\leq e^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{150}|n_{j+1}|}.

Combining this estimate with (
A.72
), we infer that for
|\mathrm{Im}(x)|\leq\frac{\delta}{2\pi}
,

(A.74)

\displaystyle\det W_{1}(x)=\det W_{1}(x+\alpha)+O(e^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{200}|n_{j+1}|}).

By Theorem
A.6
, Lemma
A.7
and (
A.74
), for all
|\mathrm{Im}(x)|\leq\frac{\delta}{2\pi}
,

(A.75)

\displaystyle\det W_{1}(x)=w_{0}+O(e^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{300}|n_{j+1}|}),

with
w_{0}\geq L^{-5C_{4}}
.
Combining (
A.73
) with (
3.7
), (
A.72
), (
A.75
) and Lemma
A.7
, we have

\displaystyle\|W_{1}(x+\alpha)^{-1}A_{E}(x)W_{1}(x)-R_{-\theta}\|_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\inf_{|\mathrm{Im}(x)|\leq\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}\det W_{1}(x)}\|W_{1}\|_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{150}|n_{j+1}|}

(A.76)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(L^{6C_{4}}e^{3\delta|n_{j}|}e^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{150}|n_{j+1}|})=O(e^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{200}|n_{j+1}|}).

Define
W_{2}(x)=(\det W_{1}(x))^{-1/2}W_{1}(x)\in C^{\omega}_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}(\mathbb{T},\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R}))
.
By (
A.2
) and (
A.75
), we have that for
|\mathrm{Im}(x)|\leq\frac{\delta}{2\pi}
,

(A.77)

\displaystyle W_{2}(x+\alpha)^{-1}A_{E}(x)W_{2}(x)=

\displaystyle\frac{(\det W_{1}(x+\alpha))^{1/2}}{(\det W_{1}(x))^{1/2}}R_{-\theta}+O(e^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{200}|n_{j+1}|})

(A.78)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R_{-\theta}+O(e^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\hat{A}_{E})}{200}|n_{j+1}|}).

Taking (
A.72
), (
A.75
) and Lemma
A.7
into account, one has

(A.79)

\displaystyle\|W_{2}\|_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}\leq L^{6C_{4}}e^{3\delta|n_{j}|}\leq L^{7C_{4}}.

The estimate of
\mathrm{deg}(W_{2})
, see Definition
B.1
, follows the same Rouché argument as the proof of Theorem 3.5 in
[
AJ2
]
. In fact, in the subcritical case, one should be able to obtain an improved bound
|\mathrm{deg}(W_{2})|\lesssim\log|n_{j}|
. We leave those details to interested readers.

Appendix B
Proof of Theorem
1.6

B.1.
Rotation number

Definition B.1
.

Any
A:\mathbb{T}\to\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
is homotopic to
x\to R_{nx}
for some integer
n
, called the degree of
A
and denoted
\mathrm{deg}(A)=n
.

Assume that
A:\mathbb{T}\to\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
is homotopic to the identity. There exist
\psi:\mathbb{T}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
(lift of
A
) and
v:\mathbb{T}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that

(B.1)

\displaystyle A(x)\left(\begin{matrix}\cos(2\pi y)\\
\sin(2\pi y)\end{matrix}\right)=v(x,y)\left(\begin{matrix}\cos(2\pi(y+\psi(x,y)))\\
\sin(2\pi(y+\psi(x,y)))\end{matrix}\right).

Let
\mu
be any probability measure on
\mathbb{T}^{2}
that is invariant under the map

T:(x,y)\to(x+\alpha,y+\psi(x,y)),

projecting over the Lebesgue measure on the first coordinate.
Then the number

(B.2)

\displaystyle\rho(\alpha,A)=\int\psi\,\mathrm{d}\mu\qquad(\mathrm{mod}\mathbb{Z}),

is independent of
\psi
and
\mu
, and is called the fibered rotation number of
(\alpha,A)
.
It is clear from the definition that

(B.3)

\displaystyle|\rho(\alpha,A)-\theta|\leq C\|A-R_{\theta}\|_{0}.

If the cocycles
(\alpha,A^{(1)})
and
(\alpha,A^{(2)})
are conjugate to each, i.e.,
B(x+\alpha)^{-1}A^{(1)}(x)B(x)=A^{(2)}(x)
for some continuous
B:\mathbb{T}\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
with
\mathrm{deg}(B)=k
, then

(B.4)

\displaystyle\rho(\alpha,A^{(1)})=\rho(\alpha,A^{(2)})+\frac{k}{2}\alpha.

The following relation between the rotation number on the one hand, and integrated density of states (see (
2.59
)) on the other hand, is well-known:

(B.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(E)=1-2\rho(\alpha,A_{E}).

B.2.
Almost localization and almost reducibility

The following connection between localization and reduciblity was first explored in
[
P
]
, leading to the proof the Ten Martini problem for the almost Mathieu operator for all Diophantine
\alpha
’s.
The exact form cited below appeared in
[
AJ2
]
.

Theorem B.1
.

[
AJ2
]
*Theorem 2.5
For
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}
, let
H_{\alpha,\theta,v}
be the Schrödinger operator with a real-valued analytic potential
v
. Let
\theta,E\in\mathbb{R}
be such that there exists a non-trivial exponentially decaying solution of the dual operator
\hat{H}\hat{u}=E\hat{u}
. Then

•

If
2\theta\notin\mathbb{Z}\alpha+\mathbb{Z}
, then there is a
B:\mathbb{T}\to\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
analytic such that
B(x+\alpha)^{-1}A_{E}(x)B(x)=R_{\pm\theta}
.

•

If
2\theta-n\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, then there exists
B:\mathbb{T}\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
and
w:\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{R}
analytic such that

(B.6)

\displaystyle B(x+\alpha)^{-1}A_{E}(x)B(x)=\left(\begin{matrix}\pm 1\,&w(x)\\
0&\pm 1\end{matrix}\right).

If moreover
\beta(\alpha)=0
, then
w
can be chosen to be a constant.

In either case,
\rho(\alpha,A_{E})=\pm\theta+\frac{m}{2}\alpha
for some
m\in\mathbb{Z}
.

B.3.
Proof of Theorem
1.6

By Theorem
1.2
, we have the following spectral consequence, which is the analogue of Theorem 4.1 in
[
AJ2
]
.

Theorem B.2
.

For
\beta(\alpha)=0
and
E\in\Sigma_{\alpha,0}
, there exists a constant
c>0
(depending on
\hat{L}_{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})
) with the following properties:

(1)

If
\rho(\alpha,A_{E})
is
c-
resonant, then there exists a sequence
B^{(n)}:\mathbb{T}\to\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R})
such that
B^{(n)}(x+\alpha)^{-1}A_{E}(x)B^{(n)}(x)
converges to a constant rotation uniformly in
\{x:|\mathrm{Im}(x)|<c\}
.

(2)

If
\rho(\alpha,A_{E})
is not
c-
resonant and
2\rho(\alpha,A_{E})\notin\mathbb{Z}\alpha+\mathbb{Z}
, then there exists
B\in C^{\omega}_{c}(\mathbb{T},\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R}))
such that
B(x+\alpha)^{-1}A_{E}(x)B(x)
is a constant rotation.

(3)

If
2\rho(\alpha,A_{E})\in\mathbb{Z}\alpha+\mathbb{Z}
, then there exists
B:\mathbb{T}\to\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
analytic such that
B(x+\alpha)^{-1}A_{E}(x)B(x)
is a constant.

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon_{0}:=\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})}{50C_{3}}
and
\theta=\theta(E),\{n_{j}\},\delta,L_{j},W
be defined as in Theorem
1.2
.
First we show that if
\theta
is resonant, then
\rho(\alpha,A_{E})
must be resonant.
Thus suppose that
\theta
is
\varepsilon_{0}
-resonant. By Theorem
1.2
, for
j>j_{0}(\alpha,v,E)
there exists
W_{j}\in C^{\omega}_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}(\mathbb{T},\mathrm{SL}(2,\mathbb{R}))
such that

(B.7)

\displaystyle\|W_{j}(x+\alpha)^{-1}A_{E}(x)W(x)-R_{-\theta}\|_{\frac{\delta}{2\pi}}\leq e^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})}{300}|n_{j+1}|},

and
|\mathrm{deg}(W_{j})|\leq C_{0}|n_{j}|
with absolute constant
C_{0}>0
.
By (
B.3
), (
B.4
) and (
B.7
),

(B.8)

\displaystyle|\rho(\alpha,A_{E})-\theta+\mathrm{deg}(W_{j})\,\alpha|\leq Ce^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})}{300}|n_{j+1}|}.

This implies, after combining the preceding with Lemma
A.7
, that

(B.9)

\displaystyle\|2\rho(\alpha,A_{E})-(n_{j}-2\mathrm{deg}(W_{j}))\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geq

\displaystyle L_{j}^{-1}-Ce^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})}{300}|n_{j+1}|}

(B.10)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle e^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})}{4000C_{4}}|n_{j+1}|}-Ce^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})}{300}|n_{j+1}|}>0,

and

(B.11)

\displaystyle\|2\rho(\alpha,A_{E})-(n_{j}-2\mathrm{deg}(W_{j}))\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}\leq

\displaystyle L_{j}^{-1}+Ce^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})}{300}|n_{j+1}|}

(B.12)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle e^{-\varepsilon_{0}|n_{j}|}+Ce^{-\frac{\hat{L}_{d}(\alpha,\hat{A}_{E})}{300}|n_{j+1}|}

(B.13)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle e^{-(2C_{0}+2)^{-1}\varepsilon_{0}|2\mathrm{deg}(W_{j})-n_{j}|}.

Together with (
3.77
) this implies that

\rho(\alpha,A_{E})
is
(2C_{0}+2)^{-1}\varepsilon_{0}
-resonant at
n_{j}-2\mathrm{deg}(W_{j})
.
Let
c=(2C_{0}+2)^{-1}\varepsilon_{0},\frac{\delta}{2\pi}
.
We divide the analysis into three cases.

Case 1. If
\rho(\alpha,A_{E})
is
c
-resonant and
\theta_{0}
is non-
\varepsilon_{0}
-resonant, then Theorem
1.7
implies that there is an exponentially decaying eigenfunction. Theorem
B.1
applies and yields the claimed result.

Case 2. If
\rho(\alpha,A_{E})
is
c
-resonant and
\theta_{0}
is
\varepsilon_{0}
-resonant, then (
B.7
) is the claimed result.

Case 3. If
\rho(\alpha,A_{E})
is non-
c
-resonant, then by the arguments above
\theta
is non-
\varepsilon_{0}
resonant. Theorem
B.1
implies the claimed result.
∎

Finally, Theorem
1.6
is the consequence of the following statement.

Theorem B.3
.

For
\beta(\alpha)=0
,
|\lambda|<1
and
|\nu|<\nu_{0}(\alpha,\lambda,w)
sufficiently small. Suppose
E\in\sigma(H^{\mathrm{PAMO}}_{\alpha,\theta,\nu,w})
is such that
\mathcal{N}(E)\in\mathbb{Z}\alpha+\mathbb{Z}
, then
E
belongs to the boundary of a component of
\mathbb{R}\setminus\sigma(H^{\mathrm{PAMO}}_{\alpha,\theta,\nu,w})
.

Proof.

Due to the arguments in
[
P
]
, it is enough to show that
(\alpha,A^{\mathrm{PAMO}}_{E})
is reducible. Suppose
(\alpha,A^{\mathrm{PAMO}}_{E})
is not reducible, then Theorem
B.2
implies that
\rho(\alpha,A_{E})
is
c
-resonant for some
c>0
. But this contradicts the property
2\rho(\alpha,A_{E})\in\mathbb{Z}\alpha+\mathbb{Z}
, which follows from (
B.5
).
∎
